vii



VMC 503: General Virology
Preface
This book has been written for M.V.Sc. students of Veterinary Microbiology to cover the course VMC
503: General Virology (2+1). It follows the prescribed syllabus and lecture schedule, with each of its 18
chapters mapped to one or more of the 32 theory lectures, so that it can serve both as a textbook for
students and as a lecture guide for teachers.
Virology has changed rapidly in recent years: the ICTV now uses binomial species names and a realm-
based megataxonomy; next-generation sequencing and metagenomics are revealing new viruses;
mRNA, vectored and marker vaccines have become realities; and the COVID-19 pandemic, the spread
of H5N1 into mammals, and the emergence of lumpy skin disease and African swine fever in India
have shown the importance of a One Health approach. The book attempts to present these
developments alongside the classical foundations of the subject.
Special attention has been given to the Indian context through India Focus boxes describing national
institutions, programmes, vaccines and research. Forty-six full-colour figures illustrate virion
structure, replication cycles, laboratory methods and pathogenesis, and every chapter ends with a
summary, glossary and question bank. Suggestions from teachers and students for improving the book
are most welcome.
Jaipur
September 2026
Dr. Ashok Baindha

VMC 503: General Virology
Acknowledgements
I express my sincere gratitude to the Vice-Chancellor, Rajasthan University of Veterinary and Animal
Sciences (RUVAS), Jaipur, and to the Dean, Post Graduate Institute of Veterinary Education and
Research (PGIVER), Jaipur, for their encouragement and for providing the academic environment in
which this book was prepared.
I am grateful to my colleagues and teachers for their guidance and for many discussions that shaped the
contents of this book, and to the reviewers for their careful reading of the manuscript and valuable
suggestions.
I thank the M.V.Sc. students of Veterinary Microbiology, whose questions in the classroom and
laboratory showed what a textbook of general virology needs to explain.
The book draws on standard textbooks, reviews and research papers, and on the publications of the
International Committee on Taxonomy of Viruses (ICTV), the World Organisation for Animal Health
(WOAH), the Indian Council of Agricultural Research (ICAR), the Indian Council of Medical
Research (ICMR) and the Department of Animal Husbandry and Dairying, Government of India. Their
authors are gratefully acknowledged in the suggested readings at the end of each chapter.
Finally, I thank my family for their patience and constant support during the writing of this book.
Dr. Ashok Baindha

x



VMC 503: General Virology
How to Use This Book
Each chapter corresponds to one or more lectures of the course and follows a uniform structure
designed for classroom teaching, self-study and examination preparation:
• Learning Objectives — what the student should be able to do after studying the chapter.
• Lecture banners — show where each lecture of the official schedule begins, so that teachers can
plan classes.
• Tables — concise comparisons and “at a glance” summaries of each disease, numbered by
chapter.
• Figures — 46 full-colour illustrations of virion structure, replication cycles, laboratory methods
and pathogenesis, numbered by chapter (for example, Figure 5.2 is the second figure of Chapter
5). A List of Figures follows the Contents.
• Disease Status in India / India Focus boxes — the Indian situation, national programmes,
institutions and research, with emphasis on Rajasthan where relevant.
• Link with Practical Classes — connects theory with the practical syllabus.
• Summary and Glossary — for quick revision.
• Question Bank — 20 multiple choice questions with answer key, short answer and long answer
questions in each chapter.
• Suggested Readings and References — standard textbooks, reviews and key research papers,
including Indian studies.
NOTE ON CURRENCY OF INFORMATION
Information on disease occurrence, taxonomy, vaccines and national programmes changes rapidly. The
contents reflect the situation as understood up to September 2026. Readers should consult the latest ICTV
taxonomy releases, WOAH (WAHIS) reports, Department of Animal Husbandry and Dairying (DAHD)
notifications and ICAR/ICMR institute publications before relying on specific figures for official purposes.
xi



VMC 503: General Virology
Course Syllabus
Course: VMC 503: General Virology | Credit hours: 2+1
Aim of the course: To impart knowledge of the history, nature, structure, classification, cultivation
and laboratory diagnosis of viruses; their replication and interactions with host cells; viral
pathogenesis, persistence, oncogenesis and epidemiology; and the immune response, vaccines and
chemotherapy against viral infections.
Theory syllabus
Unit Topics
Unit I
History of virology; origin and nature of viruses; morphology and chemical
composition; nomenclature and classification; cultivation and purification;
laboratory diagnosis; viroids and prions
Unit II Replication of DNA and RNA viruses; genetic and non-genetic interactions
between viruses
Unit III Virus–cell interactions; viral pathogenesis; persistence; oncogenic and oncolytic
viruses; epidemiology of viral diseases
Unit IV Immune response to viruses; viral vaccines; viral chemotherapy
Lecture schedule
Chapter Topic Lectures
1 History of Virology 1
2 Origin and Nature of Viruses 2
3 Morphological Structure and Chemical Composition of Viruses 3–4
4 Nomenclature and Classification of Viruses 5–6
5 Cultivation and Purification of Viruses 7–8
6 Laboratory Diagnosis of Viral Infections 9–10
7 Viroids and Prions 11
8 Replication of DNA Viruses 12–13
9 Replication of RNA Viruses 14–16
10 Genetic and Non-genetic Interactions between Viruses 17–18
11 Virus–Cell Interactions 19
12 Viral Pathogenesis 20–21
13 Viral Persistence 22
14 Oncogenic and Oncolytic Viruses 23–24
15 Epidemiology of Viral Diseases 25–26
16 Immune Response to Viruses 27–28
17 Viral Vaccines 29–30
18 Viral Chemotherapy 31–32

VMC 503: General Virology
UNIT I — GENERAL PROPERTIES OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 1
History of Virology
Lecture 1
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Trace the development of virology from early observations of viral diseases to the modern molecular
and genomic era.
▪ Describe the key experiments that led to the discovery of viruses as filterable agents and to the
understanding of their physical and chemical nature.
▪ Identify landmark discoveries in veterinary virology and the scientists associated with them.
▪ Explain Koch’s postulates, Rivers’ postulates and their modern molecular equivalents.
▪ Appreciate the contribution of Indian institutions to veterinary and medical virology, including the
eradication of rinderpest.
LECTURE 1: HISTORY OF VIROLOGY
1.1 Introduction
Virology is the study of viruses — sub-microscopic, obligate intracellular parasites that contain either
DNA or RNA as their genome and replicate only within living cells. The history of virology is a story
of how diseases known for thousands of years (rabies, smallpox, rinderpest, foot-and-mouth disease)
were gradually shown to be caused by agents quite different from bacteria. The discipline developed
through distinct phases, each driven by a new technology: filtration, animal inoculation,
embryonated eggs, electron microscopy, cell culture, molecular biology and, most recently, high-
throughput sequencing. Veterinary virology has been central to this history — the first animal virus
discovered was the foot-and-mouth disease virus (1898), and the first virus eradicated from
animals was rinderpest (2011).
Chapter 1 | Page 2



VMC 503: General Virology
1.2 The Pre-discovery Era: Viral Diseases Before Viruses
• Ancient records: an Egyptian stele (c. 1400 BCE) shows a priest with a withered, shortened leg
typical of poliomyelitis; the mummy of Pharaoh Ramses V (died c. 1145 BCE) shows lesions
resembling smallpox. Rabies was described in the Mesopotamian Laws of Eshnunna (c. 1930
BCE), which fined the owners of rabid dogs that bit people. Rinderpest (cattle plague)
epidemics were recorded in ancient and medieval times and devastated Europe in the eighteenth
century, leading to the founding of the first veterinary school at Lyon (1761).
• Variolation: deliberate inoculation of material from smallpox pustules to produce mild disease
and immunity was practised in India and China for centuries (the Indian practice of "tika" was
described by European observers), and introduced to England in 1721 by Lady Mary Wortley
Montagu.
• Edward Jenner (1796) demonstrated that inoculation with cowpox material protected against
smallpox — the beginning of vaccination (Latin vacca = cow). This was the first use of an
animal virus to protect humans, a striking early example of One Health.
• Louis Pasteur (1885) developed a rabies vaccine from dried spinal cords of rabbits infected
with "fixed" rabies virus, and on 6 July 1885 treated Joseph Meister, bitten by a rabid dog.
Pasteur could not see or culture the agent, but he recognised that it was a living, attenuable "virus"
(Latin: poison) smaller than bacteria.
• In this period, the word "virus" was used for any infectious agent or poison, and Koch’s
postulates (1884–1890) had established the germ theory for bacteria.
1.3 Discovery of Viruses as Filterable Agents (1892–1930)
The key technical advance was the Chamberland–Pasteur porcelain filter (1884), which retained
bacteria and was used to produce sterile water. Agents passing through such filters were called
"filterable viruses".
Table 1.1: Landmark discoveries of the filterable-agent era
Year Scientist(s) Discovery
1892 Dmitri Ivanovsky (Russia)
Sap of tobacco plants with tobacco mosaic disease remained
infectious after passing through a Chamberland filter; he
believed it was a toxin or very small bacterium
1898 Martinus Beijerinck
(Netherlands)
Confirmed the filterability of the tobacco mosaic agent, showed
it diffused through agar and multiplied only in living, growing
tissue; called it "contagium vivum fluidum" (contagious living
fluid) — often regarded as the birth of virology
1898 Friedrich Loeffler and Paul
Frosch (Germany)
Showed that foot-and-mouth disease of cattle was caused by a
filterable agent that multiplied in the host and was particulate
(retained by finer filters) — the first animal virus discovered
1900 John M’Fadyean; Theiler Filterability of the agent of African horse sickness
1901 Walter Reed and colleagues
(USA)
Yellow fever, the first human virus identified; transmission by
mosquitoes (Aedes aegypti) — the first arthropod-borne virus
1901 Centanni and Savonuzzi
(Italy)
Fowl plague (highly pathogenic avian influenza) caused by a
filterable agent
1902 Nicolle and Adil-Bey Filterability of the rinderpest agent
1903–1905 Remlinger; Carré Filterable agents of rabies and canine distemper
1904 Vallée and Carré (France) Equine infectious anaemia — first lentivirus (retrovirus)
disease recognised
1908 Ellermann and Bang Transmission of avian leukosis by cell-free filtrates — first

1.4 The Physical and Chemical Era (1930–1950)
• Embryonated eggs: Alice Woodruff and Ernest Goodpasture (1931) grew fowlpox virus on
the chorioallantoic membrane of embryonated hens’ eggs, providing a cheap host system. F.M.
Burnet developed egg techniques further; Max Theiler produced the 17D yellow fever vaccine
in eggs and tissue (Nobel Prize 1951). Eggs remain the substrate for most influenza and
Newcastle disease vaccines.
• Ultrafiltration and ultracentrifugation: William Elford (1931) used graded collodion
membranes to estimate virus size; ultracentrifuges (Svedberg) allowed purification and
sedimentation analysis.
• Crystallisation of a virus: Wendell Stanley (1935) crystallised tobacco mosaic virus and
believed it to be pure protein (Nobel Prize in Chemistry 1946). Bawden and Pirie (1937)
showed that TMV consisted of protein and ribonucleic acid — a nucleoprotein.
• Electron microscopy: the first electron micrograph of a virus (TMV) was published by
Kausche, Pfankuch and Ruska (1939); negative staining (Brenner and Horne, 1959) made
rapid visualisation of virus morphology possible and remains important in diagnosis.
• Haemagglutination: George Hirst (1941) discovered that influenza virus agglutinates chicken
red blood cells — the basis of the haemagglutination (HA) and haemagglutination-inhibition
(HI) tests still used for influenza and Newcastle disease viruses.
• Genetic nature of genomes: Hershey and Chase (1952) showed with bacteriophage T2 that
DNA, not protein, enters the host cell and carries genetic information; Fraenkel-Conrat and
Gierer and Schramm (1956) showed that purified TMV RNA alone is infectious.
Chapter 1 | Page 4



VMC 503: General Virology
• Rivers’ postulates (1937): Thomas Rivers modified Koch’s postulates for viruses (see Section
1.8).
1.5 The Cell Culture Era (1949–1970)
• John Enders, Thomas Weller and Frederick Robbins (1949) grew poliovirus in cultures of
non-neural human tissue, revolutionising virology (Nobel Prize 1954). Cell cultures permitted
virus isolation, titration and vaccine production without animals.
• Renato Dulbecco (1952) adapted the plaque assay to animal viruses, allowing accurate
quantification and the cloning of virus strains.
• Cytopathic effect (CPE) observation, cell lines (HeLa, 1951; Vero, 1962; MDBK, BHK-21,
MDCK) and primary cell cultures became standard tools.
• Vaccines: inactivated polio vaccine (Jonas Salk, 1955), oral live attenuated polio vaccine
(Albert Sabin, 1961), measles (Enders, 1963), and in veterinary medicine the tissue culture
rinderpest vaccine (Walter Plowright and R.D. Ferris, 1962) — the tool that made global
rinderpest eradication possible (Plowright received the World Food Prize in 1999).
• Interferon was discovered by Alick Isaacs and Jean Lindenmann (1957).
• Virus classification by physical properties was proposed by Lwoff, Horne and Tournier
(1962), and the International Committee on Nomenclature of Viruses (1966), later the
International Committee on Taxonomy of Viruses (ICTV, 1973), was established.
• Marek’s disease vaccine (turkey herpesvirus, HVT, 1970) — the first vaccine to prevent a
naturally occurring cancer.
1.6 The Molecular Era (1970–2000)
• Reverse transcriptase: discovered independently by David Baltimore and by Howard Temin
and Satoshi Mizutani (1970) in RNA tumour viruses — overturning the "central dogma" that
information flows only from DNA to RNA (Nobel Prize 1975, shared with Dulbecco).
• Baltimore classification (1971): classification of viruses into classes according to the nature of
the genome and the pathway of mRNA synthesis (Chapter 4).
• Viroids (1971): Theodor Diener described the potato spindle tuber viroid, a small, naked,
circular RNA that infects plants.
• Restriction enzymes and recombinant DNA (Arber, Smith and Nathans — Nobel 1978;
Paul Berg — 1980) enabled cloning and sequencing of viral genomes; bacteriophage φX174
(Sanger, 1977) was the first DNA genome sequenced.
• Oncogenes: J. Michael Bishop and Harold Varmus (1976) showed that the src oncogene of
Rous sarcoma virus was derived from a normal cellular gene (proto-oncogene) (Nobel 1989).
• MHC restriction: Peter Doherty and Rolf Zinkernagel (1974), working on lymphocytic
choriomeningitis virus in mice, showed that cytotoxic T cells recognise virus-infected cells only
in the context of self-MHC molecules (Nobel 1996; Doherty is a veterinarian).
• Prions: Stanley Prusiner (1982) proposed that scrapie is caused by a proteinaceous infectious
particle (prion) (Nobel 1997); Carleton Gajdusek had earlier transmitted kuru to chimpanzees
(Nobel 1976). Bovine spongiform encephalopathy (BSE) was recognised in the UK in 1986,
and its transmission to humans as variant CJD in 1996.
• Polymerase chain reaction (PCR): Kary Mullis (1983) (Nobel Prize in Chemistry 1993) — the
basis of modern viral diagnosis.
Chapter 1 | Page 5



VMC 503: General Virology
• HIV: discovered by Luc Montagnier and Françoise Barré-Sinoussi (1983) (Nobel 2008,
shared with Harald zur Hausen for human papillomaviruses causing cervical cancer); led to the
development of antiviral drugs and highly active antiretroviral therapy (HAART, 1996).
• Hepatitis C virus: identified by molecular cloning (Michael Houghton, 1989) without isolation
of the virus (Nobel 2020 with Harvey Alter and Charles Rice); Baruch Blumberg had
discovered the hepatitis B "Australia antigen" (Nobel 1976).
• Recombinant vaccines: hepatitis B surface antigen produced in yeast (1986); the vaccinia–
rabies glycoprotein recombinant oral bait vaccine used to eliminate fox rabies from much of
western Europe (1990s).
1.7 The Genomic and One Health Era (2000–Present)
• Next-generation sequencing (NGS) and metagenomics allow detection and characterisation of
new viruses directly from clinical and environmental samples, without culture; this has revealed
an enormous virosphere and led ICTV to accept virus species defined only by sequence (2016
onwards).
• Giant viruses: Mimivirus (2003) of amoebae, larger than some bacteria, challenged traditional
definitions of viruses; later Pandoravirus and Pithovirus (revived from 30,000-year-old
Siberian permafrost, 2014).
• Emerging zoonotic viruses: Nipah virus (Malaysia, 1998–1999; Kerala, India, 2018 and
later), SARS-CoV (2002–2003), highly pathogenic avian influenza H5N1 (from 1997; clade
2.3.4.4b panzootic in wild birds, poultry and mammals, including dairy cattle in the USA from
2024), pandemic H1N1 (2009), MERS-CoV (2012; dromedary camels), Ebola (West Africa,
2014–2016), Zika (2015–2016), SARS-CoV-2 and the COVID-19 pandemic (from December
2019), mpox (2022 multi-country outbreak).
• Transboundary animal viruses: African swine fever spread to Europe and Asia (from 2007;
India since 2020), lumpy skin disease spread across Asia (India 2019; major epidemic 2022),
peste des petits ruminants targeted for global eradication by 2030.
• Vaccine technologies: mRNA vaccines (Katalin Karikó and Drew Weissman — Nobel 2023
for nucleoside base modifications), viral-vectored vaccines, DIVA marker vaccines, reverse
genetics.
• Eradication successes: smallpox — declared eradicated by WHO on 8 May 1980; rinderpest
— declared eradicated by OIE (WOAH) on 25 May 2011 and by FAO on 28 June 2011 — the
only animal disease ever eradicated.
• Gene editing and antivirals: CRISPR–Cas systems (originally a bacterial antiviral defence
against phages), direct-acting antivirals for hepatitis C, and structure-based drug design against
SARS-CoV-2.
1.8 Koch’s Postulates, Rivers’ Postulates and Molecular Criteria
Table 1.2: Criteria for establishing a causal relationship between a microbe and disease
Criteria Main points Limitations for viruses
Koch’s postulates
(1890)
(1) The organism is present in every case of the
disease; (2) it is isolated and grown in pure
culture; (3) the pure culture reproduces the disease
in a susceptible host; (4) the organism is re-isolated
from the experimental host
Viruses cannot be grown in
pure culture on artificial media;
asymptomatic infections,
carriers and host-specificity
break the rules
Chapter 1 | Page 6



VMC 503: General Virology
Criteria Main points Limitations for viruses
Rivers’ postulates
(1937)
(1) A specific virus must be associated with the
disease with a degree of regularity; (2) the virus
must be shown to occur in the sick individual as the
cause of the disease and not incidentally or
accidentally; also — filterable agent reproduces
disease in a susceptible host or produces specific
antibodies
Still requires isolation or
transmission; not applicable to
non-cultivable viruses
Evans’ criteria (1976)
Epidemiological and immunological criteria —
higher prevalence and incidence in exposed groups,
temporal sequence, antibody response, prevention
by vaccination
Indirect
Molecular Koch’s
postulates (Fredericks
and Relman, 1996)
A nucleic acid sequence of a putative pathogen
should be present in most cases of disease,
preferentially in diseased organs; fewer or no
copies in hosts without disease; sequence copy
number falls with resolution of disease; detection
before disease; consistent with known biology;
demonstration in situ in affected tissues
Association does not prove
causation; used for HCV,
KSHV (HHV-8), new animal
viruses discovered by
metagenomics
1.9 Milestones in Veterinary Virology
Table 1.3: Selected milestones in veterinary virology
Year Milestone
1761 First veterinary school founded at Lyon (France), partly because of rinderpest epidemics

1901–1911 Filterable agents of fowl plague, rinderpest, canine distemper, equine infectious anaemia, avian

cattle
INDIA FOCUS: INDIA’S CONTRIBUTION TO VIROLOGY
▪ Variolation (tika) against smallpox was practised in India for centuries before Jenner’s work.
▪ ICAR–Indian Veterinary Research Institute (IVRI): founded in 1889 as the Imperial
Bacteriological Laboratory at Pune, moved to Mukteswar (Kumaon hills) in 1893, with its present
headquarters at Izatnagar (Bareilly). IVRI pioneered anti-rinderpest serum, the goat-adapted
rinderpest vaccine (Edwards, 1927), lapinised and later tissue culture rinderpest vaccines, and
vaccines against sheep pox, goat pox, Ranikhet (Newcastle) disease and other diseases.
▪ Rinderpest eradication: India controlled rinderpest through the National Rinderpest Eradication
Programme and later the National Project on Rinderpest Eradication; the country was free of clinical
Chapter 1 | Page 7



VMC 503: General Virology
rinderpest from 1995 and was recognised by OIE as free from rinderpest infection on 25 May
2006, contributing to global eradication declared in 2011.
▪ ICMR–National Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune: established in 1952 as the Virus Research
Centre with support from the Rockefeller Foundation, renamed NIV in 1978; it discovered Kyasanur
Forest disease virus (1957) and Chandipura virus (1965), developed KFD and Japanese encephalitis
vaccines, established India’s first BSL-4 (P-4) containment laboratory, and played a leading role in
the detection of Nipah, Zika and SARS-CoV-2 and in developing the inactivated COVID-19 vaccine
Covaxin with Bharat Biotech.
▪ High-security animal disease laboratory: the High Security Animal Disease Laboratory, Bhopal
(established in the late 1990s; now ICAR–National Institute of High Security Animal Diseases,
NIHSAD) handles exotic and emerging animal viruses such as HPAI, ASF and Nipah and is a WOAH
reference laboratory for avian influenza.
▪ Public health milestones: India was certified smallpox-free in 1977 and polio-free by WHO in 2014
(last wild poliovirus case in January 2011).
▪ Recent emerging viral diseases tackled by Indian virologists include HPAI H5N1 (since 2006), Nipah
(Kerala, 2018 onwards), lumpy skin disease (2019; epidemic 2022), African swine fever (2020) and
COVID-19; indigenous vaccines such as the LSD vaccine "Lumpi-ProVacInd" (ICAR–NRCE and
IVRI, 2022) and PPR and goat pox vaccines were developed.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Demonstration of a Chamberland/membrane filter and filtration of a bacterial culture versus a virus
suspension.
▪ Visit to museum/historical collection of vaccines and laboratory equipment; study of photographs of
early electron micrographs.
▪ Preparation of a timeline chart of milestones in veterinary virology (assignment).
▪ Laboratory biosafety: introduction to biosafety levels (BSL-1 to BSL-4) used in virology laboratories.
1.10 Summary
• Viral diseases such as smallpox, rabies, poliomyelitis and rinderpest were known from antiquity;
variolation, Jenner’s cowpox vaccination (1796) and Pasteur’s rabies vaccine (1885) preceded
the discovery of viruses.
• Ivanovsky (1892) and Beijerinck (1898) showed that tobacco mosaic disease was caused by a
filterable, self-replicating agent; Loeffler and Frosch (1898) discovered the first animal virus
(FMDV); Reed (1901) the first human virus (yellow fever).
• Embryonated eggs, ultracentrifugation, crystallisation (Stanley 1935), electron microscopy
(1939) and haemagglutination (Hirst 1941) defined the physical and chemical nature of viruses.
• Cell culture (Enders, Weller and Robbins 1949) and the plaque assay (Dulbecco 1952) enabled
isolation, quantification and vaccine production, including the tissue culture rinderpest vaccine
(1962).
• The molecular era brought reverse transcriptase, the Baltimore classification, viroids, prions,
oncogenes, PCR and the discovery of HIV and HCV.
• The genomic era is characterised by NGS, metagenomics, giant viruses, emerging zoonoses,
mRNA vaccines and the eradication of smallpox (1980) and rinderpest (2011).
• Koch’s postulates were adapted by Rivers (1937) and later by molecular criteria for virus–disease
causation.
• India contributed through variolation, IVRI (1889), the goat-adapted rinderpest vaccine (1927),
NIV Pune (1952), NIHSAD Bhopal and rinderpest eradication (OIE recognition 2006).
Chapter 1 | Page 8



VMC 503: General Virology
1.11 Glossary
Table 1.4: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Virus Sub-microscopic obligate intracellular parasite with a DNA or RNA genome
enclosed in a protein coat.
Variolation Deliberate inoculation of smallpox material to induce mild disease and
immunity.
Vaccination Originally inoculation with cowpox (vaccinia); now any procedure inducing
protective immunity.
Filterable agent Infectious agent passing through filters that retain bacteria.
Contagium vivum fluidum Beijerinck’s term for the living, soluble infectious agent of tobacco mosaic.
Bacteriophage Virus that infects bacteria.
Plaque assay Method of counting infectious virus particles by the zones of cell lysis they
produce.
Cytopathic effect Visible changes produced in cultured cells by viral infection.
Haemagglutination Clumping of red blood cells by certain viruses.
Reverse transcriptase RNA-dependent DNA polymerase of retroviruses and hepadnaviruses.
Viroid Small, circular, non-coding infectious RNA of plants.
Prion Proteinaceous infectious particle lacking nucleic acid.
Rivers’ postulates Criteria adapted from Koch’s postulates to establish viral causation of disease.
Metagenomics Sequencing of all nucleic acids in a sample to identify organisms without
culture.
Eradication Permanent reduction of worldwide incidence of an infection to zero.
1.12 Multiple Choice Questions
1. The first virus to be discovered was:
(a) Foot-and-mouth disease virus (b) Tobacco mosaic virus
(c) Yellow fever virus (d) Rabies virus
2. The term "contagium vivum fluidum" was coined by:
(a) Ivanovsky (b) Beijerinck (c) Pasteur (d) Loeffler
3. The first animal virus discovered was the agent of:
(a) Rinderpest (b) Foot-and-mouth disease
(c) Rabies (d) Fowl plague
4. The first human virus identified was:
(a) Poliovirus (b) Yellow fever virus
(c) Influenza virus (d) Smallpox virus

UNIT I — GENERAL PROPERTIES OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 2
Origin and Nature of Viruses
Lecture 2
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Define a virus and a virion and list the properties that distinguish viruses from other microorganisms.
▪ Discuss whether viruses are living entities, including the "virocell" concept.
▪ Explain the main hypotheses on the origin of viruses and the evidence for each.
▪ Describe giant viruses, virophages, endogenous viruses and the virosphere, and their significance for
understanding viral evolution.
▪ Appreciate the ecological, evolutionary and practical importance of viruses, including their beneficial
roles.
LECTURE 2: ORIGIN AND NATURE OF VIRUSES
2.1 What is a Virus?
A virus may be defined as an infectious, obligate intracellular parasite comprising a genome of
either DNA or RNA (never both in the infectious particle), which is enclosed in a protective protein
coat (capsid), sometimes surrounded by a lipid envelope, and which replicates only inside living
host cells, using the host’s machinery for protein synthesis and energy generation, to produce progeny
particles by assembly from newly synthesised components rather than by growth and division.
André Lwoff (1957), in his classic essay "The concept of virus", summarised the defining features:
viruses (1) contain only one type of nucleic acid; (2) are reproduced from their nucleic acid alone,
whereas other agents reproduce from the integrated sum of their constituents; (3) are unable to grow or
undergo binary fission; (4) lack the genetic information for synthesis of the cellular energy-producing
(Lipmann) system; and (5) make use of the ribosomes of their host cells.
2.1.1 Virus, Virion and Related Terms
Table 2.1: Key terms describing viruses
Term Meaning
Virion The complete, mature, extracellular infectious virus particle — the form in
which the viral genome is transmitted between cells and hosts.
Virus The biological entity as a whole, including its intracellular, replicating phase;
often used loosely as a synonym for virion.
Capsid The protein shell that encloses the genome; built from repeating capsomeres
made of protomers (structural subunits).
Nucleocapsid The capsid together with the enclosed nucleic acid (and associated proteins).
Envelope A lipid bilayer derived from host cell membranes, containing viral glycoproteins
(peplomers or spikes).
Virocell An infected cell whose metabolism has been reprogrammed to produce virions
— considered by some authors to be the "living form" of the virus.
Defective interfering (DI)
particle
A virus particle with a deleted genome that cannot replicate without a helper
virus and interferes with the replication of the standard virus.
Chapter 2 | Page 12



VMC 503: General Virology
Term Meaning
Pseudovirion / pseudotype A particle containing host DNA, or the genome of one virus enclosed in the
envelope/capsid of another.
Subviral agents Satellites (depend on a helper virus), viroids (naked circular RNA) and prions
(protein only) — Chapter 7.

2.2.1 Size and Genome Range
• Smallest viruses: circoviruses (e.g. porcine circovirus 2) — 17–20 nm, circular ssDNA genome
of about 1.7–2 kb encoding only 2–3 proteins; also the anelloviruses and some plant
geminiviruses.
• Largest viruses: poxviruses (up to 400 nm; genome ~130–375 kb) among vertebrate viruses;
giant viruses of protists — Mimivirus (~750 nm including surface fibres; genome ~1.2 Mb),
Pandoravirus (~1 μm; genome up to ~2.5 Mb, larger than some bacterial genomes) and
Pithovirus (~1.5 μm long).
• RNA genomes are limited in size by the error-prone nature of RNA replication — typically 7–30
kb; coronaviruses (~26–32 kb) have among the largest RNA genomes of vertebrate viruses,
maintained by a proofreading exonuclease (nsp14).
• Coding capacity ranges from 2 proteins (circoviruses) to more than 1,000 (giant viruses), and
viruses use strategies such as overlapping reading frames, alternative splicing, polyprotein
processing, ribosomal frameshifting and RNA editing to maximise coding capacity.
2.3 Are Viruses Alive?
Whether viruses are "alive" has been debated since their discovery. The answer depends on how life is
defined.
Table 2.3: Arguments on the living nature of viruses
Arguments that viruses are NOT living Arguments that viruses ARE living (or part of life)
Outside cells, virions are inert particles that can be
crystallised (TMV, poliovirus) like chemicals
They possess a genome, replicate, mutate, evolve by
natural selection and adapt to hosts
No metabolism, no ribosomes, no energy production,
no growth, no binary fission
Inside the host cell, the virocell carries out metabolism
directed by the viral genome (Forterre’s concept)
Depend completely on host cells for reproduction Many bacteria (Rickettsia, Chlamydia) and all animals
also depend on other organisms for essential functions
Do not respond to stimuli or maintain homeostasis
Giant viruses encode translation-related genes
(aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases), DNA repair enzymes,
and are themselves infected by virophages
—
Viruses have shaped all cellular life through gene
transfer, and viral genes are the most abundant genes
on Earth
A useful modern view is that viruses exist in two phases: an extracellular, inert virion (a transmission
vehicle, analogous to a seed or spore) and an intracellular, replicative phase in which the infected cell
becomes a virus factory (virocell). Many virologists describe viruses as being "at the edge of life".
2.4 Theories of the Origin of Viruses
Because viruses do not leave fossils and evolve rapidly, their origins must be inferred from
comparative genomics and protein structure. Three classical hypotheses have been proposed, and
current evidence suggests that viruses are polyphyletic — different groups arose independently,
probably at different times.

2.4.1 Modern Synthesis: Hallmark Genes and Virus Realms
• Viral hallmark genes — genes shared by diverse viruses but absent or rare in cellular organisms
— include the RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (RdRp), the single and double jelly-roll
capsid proteins, the HK97-fold major capsid protein with its terminase packaging ATPase (in
tailed phages and herpesviruses), superfamily 3 helicases and rolling-circle replication
initiation endonucleases.
• These genes suggest that major virus lineages have ancient origins, perhaps reaching back to the
last universal cellular ancestor (LUCA).
Chapter 2 | Page 15



VMC 503: General Virology
• On this basis, the ICTV (since 2018–2019) recognises the highest taxonomic rank of realm, each
representing an independent origin: Riboviria (RNA viruses encoding RdRp and reverse-
transcribing viruses encoding RT), Duplodnaviria (tailed phages and herpesviruses — HK97
capsid fold), Varidnaviria (double jelly-roll capsid — adenoviruses, poxviruses, asfarviruses,
giant viruses), Monodnaviria (small ssDNA viruses — parvoviruses, circoviruses,
papillomaviruses and polyomaviruses), Adnaviria (filamentous archaeal viruses) and
Ribozyviria (hepatitis delta-like viruses) (see Chapter 4).
• Some viral genes have been captured from hosts (e.g. cytokine and complement-regulator
homologues of poxviruses and herpesviruses), and some cellular genes have been captured from
viruses (e.g. telomerase is related to reverse transcriptase; the mitochondrial RNA polymerase
and DNA polymerase are phage-derived).
2.5 Giant Viruses and Virophages
• Mimivirus ("mimicking microbe", Acanthamoeba polyphaga mimivirus), discovered in
amoebae from a cooling tower in Bradford, UK, and described in 2003 (La Scola et al.), was
initially mistaken for a Gram-positive coccus. It has a ~1.2 Mb genome encoding about 1,000
proteins, including aminoacyl-tRNA synthetases and other translation factors.
• Giant viruses belong to the nucleocytoplasmic large DNA viruses (NCLDV), now classified in
the phylum Nucleocytoviricota (realm Varidnaviria), together with poxviruses, asfarviruses
(African swine fever virus) and iridoviruses — linking giant viruses with important animal
pathogens.
• Virophages such as Sputnik (2008) are small viruses that replicate only in the "virus factories"
of giant viruses, impairing their replication — a virus that infects a virus.
• The discovery of giant viruses has challenged the traditional boundary between viruses and cells
and supports the view that some viruses may have evolved by reduction from more complex
ancestors.
2.6 Endogenous Viruses and Viral Contributions to Host Genomes
• Endogenous retroviruses (ERVs) are remnants of ancient retroviral infections of germ-line
cells, inherited as Mendelian genes. About 8% of the human genome consists of ERV
sequences, and similar proportions are found in cattle, pigs, chickens and other animals.
• Most ERVs are defective, but some remain active: endogenous avian leukosis viruses (ev loci)
in chickens, porcine endogenous retroviruses (PERVs) (a concern in xenotransplantation),
endogenous Jaagsiekte sheep retroviruses (enJSRVs), which are essential for placental
development in sheep, and Koala retrovirus (KoRV), which is currently in the process of
becoming endogenous in wild koalas.
• Domesticated viral genes: syncytins — envelope genes of ERVs co-opted to form the
syncytiotrophoblast of the placenta in primates (syncytin-1 and -2), rodents and ruminants; the
Arc gene, derived from a retrotransposon, forms virus-like capsids that transfer mRNA between
neurons.
• Endogenous viral elements (EVEs) derived from non-retroviruses (e.g. bornaviruses,
filoviruses, parvoviruses, circoviruses) have also been found in animal genomes, acting as
"molecular fossils" that allow dating of viral lineages over millions of years.
• Polydnaviruses of parasitoid wasps are integrated in the wasp genome and delivered with the
eggs to suppress the immunity of the caterpillar host — an example of mutualism.
Chapter 2 | Page 16



VMC 503: General Virology
2.7 Viruses in Nature: Abundance, Ecology and Benefits
• Abundance: viruses are the most abundant biological entities on Earth — about 1030–1031 virus
particles exist in the oceans alone, outnumbering bacteria by about 10:1. Marine phages kill an
estimated 20–40% of ocean bacteria each day, influencing global carbon and nutrient cycles (the
"viral shunt").
• The virosphere: metagenomic studies have revealed that the known viruses represent a tiny
fraction of viral diversity; every cellular organism studied has its own viruses.
• Virome: the healthy animal body carries a virome — phages in the gut, endogenous viruses and
persistent viruses (e.g. anelloviruses) — with roles in immunity and health.
• Beneficial uses of viruses: vaccines (live attenuated and vector vaccines), gene therapy vectors
(adeno-associated virus, lentivirus), oncolytic virotherapy (Chapter 14), bacteriophage
therapy against antibiotic-resistant bacteria, phage display, biological control of pests (e.g.
myxoma virus and rabbit haemorrhagic disease virus against rabbits in Australia; baculoviruses
against insects), and model systems for molecular biology.
• Mutualistic viruses: some viruses benefit hosts — e.g. a plant virus conferring heat tolerance to
a grass, polydnaviruses in wasps, and latent herpesviruses in mice that increase resistance to some
bacterial infections.
2.8 Evolutionary Forces Acting on Viruses (Overview)
• Mutation: RNA viruses have very high mutation rates (10−3–10−5 substitutions per nucleotide
per replication cycle) because RdRps lack proofreading; they exist as quasispecies — clouds of
related variants. DNA viruses have lower rates (10−6–10−8).
• Recombination and reassortment (segmented genomes such as influenza, bluetongue and
rotaviruses) — Chapter 10.
• Selection by host immunity, antivirals and host range; genetic drift and bottlenecks during
transmission.
• Co-evolution with hosts — e.g. myxoma virus and European rabbits in Australia, where
virulence declined and host resistance increased after 1950.
• Cross-species transmission (spillover) and host switching — the source of most emerging viral
diseases (e.g. canine parvovirus type 2 emerging from a feline panleukopenia-like virus in the
1970s; SARS-CoV-2).
INDIA FOCUS: ORIGIN AND NATURE OF VIRUSES
▪ The first hint of bacteriophages came from India: in 1896, the British bacteriologist Ernest
Hanbury Hankin, working in Agra, reported that the waters of the Ganga and Yamuna had a
filterable, heat-labile antibacterial activity against Vibrio cholerae — nearly two decades before the
formal discovery of phages by Twort and d’Hérelle.
▪ Félix d’Hérelle conducted large-scale phage therapy trials against cholera in India (including Punjab
and Assam) in the late 1920s and 1930s with the Indian Medical Service — among the earliest field trials
of phage therapy.
▪ Indian research institutions are now actively engaged in virus discovery using metagenomics, for
example in bats, rodents and mosquitoes (ICMR–NIV Pune, ICAR–NIHSAD Bhopal and others),
which has identified Nipah virus in fruit bats in several states and novel viruses whose significance is
being investigated.
▪ Bacteriophage therapy and phage-based products against multidrug-resistant bacteria (including
veterinary pathogens) are an active area of research in Indian universities and ICAR institutes, in line
Chapter 2 | Page 17



VMC 503: General Virology
with India’s National Action Plan on AMR.
▪ Studies of endogenous retroviruses in Indian livestock breeds (e.g. endogenous retroviral elements in
cattle, buffalo, sheep and goat genomes) contribute to understanding genome evolution and are relevant
to xenotransplantation and biologicals safety.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Demonstration of filterability: passing bacterial and bacteriophage suspensions through 0.22 μm
membrane filters and testing the filtrates.
▪ Isolation of bacteriophages from sewage or water against E. coli (plaque formation) — illustrating viral
abundance in the environment.
▪ Observation of electron micrographs of viruses of different sizes and shapes.
▪ Discussion: preparing a comparison chart of viruses, bacteria, rickettsiae, chlamydiae and
mycoplasmas.
2.9 Summary
• Viruses are obligate intracellular parasites with a DNA or RNA genome in a protein capsid (with
or without an envelope) that replicate by assembly of components synthesised in host cells.
• Lwoff (1957) defined viruses by their single type of nucleic acid, replication from nucleic acid
alone, lack of growth and division, absence of energy-generating systems and dependence on host
ribosomes.
• Viruses range from 17–20 nm circoviruses (~2 kb genome) to giant viruses over 1 μm with
genomes of up to ~2.5 Mb.
• Viruses exist as inert virions outside cells and as replicating virocells inside; they evolve, adapt
and have shaped cellular life, placing them "at the edge of life".
• The regressive, escape and virus-first hypotheses each explain some features; modern evidence
from hallmark genes suggests viruses are polyphyletic, and ICTV recognises several realms
reflecting independent origins.
• Giant viruses, virophages, endogenous retroviruses (about 8% of the human genome) and
domesticated viral genes such as syncytins show the deep evolutionary interplay between viruses
and hosts.
• Viruses are the most abundant biological entities, have important ecological roles and many
beneficial uses; Hankin’s 1896 observations on Ganga water in India foreshadowed phage
discovery.
2.10 Glossary
Table 2.5: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Virion Complete infectious extracellular virus particle.
Capsid Protein shell enclosing the viral genome.
Capsomere Morphological subunit of the capsid visible by electron microscopy.
Eclipse phase Period after infection when no infectious virus can be recovered from cells.
Virocell A virus-infected cell reprogrammed to produce virions.
Quasispecies Population of closely related viral genome variants.
Hallmark genes Genes shared by many virus groups but absent from cells.
Realm Highest rank in ICTV virus taxonomy, reflecting an independent origin.
Chapter 2 | Page 18



VMC 503: General Virology
Term Meaning
LUCA Last universal common ancestor of cellular life.
Giant virus Very large DNA virus of protists with genomes exceeding 1 Mb.
Virophage Small virus that replicates only in cells co-infected with a giant virus.
Endogenous retrovirus Retroviral provirus integrated in the germ line and inherited.
Syncytin Placental fusion protein derived from an endogenous retroviral envelope gene.
Virome The collection of viruses in an organism or environment.
Spillover Transmission of a virus from its reservoir host to a new host species.

UNIT I — GENERAL PROPERTIES OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 3
Morphological Structure and Chemical Composition of Viruses
Lectures 3 and 4
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Describe the structural components of virions — genome, capsid, capsomeres, nucleocapsid, envelope
and peplomers.
▪ Explain helical, icosahedral and complex symmetry, including the principles of quasi-equivalence and
triangulation numbers.
▪ Describe the methods used to study virus structure, including negative-stain electron microscopy, cryo-
electron microscopy and X-ray crystallography.
▪ Describe the chemical composition of viruses — nucleic acids, proteins, lipids and carbohydrates — and
the diversity of viral genomes.
▪ Relate structure and composition to the physicochemical properties of viruses, their stability,
inactivation and choice of disinfectants.
LECTURE 3: MORPHOLOGICAL STRUCTURE OF VIRUSES
3.1 Introduction
A virion is a metastable structure: it must be stable enough to protect the genome in the harsh
extracellular environment (drying, UV light, enzymes, pH changes), yet unstable enough to
disassemble and release its genome on entering a suitable host cell. Virus structure is therefore a
compromise shaped by evolution. Virions are built on principles of economy and self-assembly:
because small genomes can encode only a few proteins, capsids are made from many copies of one or
a few protein subunits arranged with symmetry — a principle first proposed by Crick and Watson
(1956) and developed by Caspar and Klug (1962).

3.3 Capsid Symmetry
Viral capsids show three main architectures: helical, icosahedral (cubic) and complex.
3.3.1 Helical Symmetry
• Identical protein subunits (protomers) are arranged in a helix around the nucleic acid, which lies
in a groove between successive turns; each protomer interacts with the same number of
nucleotides (e.g. 3 nucleotides per subunit in TMV).
• The length of the helix is determined by the length of the genome; the diameter by the protein
subunit. Helical capsids can in principle accommodate genomes of any length.
• Plant viruses with helical symmetry are often naked, rigid rods (TMV, 300 × 18 nm) or flexuous
filaments (potyviruses).
• All animal viruses with helical nucleocapsids are enveloped and have negative-sense or
ambisense RNA genomes (orthomyxo-, paramyxo-, pneumo-, rhabdo-, filo-, bunya-like viruses)
— plus the positive-sense coronaviruses and arteriviruses. The flexible helical
ribonucleoprotein (RNP) is coiled inside the envelope and is the active template for the viral
polymerase.
Chapter 3 | Page 23



VMC 503: General Virology
• Rhabdoviruses show a bullet-shaped (rabies, VSV) or bacilliform particle; filoviruses are long
filaments; influenza virions are pleomorphic, spherical or filamentous.
3.3.2 Icosahedral Symmetry
• The icosahedron is a regular solid with 20 equilateral triangular faces, 12 vertices and 30
edges, showing 5:3:2 rotational symmetry — five-fold axes through the vertices, three-fold
axes through the faces and two-fold axes through the edges. It is the most efficient arrangement of
identical subunits to enclose a space (a "sphere") and gives a capsid of maximum volume.
• The simplest icosahedral capsid contains 60 identical subunits (3 per face), each in an identical
environment (e.g. parvoviruses, circoviruses; T = 1).
• Quasi-equivalence (Caspar and Klug, 1962): larger capsids contain 60T subunits, where T is
the triangulation number; subunits occupy slightly different (quasi-equivalent) environments
by making small adjustments in their bonding. The subunits are clustered into 12 pentamers at
the vertices and 10(T − 1) hexamers on the faces.
• T = h2 + hk + k2, where h and k are non-negative integers; permitted values are 1, 3, 4, 7, 9, 12, 13,
16, 19, 21, 25 … Examples: T = 1 — parvoviruses, circoviruses; pseudo T = 3 (P = 3) —
picornaviruses (60 copies each of VP1, VP2 and VP3); T = 3 — caliciviruses, nodaviruses; T = 4
— togaviruses (alphaviruses); T = 7 — papillomaviruses and polyomaviruses (pentamers only,
72 capsomeres); T = 13 — birnaviruses (IBDV) and reovirus outer shells; T = 16 — herpesvirus
capsid (162 capsomeres: 150 hexons, 12 pentons); pseudo T = 25 — adenoviruses (252
capsomeres: 240 hexons and 12 pentons).
• Number of capsomeres = 10T + 2 (e.g. herpesviruses: 10 × 16 + 2 = 162).
• Icosahedral capsids may be naked (picorna-, calici-, parvo-, circo-, adeno-, papilloma-,
polyoma-, reo-, birnaviruses) or enveloped (herpes-, toga-, flavi-, asfar- and retroviruses — the
retroviral core varies in shape).
• Some icosahedral viruses have multiple concentric shells: reoviruses and orbiviruses (e.g.
bluetongue virus) have inner core, outer core and outer capsid layers; rotaviruses are triple-
layered particles (TLPs) — only TLPs are infectious.
Chapter 3 | Page 24



VMC 503: General Virology
3.3.3 Complex Symmetry
• Poxviruses — the largest animal viruses (e.g. vaccinia 360 × 270 × 250 nm): brick-shaped
(orthopoxviruses) or ovoid (parapoxviruses such as orf virus, with a characteristic criss-cross
"ball of wool" surface pattern of tubules). Structure: outer membrane with surface tubules, a
biconcave (dumbbell-shaped) core containing the dsDNA genome and transcription enzymes,
and two lateral bodies. Two infectious forms: mature virions (MV) with one membrane and
extracellular enveloped virions (EV) with an additional envelope.
• Tailed bacteriophages (e.g. T4) — icosahedral (or elongated) head, helical tail with a contractile
sheath, base plate and tail fibres (binal symmetry).
• Retroviruses — spherical envelope surrounding a conical (lentiviruses — HIV, EIAV),
spherical or eccentric core.
• Giant viruses (Mimivirus) — icosahedral capsids covered with fibres, with a special vertex
("stargate") for genome release; Pithovirus is amphora-shaped.
3.4 The Viral Envelope
• Origin: acquired by budding through a host membrane — plasma membrane (orthomyxo-,
paramyxo-, rhabdo-, retro-, togaviruses), inner nuclear membrane (herpesviruses, primary
envelopment), endoplasmic reticulum (flaviviruses, rotavirus transient envelope), ER–Golgi
intermediate compartment (coronaviruses, arteriviruses) or Golgi (bunyaviruses). Poxviruses
and ASFV acquire membranes by wrapping with ER/Golgi cisternae.
• Composition: lipids reflect the host membrane from which they derive (phospholipids,
cholesterol, glycolipids), but viral envelope proteins replace host proteins. Viral glycoproteins
are type I or type II transmembrane proteins, often trimers (HA, S, F) or tetramers (NA).
Chapter 3 | Page 25



VMC 503: General Virology
• Functions of envelope glycoproteins: receptor binding (attachment), membrane fusion (class I
fusion proteins — influenza HA, paramyxovirus F, coronavirus S, retrovirus Env; class II —
flavivirus E, alphavirus E1; class III — rabies/VSV G, herpesvirus gB), receptor-destroying
enzyme activity (neuraminidase, haemagglutinin-esterase), haemagglutination, and
antigenicity.
• Consequences: enveloped viruses are generally sensitive to lipid solvents (ether, chloroform),
detergents, drying and acid, and are therefore usually transmitted by respiratory droplets,
blood, bites, sexual contact or close contact rather than by the faecal–oral route (exceptions exist).
• Matrix proteins line the inner face of the envelope, interact with the nucleocapsid and
cytoplasmic tails of glycoproteins, and drive assembly and budding.

VMC 503: General Virology
LECTURE 4: CHEMICAL COMPOSITION AND PHYSICOCHEMICAL PROPERTIES OF
VIRUSES
3.7 Chemical Composition: Overview
Virions are composed of nucleic acid (1–50% of mass; about 1% in influenza virus, 25–30% in
picornaviruses, and up to 50% in some phages), protein (the major component of most virions), and, in
enveloped viruses, lipid (20–35%) and carbohydrate (mainly as glycoproteins and glycolipids). Some
viruses also contain polyamines (spermidine, spermine) that neutralise nucleic acid charge, metal ions
(e.g. Ca2+ stabilising rotavirus outer capsid), and host cell components (e.g. host tRNA primers in
retroviruses, host actin or cyclophilin in some virions).
3.8 Viral Nucleic Acids
3.8.1 Diversity of Viral Genomes
Viral genomes are more diverse than those of any other biological group. Unlike cells, which all use
double-stranded DNA, viruses may use any form of nucleic acid.

VMC 503: General Virology
3.8.2 Structural Features of Viral Genomes
• Size: from about 1.7 kb (circoviruses) to 375 kb (poxviruses) in animal viruses and up to ~2.5 Mb
in giant viruses. RNA genomes are generally smaller (up to ~32 kb) because of the high error rate
of RNA polymerases.
• Sense (polarity): positive (+) sense RNA has the same sequence as mRNA; negative (−) sense
RNA is complementary to mRNA; ambisense RNA has both polarities on the same segment
(arenaviruses, some bunyaviruses).
• Segmentation: genomes may be divided into 2–12 segments (all in one particle in animal
viruses); segmented viruses can undergo reassortment (influenza antigenic shift; BTV).
• Terminal structures: 5′ methylated cap (m7GpppN; e.g. flavi-, corona-, toga-, retroviruses) or
5′ VPg (a genome-linked protein; picorna- and caliciviruses); 3′ poly(A) tail (picornaviruses,
coronaviruses); internal ribosome entry sites (IRES) in picornaviruses and pestiviruses;
inverted terminal repeats (ITRs) in adeno- and parvoviruses; long terminal repeats (LTRs) in
retroviral proviruses; covalently closed hairpin loops at the ends of poxvirus and ASFV DNA;
terminal proteins covalently linked to adenovirus DNA (priming).
• Coding strategies to maximise information: overlapping reading frames (e.g. P/V/C of
paramyxoviruses via RNA editing), ribosomal frameshifting (coronaviruses ORF1a/1b;
retrovirus gag–pol), leaky scanning, alternative splicing (adeno-, herpes-, retro-,
orthomyxoviruses), polyprotein synthesis and proteolytic processing (picorna-, flavi-,
retroviruses), subgenomic mRNAs (toga-, corona-, arteriviruses).
• Infectivity of naked genome: purified genomes of +ssRNA viruses (e.g. poliovirus, FMDV)
and some dsDNA viruses (papilloma-, polyoma-, adeno-, herpesviruses) are infectious when
introduced into cells (transfection); −ssRNA and dsRNA genomes and poxvirus DNA are not
infectious because they need virion enzymes — the basis of reverse genetics systems that supply
these proteins.
Chapter 3 | Page 29



VMC 503: General Virology
3.9 Viral Proteins
• Structural proteins are components of the virion: capsid proteins, nucleoproteins, matrix
proteins, tegument proteins, envelope glycoproteins and virion-associated enzymes.
• Non-structural proteins are encoded by the genome and synthesised in infected cells but not
incorporated into virions: replicases and helicases, proteases, proteins that modify host functions
(e.g. inhibitors of interferon responses, apoptosis regulators), and assembly scaffolding proteins.
• Functions of structural proteins: protection of the genome; recognition of cell receptors;
membrane fusion or penetration; genome delivery; determinants of antigenicity (neutralising
epitopes), host range, tissue tropism and virulence.

maturation of virions

3.9.1 Glycoproteins and Antigenic Properties
• Envelope proteins are glycosylated by host enzymes in the ER and Golgi (N-linked to
asparagine; O-linked to serine/threonine). Glycosylation assists folding, transport and receptor
binding, and glycan shields can mask epitopes from antibodies (e.g. HIV Env, influenza HA).
• Many viral fusion proteins are synthesised as inactive precursors that must be cleaved by host
proteases (e.g. influenza HA0 → HA1 + HA2; NDV F0 → F1 + F2; coronavirus S at S1/S2). A
multibasic cleavage site cleavable by ubiquitous furin-like proteases is a key virulence
determinant (HPAI viruses, velogenic NDV).
• Surface proteins exposed on the virion are the main targets of neutralising antibodies and
therefore the key antigens in vaccines (e.g. HA of influenza, VP1 of FMDV, G of rabies, VP2 of
IBDV and CPV, S of coronaviruses).
3.10 Lipids and Carbohydrates
• Lipids are found in enveloped viruses (and a few non-enveloped viruses with internal
membranes, e.g. some phages). Their composition reflects the host membrane at which budding
Chapter 3 | Page 30



VMC 503: General Virology
occurs — viruses budding from plasma membrane lipid rafts are rich in cholesterol and
sphingolipids. Poxviruses and ASFV contain internal lipid membranes.
• Lipids make enveloped viruses susceptible to lipid solvents and detergents; this sensitivity (the
ether/chloroform test) is used in virus classification.
• Carbohydrates occur mainly as glycoproteins and glycolipids of the envelope; host-derived
glycans may be incorporated. Virus-specific glycans influence antigenicity and immune evasion;
lectin-like binding to host sialic acids and glycosaminoglycans (e.g. heparan sulphate) mediates
attachment of many viruses.
3.11 Physicochemical Properties and Stability
Table 3.6: Physical and chemical properties of virions
Property Significance and examples
Sedimentation coefficient
(S)
Rate of sedimentation in a centrifugal field; depends on mass and shape; used in
purification (e.g. picornaviruses ~150–160S)
Buoyant density
Density at which virions band in CsCl or sucrose gradients; non-enveloped viruses
are denser (CsCl 1.30–1.45 g/ml) than enveloped viruses (1.15–1.25 g/ml); used
for purification and characterisation
Stability at different pH
Enteroviruses, caliciviruses, rotaviruses are acid-stable (pH 3), allowing
survival in the stomach; aphthoviruses (FMDV) and rhinoviruses are acid-labile
(inactivated below pH 6.5–6) — FMDV is inactivated by the post-mortem
acidification of muscle (rigor), but survives in lymph nodes, bone marrow and
offal where pH remains neutral
Heat stability
Most viruses are inactivated at 56–60 °C for 30 min (exceptions: parvoviruses,
circoviruses, papillomaviruses, and prions); MgCl2 stabilises some viruses
(picornaviruses — test for enteroviruses) but not others; freezing at −70 °C or
lyophilisation preserves infectivity
Lipid solvents and
detergents
Ether, chloroform, sodium deoxycholate and non-ionic detergents inactivate
enveloped viruses; non-enveloped viruses are resistant
Radiation
UV (254 nm) damages nucleic acids (pyrimidine dimers); ionising radiation;
photodynamic inactivation with dyes (neutral red, methylene blue); inactivated
viruses may be reactivated by multiplicity reactivation
Chemical inactivants
Formaldehyde, β-propiolactone and binary ethylenimine (BEI) — used in
producing inactivated vaccines (BEI is preferred for FMD vaccines because it
alkylates nucleic acid while preserving capsid antigenicity)
Drying and
environmental survival
Non-enveloped viruses (e.g. canine parvovirus, FMDV in organic matter,
rotavirus, ASFV — enveloped but exceptionally stable in blood and pork
products) may persist for months in the environment
3.11.1 Relative Resistance of Viruses to Disinfectants
• Most susceptible — enveloped viruses (influenza, NDV, herpesviruses, coronaviruses,
PRRSV): inactivated by most disinfectants, including detergents, alcohols (70%), quaternary
ammonium compounds, phenolics, hypochlorite and oxidising agents.
• Intermediate — large non-enveloped viruses (adenoviruses, reoviruses, rotaviruses): need
hypochlorite, aldehydes, oxidising agents (peroxygen compounds) or alkalis.
• Most resistant — small non-enveloped viruses (parvoviruses, circoviruses, picornaviruses,
caliciviruses): resistant to alcohols, quaternary ammonium compounds and many phenolics;
inactivated by sodium hypochlorite (1:30 bleach), aldehydes (formalin, glutaraldehyde),
peracetic acid, potassium peroxymonosulphate, and strong alkalis (2% NaOH) or acids (for
Chapter 3 | Page 31



VMC 503: General Virology
acid-labile FMDV, 0.2% citric acid; for alkali-sensitive viruses, 4% sodium carbonate (washing
soda)).
• Prions are the most resistant infectious agents (Chapter 7) — require autoclaving at 134 °C for 18
min or 1–2 M NaOH.
INDIA FOCUS: APPLYING VIRUS STRUCTURE AND COMPOSITION IN INDIA
▪ Disinfection in Indian outbreak control: national contingency plans and field practice rely on
structure-based choice of disinfectants — e.g. 4% sodium carbonate (washing soda) or 2% sodium
hydroxide and 0.2% citric acid for FMD (an acid-labile, non-enveloped picornavirus), hypochlorite and
quaternary ammonium–aldehyde combinations for HPAI and Newcastle disease (enveloped), and 2%
NaOH or hypochlorite for African swine fever and lumpy skin disease premises. Choice of the wrong
agent (e.g. alcohol or QACs alone against canine parvovirus) is a common cause of failure in kennels
and clinics.
▪ Vaccine production: Indian manufacturers of inactivated FMD vaccines (for the NADCP) use binary
ethylenimine (BEI) inactivation and sucrose/PEG purification of 146S virions; the 146S intact capsid
(as opposed to 12S pentameric subunits) is the immunogenic form, and vaccine potency depends on
maintaining capsid integrity — a direct application of structural virology. The thermolabile FMDV
capsid has motivated research at ICAR institutes on thermostable capsid mutants and virus-like
particle (VLP) vaccines.
▪ Structural biology facilities: high-end cryo-electron microscopes are now available at Indian
institutions such as the Indian Institute of Science (IISc), Bengaluru, the National Centre for
Biological Sciences (NCBS)–inStem, Bengaluru, and other national facilities, enabling Indian
scientists to determine near-atomic structures of viral proteins and capsids, including SARS-CoV-2
spike–antibody complexes.
▪ Electron microscopy in diagnosis remains available at several veterinary institutes (e.g. ICAR–IVRI,
ICAR–NIHSAD) for rapid identification of poxviruses (goatpox, sheep pox, LSD, orf), rotaviruses and
other agents by negative staining.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
Chapter 3 | Page 32



VMC 503: General Virology
▪ Study of electron micrographs of viruses of different families; drawing and identification of helical,
icosahedral and complex forms.
▪ Construction of an icosahedral capsid model from paper templates; calculation of capsomere numbers
using T-numbers.
▪ Negative staining technique (demonstration) with phosphotungstic acid.
▪ Ether/chloroform sensitivity test to determine the presence of an envelope; acid (pH 3) stability test; heat
stability test with and without MgCl2.
▪ Purification of a virus by sucrose density gradient centrifugation (demonstration).
▪ Selection and preparation of disinfectant solutions (2% NaOH, 4% sodium carbonate, hypochlorite) for
viral outbreak control.
3.12 Summary
• A virion consists of a genome enclosed in a protein capsid made of capsomeres (formed from
protomers), with or without a lipid envelope bearing glycoprotein peplomers; some viruses have
matrix, tegument or core layers.
• Capsids show helical symmetry (all animal viruses with helical nucleocapsids are enveloped),
icosahedral symmetry (60T subunits; T = h2 + hk + k2; 10T + 2 capsomeres) or complex
symmetry (poxviruses, tailed phages).
• Envelopes are derived from host membranes by budding and carry fusion and attachment
glycoproteins; enveloped viruses are sensitive to lipid solvents and detergents.
• Structures are studied by negative-stain EM, thin-section EM, cryo-EM (Nobel 2017), X-ray
crystallography and computational prediction.
• Viral genomes are uniquely diverse — ds or ss DNA or RNA, linear, circular or segmented,
positive, negative or ambisense — with special terminal features (cap, VPg, poly(A), IRES, ITRs,
hairpins, LTRs).
• Viral proteins are structural or non-structural; virion enzymes (RdRp, reverse transcriptase,
neuraminidase) are essential for certain families; proteolytic cleavage of fusion proteins
determines virulence.
• Physicochemical properties — buoyant density, sedimentation, pH and heat stability, solvent
sensitivity — aid classification, purification, inactivation for vaccines (BEI, β-propiolactone) and
rational choice of disinfectants (enveloped < large naked < small naked viruses < prions).
3.13 Glossary
Table 3.7: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Protomer Basic protein subunit of a capsid.
Capsomere Morphological subunit of a capsid (pentamer or hexamer).
Nucleocapsid Capsid together with the enclosed genome.
Peplomer Glycoprotein spike projecting from the viral envelope.
Tegument Protein layer between capsid and envelope of herpesviruses.
Quasi-equivalence Slightly different bonding environments of identical subunits in larger capsids.
Triangulation number (T) Number describing the size and subunit arrangement of an icosahedral capsid.
Ribonucleoprotein (RNP) Complex of viral RNA with nucleoprotein, the template for transcription.
VPg Viral protein genome-linked, attached to the 5′ end of picornavirus RNA.
IRES Internal ribosome entry site allowing cap-independent translation.
Chapter 3 | Page 33



VMC 503: General Virology
Term Meaning
Ambisense Genome segment with both positive- and negative-sense coding regions.
Buoyant density Density at which a virion bands in a density gradient.

structures.
BEI Binary ethylenimine, a nucleic acid-alkylating agent used to inactivate viruses
for vaccines.
146S particle Intact FMDV virion, the immunogenic component of FMD vaccines.

LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Trace the development of virus classification from host- and disease-based groupings to modern
genome-based taxonomy.
▪ Describe the criteria used to classify viruses and the role of the International Committee on Taxonomy
of Viruses (ICTV).
▪ Explain the 15-rank ICTV hierarchy, virus realms and the binomial format of virus species names, and
distinguish a virus from a virus species.
▪ Describe the Baltimore classification and assign important animal virus families to Baltimore classes.
▪ Apply correct nomenclature for viruses, strains, serotypes, genotypes and lineages, including influenza
and FMD strain designations.
LECTURE 5: PRINCIPLES, HISTORY AND THE ICTV SYSTEM
4.1 Why Classify Viruses?
Classification (taxonomy) orders the enormous diversity of viruses into groups that share common
properties and evolutionary history, while nomenclature provides names for these groups. A sound
classification allows virologists to predict properties of a newly discovered virus (e.g. its replication
strategy, stability, antiviral susceptibility and likely diseases), aids diagnosis, epidemiological
communication, regulation (biosafety, trade, vaccine licensing) and the study of viral evolution.
4.2 Historical Approaches to Classification

4.3 Criteria Used in Classification
Table 4.2: Properties used for virus classification
Category Properties
Genome
Type of nucleic acid (DNA/RNA); strandedness (ss/ds); linear/circular; segmented
or not (number of segments); sense (+, −, ambisense); size; G + C content;
presence of 5′ cap or VPg, 3′ poly(A); terminal repeats; nucleotide sequence and

replication
Gene order; number and position of ORFs; transcription and translation strategies;

Antigenic properties Serological relationships (neutralisation, HI, CFT, ELISA) — especially for
species and serotype distinctions
Biological properties Natural host range; mode of transmission; vector relationships; geographic
distribution; pathogenicity; tissue tropism; pathology
Today, phylogenetic analysis of genome sequences (particularly conserved genes such as RdRp,
DNA polymerase, helicase and major capsid protein) is the principal basis for classification at all ranks,
supplemented by the other properties. Species demarcation criteria vary by family (e.g. percentage
sequence identity thresholds, host range, antigenicity).
4.4 The International Committee on Taxonomy of Viruses (ICTV)
• The ICTV is the only body authorised by the International Union of Microbiological Societies
(IUMS) to develop, refine and maintain a universal virus taxonomy. Founded in 1966 as the
International Committee on Nomenclature of Viruses (ICNV) and renamed ICTV in the 1970s.
• Taxonomic changes are proposed as taxonomic proposals (TaxoProps), reviewed by study
groups and subcommittees (animal DNA viruses and retroviruses; animal dsRNA and ssRNA−
viruses; animal ssRNA+ viruses; bacterial and archaeal viruses; fungal and protist viruses; plant
viruses), approved by the Executive Committee and ratified by the ICTV membership.
• Approved taxonomy is published annually in the Master Species List (MSL) and the online
ICTV Report on Virus Taxonomy (ictv.global), with "Virus Metadata Resource" linking
species to exemplar isolates and GenBank sequences.
• Scale of current taxonomy: the 2025-2026 ICTV taxonomy release (MSL41) recognises 10
realms, 11 kingdoms, 22 phyla, 50 classes, 105 orders, 427 families, 232 subfamilies, 4,149
Chapter 4 | Page 38



VMC 503: General Virology
genera and 17,554 species — and the numbers grow every year as metagenomic discoveries are
classified.
• The International Code of Virus Classification and Nomenclature (ICVCN) lays down the
rules; it governs names of taxa (from species to realm) but not the names of viruses themselves,
which are not regulated by ICTV.
4.5 The ICTV Hierarchy and Nomenclature Rules
4.5.1 The 15 Ranks
In 2019–2020, ICTV expanded the classification from the traditional five ranks (order, family,
subfamily, genus, species) to a 15-rank hierarchy (Gorbalenya et al., 2020), comparable to the
Linnaean system used for cellular organisms. Eight principal ranks and seven derived ranks are
recognised.
Table 4.3: ICTV ranks and suffixes, with rabies virus as an example
Rank Suffix Example (rabies virus)
Realm -viria Riboviria
Subrealm -vira —
Kingdom -virae Orthornavirae
Subkingdom -virites —
Phylum -viricota Negarnaviricota
Subphylum -viricotina Haploviricotina
Class -viricetes Monjiviricetes
Subclass -viricetidae —
Order -virales Mononegavirales
Suborder -virineae —
Family -viridae Rhabdoviridae
Subfamily -virinae Alpharhabdovirinae
Genus -virus Lyssavirus
Subgenus -virus —
Species Binomial: genus + epithet Lyssavirus rabies

VMC 503: General Virology
4.5.2 Rules for Writing Names
• Names of taxa (realm to genus and species) are written in italics with the first letter capitalised
(e.g. Paramyxoviridae, Morbillivirus). When a taxon name is used formally, the rank may
precede it: "the family Poxviridae".
• Binomial species names: since 2021, ICTV has required all virus species to have Linnaean-
style binomial names consisting of the genus name followed by a species epithet (e.g.
Betacoronavirus pandemicum, Orthoflavivirus japonicum). The renaming of existing species
was largely completed during 2021–2024. The entire binomial is italicised; the epithet begins
with a lower-case letter (or may be alphanumeric).
• Viruses are not species: a species is an abstract taxonomic category (a concept), whereas a virus
is a physical entity that is assigned to a species. Virus names are not italicised and not
capitalised (except proper nouns): "rabies virus", "Newcastle disease virus", "severe acute
respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2)". Thus Betacoronavirus pandemicum is the
species; SARS-CoV-2 and SARS-CoV are viruses belonging to it.
• Abbreviations of virus names are widely used (FMDV, NDV, PPRV, BTV, LSDV) but are not
official taxa.
• Informal and vernacular names such as "herpesviruses", "poxviruses", "arboviruses" and
"coronaviruses" are written in plain type without capitals.
• Below species (not regulated by ICTV): genotype, serotype, lineage/clade, topotype, strain,
isolate, variant, pathotype (e.g. velogenic NDV) and biotype (e.g. cytopathic and non-
cytopathic BVDV) — Section 4.9.

4.5.3 Current Realms
• A realm groups viruses believed to share a common origin, identified by hallmark genes or
protein folds. The MSL41 recognises 10 realms. Those containing animal viruses are:
◦ Riboviria — all RNA viruses that encode an RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (kingdom
Orthornavirae) and viruses encoding a reverse transcriptase (kingdom Pararnavirae —
retroviruses and hepadnaviruses).
◦ Duplodnaviria — dsDNA viruses with the HK97-fold major capsid protein — tailed
bacteriophages (Caudoviricetes) and herpesviruses (order Herpesvirales).
◦ Varidnaviria — dsDNA viruses with double (or single) jelly-roll capsid proteins —
adenoviruses, poxviruses, asfarviruses (ASFV), iridoviruses and giant viruses
(Nucleocytoviricota).
◦ Monodnaviria — small ssDNA viruses with rolling-circle replication initiation proteins
and their descendants — parvoviruses, circoviruses, and the small dsDNA
papillomaviruses and polyomaviruses.
◦ Ribozyviria — hepatitis delta virus-like agents.
• Other realms (e.g. Adnaviria and the newly created Singelaviria, 2025) contain viruses of
archaea, bacteria and protists. Some families (e.g. Anelloviridae) remain unassigned to a realm.
Chapter 4 | Page 41



VMC 503: General Virology
LECTURE 6: BALTIMORE CLASSIFICATION, ANIMAL VIRUS FAMILIES AND
BELOW-SPECIES NOMENCLATURE
4.6 The Baltimore Classification
In 1971, David Baltimore proposed that all viruses must produce messenger RNA (mRNA) that can
be translated by host ribosomes, and that viruses could be grouped according to the pathway from
genome to mRNA. By convention, mRNA is designated positive (+) sense; nucleic acid
complementary to mRNA is negative (−) sense. The system, later expanded to seven classes, is not a
formal taxonomy but is an extremely useful framework for understanding replication strategies
(Chapters 8 and 9) and for predicting which enzymes a virus must carry or encode.

4.9.1 Strain Designation Conventions
• Influenza viruses (WHO convention): type / host (omitted if human) / geographic origin / strain
number / year of isolation (subtype) — e.g. A/chicken/India/10CA01/2021(H5N1) or
A/swine/Iowa/15/1930(H1N1).
• FMD viruses: serotype / country code / isolate number / year — e.g. O/IND/R2/1975 (Indian
vaccine strain), with topotype and lineage defined by VP1 sequencing (e.g. O/ME-SA/Ind-
2001e).
• Rotaviruses: group / species of origin / country / strain / year / G and P genotypes — e.g.
RVA/Cow-wt/IND/B-11/2011/G10P[11].
• Sequence-based names are linked to GenBank accession numbers; metagenomically discovered
viruses are often named by location or host (e.g. "Kasba virus", "Umbre virus").
INDIA FOCUS: VIRUSES NAMED FROM INDIA AND INDIAN NOMENCLATURE IN
PRACTICE
▪ Viruses discovered and named in India by ICMR–NIV Pune and collaborators include Kyasanur
Forest disease virus (Shimoga district, Karnataka, 1957; Orthoflavivirus), Chandipura virus
(Chandipur village, Maharashtra, 1965; Vesiculovirus, Rhabdoviridae), Ganjam virus (Odisha; the
Asian variant of Nairobi sheep disease virus, Nairoviridae), Bhanja virus (Odisha; Phenuiviridae)
and several other arboviruses named after Indian localities (e.g. Kasba, Umbre, Sathuperi, Chittoor
viruses).
▪ Serotypes and lineages in the national FMD programme: India has three FMDV serotypes — O, A
and Asia 1 (serotype C has not been reported since 1995). Serotype O viruses belong to the ME-SA
topotype, with successive dominant lineages (e.g. Ind-2001, with sublineages Ind-2001d and Ind-
2001e) identified by VP1 sequencing at ICAR–National Institute on Foot-and-Mouth Disease
(NIFMD), Bhubaneswar, which also guides vaccine-strain matching (e.g. O/IND/R2/1975,
A/IND/40/2000, Asia 1/IND/63/1972).
▪ Bluetongue: India is one of the most BTV-diverse countries, with more than 20 serotypes reported; the
indigenous inactivated pentavalent/multivalent BTV vaccine (ICAR) is based on locally prevalent
serotypes, illustrating why serotype nomenclature matters for vaccine design.
▪ Other examples: NDV genotype XIII viruses predominate in Indian poultry outbreaks; Indian PPRV
isolates belong to lineage IV, and the homologous vaccine strain Sungri/96 is widely used in India;
H5N1 viruses in Indian poultry have belonged mainly to clades 2.2 (2006–2008) and 2.3.2.1a
(predominant in later outbreaks), while clade 2.3.4.4b viruses have spread widely across Asia.
▪ Correct use of binomial species names and strain designations is now expected in Indian theses and
journals, and GenBank submissions from Indian laboratories should follow ICTV and WHO/WOAH
conventions.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Use of the ICTV website (ictv.global) — taxonomy browser, Master Species List and Report chapters
— to classify selected veterinary viruses.
▪ Exercise: assigning given viruses to Baltimore classes and ICTV families from their properties.
▪ Writing correct names: italicised species names versus plain virus names; writing influenza and FMD
strain designations.
Chapter 4 | Page 46



VMC 503: General Virology
▪ Retrieval of reference sequences from GenBank and construction of a simple phylogenetic tree (e.g.
FMDV VP1 or NDV F gene) using MEGA software (demonstration).
4.10 Summary
• Early classifications by host, disease or transmission are useful but not taxonomic; the LHT
system (1962) introduced classification by virion properties.
• The ICTV (founded 1966) is the official body for virus taxonomy; the MSL41 (2026) recognises
10 realms, 427 families, 4,149 genera and 17,554 species.
• Classification uses genome, morphology, physicochemical, protein, replication, antigenic and
biological properties, with genome phylogeny now central.
• ICTV uses 15 ranks from realm (-viria) to species; since 2021 species have binomial names (e.g.
Lyssavirus rabies, Betacoronavirus pandemicum).
• Species are italicised taxa; viruses (rabies virus, SARS-CoV-2) are physical entities, written in
plain type and not regulated by ICTV.
• The Baltimore classification groups viruses into seven classes according to the route from
genome to mRNA.
• Below-species categories — serotype, subtype, genotype, lineage, topotype, pathotype, biotype,
strain, isolate and variant — are important in diagnosis, epidemiology and vaccine selection;
influenza and FMD viruses have standard strain designations.
• India has contributed virus names such as KFD, Chandipura and Ganjam viruses, and uses
serotype/lineage nomenclature for FMD, BTV, NDV, PPRV and avian influenza control.
4.11 Glossary
Table 4.10: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Taxonomy Science of classification and naming of organisms.
ICTV International Committee on Taxonomy of Viruses.
MSL Master Species List — official annual list of ICTV-approved taxa.
Realm Highest ICTV rank, grouping viruses of a common origin.
Binomial name Two-part species name: genus + species epithet.
Virus species A taxon (concept) to which individual viruses are assigned.
Baltimore classification Grouping of viruses by the pathway from genome to mRNA.
Positive sense Nucleic acid with the same sequence as mRNA.
Negative sense Nucleic acid complementary to mRNA.
Serotype Antigenically distinct group defined by neutralisation.
Genotype Genetically defined group within a species.
Topotype Geographically associated genetic group (FMDV).
Pathotype Group defined by virulence.
Biotype Group defined by effect in cell culture (e.g. cp/ncp BVDV).
Arbovirus Arthropod-borne virus.
Isolate Virus obtained from a single source.
