UNIT I — GENERAL PROPERTIES OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 5
Cultivation and Purification of Viruses
Lectures 7 and 8
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Describe the host systems used to cultivate viruses — laboratory animals, embryonated eggs, organ
cultures and cell cultures — and their applications.
▪ Explain the types of cell cultures, commonly used cell lines, culture media and the recognition of virus
growth in cell cultures.
▪ Describe the methods for quantifying viruses, including plaque assay, TCID50, EID50, haemagglutination
titre and molecular methods, and calculate end-point titres.
▪ Explain the principles of virus concentration and purification by precipitation, ultracentrifugation,
density gradients and chromatography.
▪ Relate cultivation and purification methods to diagnosis, research and veterinary vaccine production in
India.
LECTURE 7: CULTIVATION OF VIRUSES AND VIRUS QUANTIFICATION
5.1 Introduction
Because viruses are obligate intracellular parasites, they cannot be grown on inanimate media. They
must be propagated in living host systems: whole animals, embryonated eggs, organ cultures or cell
cultures. Cultivation is required for isolation and identification of viruses in diagnosis, for research
on virus biology, for production of antigens for serological tests and, above all, for manufacture of
vaccines. Each system has advantages and limitations, and the choice depends on the virus and the
purpose.
5.2 Laboratory Animals
• Historical role: the first host systems (Pasteur’s rabbits for rabies; suckling mice for
arboviruses); still used when no suitable cell culture exists or for pathogenesis, virulence and
vaccine potency testing.
• Commonly used animals: suckling mice (1–3 days old; intracerebral inoculation for rabies,
arboviruses such as KFD and Chandipura, coxsackieviruses); adult mice; guinea pigs; rabbits;
ferrets (influenza); hamsters; chickens; and the natural host (e.g. calves for FMD, pigs for ASF,
dogs for CDV) for challenge studies.
• Routes: intracerebral, intraperitoneal, intranasal, subcutaneous, intramuscular, intravenous,
footpad, oral.
• Evidence of growth: clinical signs (paralysis, death), lesions, histopathology and inclusion
bodies (e.g. Negri bodies in rabies), seroconversion.
• Uses today: mouse neutralisation test and rabies vaccine potency (NIH test); intracerebral
pathogenicity index (ICPI) in day-old chicks for NDV and intravenous pathogenicity index
(IVPI) in 6-week-old chickens for avian influenza (WOAH tests for pathotyping); LD50
Chapter 5 | Page 51



VMC 503: General Virology
determination; production of hyperimmune sera and monoclonal antibodies; genetically
modified mice (e.g. receptor-transgenic, interferon-receptor knockout) for research.
• Limitations: cost, ethical concerns, variable susceptibility, possible latent infections, and
biosafety. Their use is guided by the 3Rs — Replacement, Reduction and Refinement — and
regulated in India by the Committee for Control and Supervision of Experiments on Animals
(CCSEA) (formerly CPCSEA) and institutional animal ethics committees (IAEC).
5.3 Embryonated Eggs
Introduced by Woodruff and Goodpasture (1931) and developed by Burnet, embryonated hens’
eggs remain a cheap, convenient and sterile host system, especially for avian viruses, influenza
viruses and poxviruses. Specific-pathogen-free (SPF) eggs — from flocks free of avian pathogens
and antibodies to them — should be used for diagnosis and vaccine production to avoid interference
and contamination.
Table 5.1: Routes of inoculation of embryonated eggs
Route Age of embryo
(days) Viruses Evidence of growth
Chorioallantoic
membrane (CAM) 10–12
Poxviruses (fowlpox, sheeppox,

(ILTV, HSV)

(rarely) Encephalitic lesions
• Procedure: eggs are candled to locate the embryo, air sac and blood vessels; the shell is
disinfected (70% alcohol/iodine), a hole is drilled, inoculum (0.1–0.2 ml) is introduced by the
appropriate route, the hole is sealed with wax or glue, and eggs are incubated at 35–37 °C and
candled daily; embryos dying within 24 h are discarded (non-specific trauma).
• Harvesting: chilling at 4 °C overnight (kills the embryo and reduces bleeding), then collection of
allantoic/amniotic fluid, CAM, yolk sac or embryo tissues.
• Advantages: cheap, available, sterile, free of antibodies (SPF), easy to handle, large yields (e.g.
10 ml allantoic fluid per egg) — the basis of most influenza vaccine and many poultry vaccine
(NDV, IBV, fowlpox) production.
Chapter 5 | Page 52



VMC 503: General Virology
• Limitations: limited to viruses adapted to avian cells; maternal antibodies in non-SPF eggs;
bacterial contamination; egg-adaptation mutations may alter antigenicity (e.g. in human
influenza HA).
5.4 Organ Cultures
• Small pieces of tissue maintained in vitro so that their differentiated architecture and function
are preserved (e.g. tracheal ring cultures with beating cilia; intestinal explants; skin explants).
• Used for viruses that grow only in differentiated epithelium — e.g. infectious bronchitis virus
(ciliostasis in chicken tracheal organ cultures, TOC — used for isolation and virus neutralisation),
human coronaviruses and rhinoviruses; 3D organoids (intestinal, respiratory) derived from stem
cells are the modern equivalent (e.g. for human norovirus, rotavirus and SARS-CoV-2).
5.5 Cell Cultures
Since Enders, Weller and Robbins (1949), cell (tissue) culture has been the principal system for
virus cultivation. Cells are dispersed from tissues by trypsinisation and grown as monolayers
attached to glass or plastic (flasks, roller bottles, multi-well plates, microcarriers) or in suspension (e.g.
BHK-21 suspension cells for FMD vaccines) at 37 °C in 5% CO2 in a humidified incubator.

Human viruses, adenoviruses, SARS-CoV-2 (with
ACE2), HEV
5.5.2 Culture Media and Conditions
• Basal media: balanced salt solutions (Hanks’, Earle’s) plus amino acids, vitamins and glucose —
e.g. Eagle’s Minimum Essential Medium (MEM), Dulbecco’s Modified Eagle’s Medium
Chapter 5 | Page 54



VMC 503: General Virology
(DMEM), Glasgow MEM (GMEM, for BHK-21), RPMI-1640, Medium 199, Leibovitz L-15
(CO2-independent); buffered with bicarbonate–CO2 or HEPES; phenol red as pH indicator.
• Serum: foetal bovine serum (FBS) 5–10% in growth medium (supplies growth factors,
attachment factors, hormones) and 1–2% in maintenance medium after infection; FBS must be
tested (and preferably irradiated) for BVDV, antibodies to BVDV and other adventitious agents.
Serum-free and chemically defined media are increasingly used in vaccine manufacture.
• Antibiotics and antifungals: penicillin–streptomycin, gentamicin, amphotericin B to prevent
bacterial and fungal contamination (they do not prevent mycoplasma contamination).
• Additives for specific viruses: trypsin (TPCK-treated) for influenza, rotavirus and some
coronaviruses (activates surface proteins); DEAE-dextran; polybrene.
• Maintenance of cell lines: subculture (passage) by trypsin–EDTA when confluent;
cryopreservation in liquid nitrogen (−196 °C) with 10% DMSO; master cell bank and
working cell bank system for vaccine production.
• Quality control: tests for mycoplasma (PCR, DNA staining — the commonest contaminant),
bacteria and fungi, adventitious viruses (e.g. BVDV, porcine circovirus, retroviruses), cell
identity (karyotyping, DNA barcoding/STR profiling), and tumorigenicity for vaccine cell
substrates.
5.5.3 Recognition of Virus Growth in Cell Culture
Table 5.4: Methods of detecting virus replication in cell cultures
Method Description and examples
Cytopathic effect
(CPE)
Morphological changes visible under the inverted microscope: rounding, shrinkage
and detachment (picorna-, entero-, parvoviruses); ballooning and clusters
(herpesviruses, adenoviruses — "grape-like" clusters); syncytia (giant cells) formed
by cell fusion (paramyxoviruses — PPRV, CDV, NDV; RSV; herpesviruses;
coronaviruses); vacuolation (polyomaviruses); focal proliferation/transformation
(oncogenic viruses); inclusion bodies — intracytoplasmic (poxviruses — Guarnieri
bodies; rabies — Negri bodies; reoviruses), intranuclear (herpes — Cowdry type A;
adenoviruses), or both (morbilliviruses)
Haemadsorption
Adherence of erythrocytes to infected cells expressing viral haemagglutinin
(orthomyxo-, paramyxoviruses); ASFV haemadsorption with pig RBC in leucocyte
cultures (rosettes)
Haemagglutination of
culture fluid Released virus agglutinates RBC (influenza, NDV)
Interference
Non-cytopathic virus prevents CPE by a second (challenge) virus — e.g. END
(exaltation of Newcastle disease virus) and interference tests for ncp BVDV and
classical swine fever virus
Immunostaining
Detection of viral antigens by immunofluorescence (IFA/FAT) or
immunoperoxidase (IPMA) — essential for non-cytopathic viruses (ncp BVDV,
CSFV, PCV2, PRRSV in some cells)
Molecular detection PCR/RT-qPCR on culture supernatant; reporter viruses (GFP/luciferase)
Electron microscopy Morphology of virions in cell culture harvests
Chapter 5 | Page 55



VMC 503: General Virology
5.6 Quantification of Viruses (Titration)
Viruses can be measured either as infectious units (biological assays) or as physical
particles/genomes. The ratio of physical particles to infectious units (particle-to-infectivity ratio) is
typically 1–10 for picornaviruses and parvoviruses in optimal conditions, but may be 100–1,000 or
more for other viruses, because many particles are defective or fail to initiate infection.
5.6.1 Infectivity Assays
• Plaque assay (Dulbecco, 1952): serial ten-fold dilutions inoculated onto confluent monolayers;
after adsorption, cells are overlaid with semi-solid medium (agarose, methylcellulose,
carboxymethylcellulose) to restrict spread to neighbouring cells; after incubation, cells are fixed
and stained (crystal violet or neutral red) and plaques (zones of dead cells) are counted. Titre =
plaque-forming units (PFU)/ml = number of plaques ÷ (dilution × volume inoculated).
Example: 45 plaques at 10−6 dilution with 0.1 ml inoculum → 45 ÷ (10−6 × 0.1) = 4.5 × 108
PFU/ml.
• Focus-forming assay: for non-lytic or transforming viruses — foci detected by immunostaining
(FFU/ml).
• Pock assay: counting pocks on the CAM (poxviruses) — pock-forming units.
• End-point (quantal) assays: ten-fold dilutions inoculated into several replicate cultures, eggs or
animals; each unit is scored as infected or not, and the dilution infecting 50% of units is
calculated — TCID50 (tissue culture infectious dose 50%), EID50 (egg infectious dose), ELD50
(egg lethal dose), LD50 / ID50 (animal lethal/infectious dose), PD50 (protective dose, vaccine
potency). Calculated by the Reed–Muench (1938) or Spearman–Kärber method. As a rule of
thumb, 1 TCID50 ≈ 0.7 PFU (Poisson distribution).
Chapter 5 | Page 56



VMC 503: General Virology
• Multiplicity of infection (MOI): ratio of infectious units to cells in a culture (e.g. MOI 0.01 for
multistep growth, MOI 5–10 for one-step growth curves).
5.6.2 Worked Example: Reed–Muench Calculation of TCID50
Table 5.5: Example data (8 wells per dilution)
Dilution Infected wells Uninfected
wells
Cumulative
infected (↑)
Cumulative
uninfected (↓) % infected
10−4 8 0 20 0 100
10−5 7 1 12 1 92.3
10−6 4 4 5 5 50.0
10−7 1 7 1 12 7.7
10−8 0 8 0 20 0
• Cumulative infected values are added upwards from the highest dilution (because a unit infected
at a high dilution would also be infected at lower dilutions); cumulative uninfected values are
added downwards.
• Proportionate distance (PD) = (% infected at dilution just above 50% − 50) ÷ (% above 50% −
% below 50%). Here the 10−6 dilution gives exactly 50%, so PD = 0 and the 50% end-point is
10−6.0.
• If the inoculum per well was 0.1 ml, the titre = 106.0 TCID50 per 0.1 ml = 107.0 TCID50/ml.
• Where 50% falls between dilutions (e.g. 69% at 10−5, 30% at 10−6), PD = (69 − 50)/(69 − 30) =
0.49, and the end-point = 10−(5 + 0.49) = 10−5.49.
• Spearman–Kärber formula: log10 end-point dilution = x0 − d/2 + d(Σr/n), where x0 is the log of
the highest dilution at which all units are infected, d the log dilution interval, and Σr/n the sum of
proportions infected at and beyond x0.
5.6.3 Physical and Molecular Assays
• Haemagglutination (HA) titre: highest dilution of virus giving complete agglutination of RBC;
expressed as HA units (e.g. 4 HA units used in the HI test). For influenza, 1 HA unit ≈ 107
virions.
• Electron microscopic particle counts — mixing with latex beads of known concentration.
• Quantitative PCR (qPCR/RT-qPCR): genome copies/ml using a standard curve; digital PCR
gives absolute counts without a standard curve. Genome copies do not distinguish infectious from
non-infectious virus.
• Antigen quantification: ELISA (e.g. p24; FMDV 146S antigen by sucrose gradient — the
potency determinant of FMD vaccines), nanoparticle tracking analysis, flow virometry.
5.6.4 Virus Growth Curves
• One-step growth curve (Ellis and Delbrück, 1939, for phages): cells infected at high MOI so
that all are infected synchronously; samples taken at intervals and titrated. Phases: eclipse period
(no infectious virus detectable inside cells — uncoating and synthesis), latent period (no virus
released extracellularly), rise (maturation/release) period and plateau; the burst size (yield per
cell) varies from ~10 to 10,000 PFU per cell (e.g. picornaviruses ~103–104).
• Multistep growth curves (low MOI) reflect several rounds of infection and are used to compare
fitness and attenuation of vaccine strains.
Chapter 5 | Page 57



VMC 503: General Virology
LECTURE 8: CONCENTRATION AND PURIFICATION OF VIRUSES
5.7 Why Purify Viruses?
Crude virus harvests (culture fluid, allantoic fluid, tissue homogenates) contain host cell debris, host
proteins and nucleic acids, serum components and medium. Purification yields preparations suitable
for structural and biochemical studies (electron microscopy, cryo-EM, protein and genome
analysis), antigens for serological tests (ELISA, AGID, CFT), production of antisera and
monoclonal antibodies, and vaccines of high purity and safety — for example, removal of non-
structural proteins from FMD vaccines so that vaccinated animals can be distinguished from infected
ones (DIVA), and removal of host DNA and serum proteins to avoid adverse reactions. A purification
scheme aims to maximise yield and retain infectivity while achieving the required purity, and usually
combines several steps.
5.8 Release, Clarification and Concentration
• Release of cell-associated virus: repeated freeze–thawing, sonication or homogenisation; mild
detergent treatment where intact enveloped particles are not required.
• Clarification: low-speed centrifugation (about 3,000–10,000 × g for 20–30 minutes) or depth
and membrane filtration (0.45 μm) to remove cells and debris.
• Nuclease treatment (e.g. Benzonase) to digest host nucleic acids.
Table 5.6: Methods of virus concentration
Method Principle Comments and examples
Polyethylene glycol
(PEG) precipitation

M NaCl excludes water and precipitates
virions, which are then pelleted at low

used for concentrating FMDV antigen
and many other viruses

5.9 Purification by Centrifugation
• Differential (rate-zonal by steps) centrifugation: alternate low- and high-speed spins remove
debris and then pellet virus; simple but gives only partial purity.
• Rate-zonal density gradient centrifugation: the sample is layered on a preformed gradient
(commonly 10–40% sucrose) and centrifuged for a limited time; particles separate according to
their size, shape and density (sedimentation coefficient) and must be collected before reaching
equilibrium. This is how the intact 146S particle of FMDV is separated from the 75S empty
capsids and 12S pentamers.
• Isopycnic (equilibrium) density gradient centrifugation: centrifugation is continued until
each particle reaches the point where its buoyant density equals that of the gradient. Gradients of
caesium chloride (CsCl), potassium tartrate, sodium bromide or iodixanol are used. Non-
enveloped viruses band at about 1.30–1.45 g/ml and enveloped viruses at about 1.15–1.25 g/ml,
so the method also characterises the virus. CsCl may damage enveloped viruses, for which
sucrose or iodixanol is preferred.
• Collection of bands: visible opalescent bands are collected by side puncture, by upward
displacement or by fractionation from the top, then dialysed or buffer-exchanged to remove
gradient material.
Table 5.7: Other purification methods
Method Principle and applications
Gel-filtration (size-exclusion)
chromatography
Large virions are excluded from the pores of the gel and elute first, ahead
of smaller host proteins; gentle and scalable

modern vaccine manufacture
Affinity chromatography
Immobilised ligands — monoclonal antibodies, heparin, lectins,
sulphated cellulose or receptor molecules — bind the virus specifically;
highly selective
Immunoprecipitation and

VMC 503: General Virology
Method Principle and applications
Enzyme treatment Trypsin, ribonuclease or deoxyribonuclease digest host proteins and
nucleic acids that resist removal by physical means
5.9.1 Assessing Purity and Yield
• Protein estimation (Lowry, Bradford, bicinchoninic acid or ultraviolet absorbance at 280 nm)
and nucleic acid estimation (absorbance at 260 nm); the A260/A280 ratio indicates the relative
nucleic acid content.
• SDS–PAGE and western blotting show the expected structural proteins and the absence of host
proteins.
• Electron microscopy confirms morphology and the absence of debris; cryo-EM requires high
purity.
• Specific infectivity — infectivity per milligram of protein — should rise at each step; recovery
(yield) is calculated as the percentage of the starting infectivity retained.
• Residual host DNA and protein are measured for vaccine batches against pharmacopoeial
limits; sterility and mycoplasma testing are mandatory.

INDIA FOCUS: VIRUS CULTIVATION AND VACCINE PRODUCTION IN INDIA
▪ Historic contribution: the goat-adapted rinderpest vaccine developed by J.T. Edwards at
Mukteswar (1927) and the later lapinised and tissue culture rinderpest vaccines produced at ICAR–
IVRI illustrate the progression from animal to cell culture systems in India.
▪ Foot-and-mouth disease: inactivated FMD vaccines for the National Animal Disease Control
Programme are produced by Indian manufacturers in large-scale BHK-21 suspension cultures, with
binary ethylenimine inactivation, concentration and purification of the 146S antigen, and blending as
trivalent (serotypes O, A and Asia 1) vaccine. Vaccine strains are selected on the basis of antigenic and
Chapter 5 | Page 60



VMC 503: General Virology
genetic matching carried out at ICAR–National Institute on Foot-and-Mouth Disease (NIFMD),
Bhubaneswar.
▪ Small ruminant and capripox vaccines: the PPR vaccine (Sungri/96 strain) and sheeppox, goatpox
and lumpy skin disease vaccines are produced in Vero cells by ICAR institutes and Indian companies;
the indigenous LSD vaccine Lumpi-ProVacInd was developed by ICAR–NRCE, Hisar with ICAR–
IVRI.
▪ Poultry vaccines: Newcastle disease (LaSota, Mukteswar, R2B), infectious bronchitis and fowlpox
vaccines are produced in SPF embryonated eggs, and Marek’s disease vaccines in chicken embryo
fibroblasts; SPF egg production units operate at several institutes and private companies.
▪ Regulation and biosafety: vaccine production and quality control follow Indian Pharmacopoeia and
Department of Animal Husbandry and Dairying requirements, with batch testing by the Indian
Veterinary Biological Standardisation Division, IVRI, Izatnagar. Work with exotic and high-risk
viruses (HPAI, ASFV, Nipah) is carried out under high containment at ICAR–NIHSAD, Bhopal and
ICMR–NIV, Pune. Animal experiments require approval of the Institutional Animal Ethics
Committee under CCSEA rules, and the 3Rs are applied in teaching and research.
▪ Diagnostic laboratories in state and university veterinary colleges commonly maintain Vero, BHK-21,
MDBK and CEF cultures for isolation of PPRV, capripoxviruses, BoHV-1, canine distemper virus and
canine parvovirus, with egg inoculation for NDV and IBV.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Preparation and sterilisation of glassware and media; preparation of cell culture medium with serum and
antibiotics.
▪ Preparation of a primary chicken embryo fibroblast culture by trypsinisation; subculture and
cryopreservation of a continuous cell line.
▪ Candling and inoculation of embryonated eggs by the allantoic and chorioallantoic membrane routes;
harvesting of allantoic fluid and demonstration of pocks.
▪ Observation and recording of cytopathic effects; demonstration of haemadsorption and
haemagglutination.
▪ Titration of a virus by plaque assay and by the end-point method, with calculation of PFU/ml and
TCID50 by the Reed–Muench and Spearman–Kärber methods.
▪ Concentration of a virus by PEG precipitation and demonstration of sucrose density gradient
centrifugation.
5.11 Summary
• Viruses require living host systems for cultivation: laboratory animals, embryonated eggs, organ
cultures and cell cultures.
• Laboratory animals remain necessary for pathogenesis studies, pathotyping (ICPI, IVPI),
potency testing and antiserum production, under the 3Rs and CCSEA regulation.
• Embryonated SPF eggs are inoculated by the chorioallantoic membrane, allantoic, amniotic, yolk
sac, intravenous or intracerebral routes, with characteristic results such as pocks,
haemagglutinating fluid and dwarfed embryos.
• Cell cultures are primary, diploid or continuous; Vero, BHK-21, MDBK, MDCK, MA-104,
CRFK and CEF are widely used in veterinary virology, with defined media, serum and strict
quality control for mycoplasma and adventitious agents.
• Virus growth is recognised by cytopathic effects (rounding, syncytia, inclusion bodies),
haemadsorption, haemagglutination, interference, immunostaining and molecular tests.
Chapter 5 | Page 61



VMC 503: General Virology
• Infectivity is measured by plaque assay (PFU/ml) or end-point titration (TCID50, EID50, LD50)
calculated by Reed–Muench or Spearman–Kärber; physical assays include haemagglutination
titre, particle counts and quantitative PCR.
• Purification combines clarification, concentration (PEG or salt precipitation, ultrafiltration,
adsorption) and separation by rate-zonal or isopycnic density gradient centrifugation, with
chromatography for high-purity preparations; purity is judged by protein and nucleic acid assays,
SDS–PAGE, electron microscopy and specific infectivity.
• These methods underpin Indian veterinary vaccine production — FMD vaccine in BHK-21 cells,
PPR and capripox vaccines in Vero cells, and poultry vaccines in SPF eggs and chicken embryo
fibroblasts.
5.12 Glossary
Table 5.9: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
SPF eggs Eggs from flocks free of specified pathogens and their antibodies.
Chorioallantoic membrane Vascular membrane lining the shell of the embryonated egg, used for pox and
herpesvirus growth.
Pock Focal white lesion produced on the chorioallantoic membrane by poxviruses.
Primary cell culture Culture prepared directly from tissue, with a limited lifespan.
Hayflick limit Finite number of divisions (about 50) that a diploid cell strain can undergo.
Continuous cell line Immortalised, indefinitely subculturable cell line, often aneuploid.
Cytopathic effect Visible degenerative change in cells caused by virus replication.
Syncytium Multinucleated giant cell formed by virus-induced cell fusion.
Inclusion body Intranuclear or intracytoplasmic mass of viral material or altered host
structures.
Haemadsorption Adherence of erythrocytes to cells expressing viral haemagglutinin.
Plaque-forming unit One infectious particle able to produce a plaque in a monolayer.
TCID50 Dose of virus infecting 50% of inoculated cell cultures.
EID50 Dose of virus infecting 50% of inoculated embryonated eggs.
Multiplicity of infection Ratio of infectious units to cells in a culture.
Eclipse period Interval after entry when no infectious virus can be recovered from cells.
Burst size Number of infectious particles released per infected cell.
Isopycnic centrifugation Centrifugation to equilibrium, where particles band at their own buoyant
density.
146S particle Intact FMDV virion, the immunogenic component of FMD vaccines.

UNIT I — GENERAL PROPERTIES OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 6
Laboratory Diagnosis of Viral Infections
Lectures 9 and 10
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Explain the principles of selection, collection, preservation and transport of clinical specimens for viral
diagnosis, with attention to biosafety.
▪ Describe methods for direct detection of viruses, viral antigens and virus-induced lesions, and for virus
isolation.
▪ Describe nucleic acid-based diagnostic methods, including conventional and real-time PCR, isothermal
amplification, sequencing and metagenomics.
▪ Describe serological tests and interpret serological results, including seroconversion, maternal
antibodies and DIVA.
▪ Evaluate diagnostic tests using sensitivity, specificity and predictive values, and outline quality
assurance and the Indian veterinary diagnostic network.
LECTURE 9: SPECIMENS, DIRECT DETECTION AND VIRUS ISOLATION
6.1 Introduction
Laboratory diagnosis of viral infections is needed to confirm a clinical diagnosis, to guide treatment
and control (isolation, culling, vaccination), for surveillance and disease-freedom certification in
trade, to evaluate vaccine efficacy, and to detect emerging and zoonotic viruses early. Diagnostic
approaches fall into two broad groups: direct methods, which demonstrate the virus, its antigens or its
nucleic acid in the specimen, and indirect methods, which demonstrate the host’s antibody response.
No single test is perfect; the choice depends on the stage of infection, the purpose of testing, the
specimen available and the resources of the laboratory.
Chapter 6 | Page 66



VMC 503: General Virology
6.2 Specimens: Selection, Collection and Transport
6.2.1 General Principles
• Timing: virus shedding is usually greatest early in infection, often before or at the onset of
clinical signs. Specimens for virus detection should therefore be collected as early as possible,
preferably from acutely ill, untreated animals — not from animals in the terminal or recovery
phase.
• Site: collect from the site of virus replication or the target organ suggested by the clinical signs
(e.g. nasal and ocular swabs in respiratory disease, vesicular fluid and epithelium in vesicular
disease, faeces in enteric disease, brain in neurological disease, blood in viraemic and systemic
disease).
• Multiple animals: in herd and flock problems, sample several affected animals (and in-
contacts) because individual animals may be at different stages of infection.
• Quantity and labelling: adequate amount; each container clearly labelled with animal identity,
specimen type and date; accompanied by a case history form (species, age, number affected and
dead, clinical signs, vaccination history, treatment, suspected disease).
• Aseptic collection and use of sterile, leak-proof containers; separate instruments for each
animal and organ to avoid cross-contamination (particularly important for PCR).
• Post-mortem specimens should be collected from fresh carcasses, as soon as possible after
death, both fresh/chilled (for isolation and PCR) and in 10% neutral buffered formalin (for
histopathology and immunohistochemistry).

6.2.2 Preservation and Transport
• Viral transport medium (VTM): buffered salt solution or cell culture medium containing
protein (bovine serum albumin, gelatin or serum) to stabilise the virus, antibiotics and
antifungals to control contaminants, and phenol red; swabs should have synthetic (Dacron,
nylon flocked) tips and plastic shafts — cotton and wooden shafts may inhibit viruses and PCR.
For FMD, 50% glycerol–phosphate buffer (pH 7.2–7.6) is used for epithelial samples.
• Temperature: keep specimens chilled at 4 °C and deliver within 24–48 hours; if longer delays
are expected, freeze at −70 °C or below (not at −20 °C, which damages many viruses) and ship on
dry ice. Avoid repeated freeze–thawing. Serum can be stored at −20 °C; formalin-fixed tissues at
room temperature.
• Inactivating media (containing chaotropic salts such as guanidinium thiocyanate) preserve
nucleic acid for PCR and render samples non-infectious, but preclude virus isolation.
• Filter paper (FTA) cards and dried blood spots allow ambient-temperature transport of
nucleic acid from remote areas.
• Packaging and dispatch: specimens are sent in approved leak-proof packaging with absorbent
material, following national and international regulations for the transport of diagnostic
specimens, and after prior contact with the receiving laboratory.
• Laboratory biosafety: specimens are opened in biosafety cabinets by trained staff using
personal protective equipment, in facilities of the containment level appropriate to the suspected
agent.
Chapter 6 | Page 68



VMC 503: General Virology
6.3 Direct Detection Methods
6.3.1 Electron Microscopy
• Negative-stain EM of faeces, vesicular fluid, scab suspensions or culture fluid gives rapid
(within an hour) morphological identification to family level — a "catch-all" method that does
not require prior suspicion of a specific virus (e.g. identification of rotaviruses, coronaviruses,
parvoviruses and caliciviruses in faeces; poxviruses in scabs — orthopox brick-shaped vs
parapox ovoid with criss-cross pattern).
• Requires at least 105–106 particles/ml; sensitivity is increased by immunoelectron microscopy
(IEM) or ultracentrifugation; equipment is expensive and skilled operators are needed.
6.3.2 Histopathology and Cytology
• Characteristic lesions and inclusion bodies in H&E-stained sections or impression smears
suggest virus groups: intracytoplasmic — Negri bodies (rabies), Bollinger bodies (fowlpox),
Guarnieri bodies (vaccinia), Borrel bodies; intranuclear — Cowdry type A (herpesviruses,
e.g. ILT, BoHV-1), adenoviruses (IBH), parvoviruses (CPV in crypt epithelium); both —
morbilliviruses (CDV, PPRV), with syncytia.
• Cytology: Seller’s stain for Negri bodies in brain smears (now largely replaced by FAT);
Tzanck-type smears for herpesvirus syncytia; conjunctival smears for CDV inclusions.

6.4 Virus Isolation
• Virus isolation is the traditional "gold standard" because it proves the presence of infectious
virus and provides the isolate for characterisation (serotyping, sequencing, antigenic matching
for vaccines, antiviral testing, pathogenicity tests) and vaccine development.
• Systems: cell cultures (most common), embryonated SPF eggs, and occasionally laboratory
animals (Chapter 5); blind passages (2–3) may be needed to reveal CPE.
• Identification of isolates: by CPE type, virus neutralisation, HI (haemagglutinating viruses),
immunofluorescence or immunoperoxidase staining of infected cells, antigen ELISA, EM and
increasingly PCR and sequencing.
• Advantages: detects unexpected or novel viruses; demonstrates viability; supplies material for
further study.
• Limitations: slow (days to weeks), labour-intensive, needs viable virus and a good cold chain,
and some viruses grow poorly or not at all in available culture systems; it is progressively being
supplemented by molecular methods.
LECTURE 10: MOLECULAR AND SEROLOGICAL DIAGNOSIS, TEST EVALUATION
6.5 Nucleic Acid-Based Detection
Detection of the viral genome is now the mainstay of viral diagnosis because it is rapid, highly
sensitive and specific, can be quantitative, works on non-viable virus and on viruses that do not grow
in culture, and provides sequence for typing and epidemiology. Its main limitations are the need for
equipment, trained staff and strict measures against cross-contamination, and the fact that it detects
nucleic acid whether or not the virus is infectious.

• Preventing contamination: physically separated areas for reagent preparation, sample addition
and amplification; unidirectional workflow; dedicated equipment and pipettes with filter tips;
positive, negative and internal (housekeeping gene) controls; uracil-N-glycosylase (UNG)
systems.
• Interpretation: a positive result confirms the presence of viral nucleic acid but not necessarily
infectious virus or disease causation; Ct values give an indication of viral load; primer/probe
mismatches with variant strains can cause false negatives, so assays must be updated.
6.6 Serological (Antibody) Detection
Serology demonstrates the host antibody response and is used for diagnosis (especially by paired
sera), serosurveillance, vaccine monitoring, certification of freedom and screening. Because
antibodies appear only after 1–2 weeks and persist for months to years, a single positive result usually
Chapter 6 | Page 71



VMC 503: General Virology
indicates exposure (past infection or vaccination) rather than current disease. Diagnosis of active
infection by serology requires a rising titre or IgM detection.

6.6.1 Interpretation of Serological Results
• Paired (acute and convalescent) sera: a four-fold or greater rise in antibody titre between
samples taken 2–4 weeks apart confirms recent/active infection; a single high titre is difficult to
interpret.
• IgM antibodies indicate recent infection (they appear first and are short-lived); IgG indicates
past exposure or vaccination.
• Maternal (colostral) antibodies in young animals reflect the dam’s status, not active infection,
and interfere with vaccination scheduling and with serodiagnosis in neonates.
• DIVA (Differentiating Infected from Vaccinated Animals): tests that detect antibodies to viral
proteins absent from the vaccine (e.g. non-structural proteins (NSP) of FMDV in animals
vaccinated with purified antigen; glycoprotein E of gE-deleted BoHV-1 and pseudorabies marker
vaccines) allow serosurveillance in vaccinated populations — essential for control-and-
eradication programmes.
• Serosurveillance and freedom certification: herd- and population-level serology (with defined
sensitivity, specificity and sample sizes) supports claims of freedom for trade (e.g. FMD, CSF,
avian influenza).
• Limitations: cross-reactions between related viruses; vaccination confounds interpretation; slow
antibody rise means serology is not useful for early diagnosis.
Chapter 6 | Page 72



VMC 503: General Virology
6.7 Evaluation and Quality of Diagnostic Tests
• Diagnostic sensitivity (Se): proportion of truly infected animals that test positive = TP / (TP +
FN). A highly sensitive test has few false negatives and is used for screening and to rule out
disease.
• Diagnostic specificity (Sp): proportion of truly uninfected animals that test negative = TN / (TN
+ FP). A highly specific test has few false positives and is used for confirmation and to rule in
disease.
• Predictive values: positive predictive value (PPV) = TP / (TP + FP) and negative predictive
value (NPV) = TN / (TN + FN); these depend on the prevalence of infection in the tested
population — in low-prevalence populations even a specific test gives many false positives, so
confirmatory testing is essential.
• Analytical sensitivity (limit of detection) and analytical specificity (freedom from cross-
reaction) describe the assay itself.
• Accuracy and precision: agreement with the true value and reproducibility (repeatability within
a run and between runs); assessed against a reference ("gold") standard or by latent-class
analysis where no perfect standard exists; agreement measured by kappa.
• Quality assurance: standard operating procedures, validated methods (WOAH validation
pathway), positive/negative and internal controls, reference reagents, participation in
proficiency testing / ring trials, laboratory accreditation (ISO/IEC 17025), and confirmation
of screening-positive results by a reference laboratory.
• Test choice by purpose: a sensitive screening test (e.g. antigen or antibody ELISA, real-time
PCR) followed by a specific confirmatory test (e.g. virus neutralisation, sequencing) is the usual
strategy.
Table 6.5: Two-by-two table for test evaluation
Disease present Disease absent Total
Test positive True positive (TP) False positive (FP) TP + FP
Test negative False negative (FN) True negative (TN) FN + TN
Total TP + FN FP + TN N
Sensitivity = TP/(TP + FN); Specificity = TN/(TN + FP); PPV = TP/(TP + FP); NPV = TN/(TN +
FN); Youden’s index = Se + Sp − 1.
INDIA FOCUS: VIRAL DIAGNOSTIC NETWORK AND CAPABILITY IN INDIA
▪ Animal health network: viral diagnosis in India is carried out by state Animal Disease Diagnostic
Laboratories, veterinary college laboratories, and ICAR institutes. The All India Network
Programme on Bluetongue, the PPR and FMD control programme laboratories, and the ICAR–
National Institute of Veterinary Epidemiology and Disease Informatics (NIVEDI), Bengaluru
(with its NADRES v2 forecasting system) coordinate surveillance.
▪ Reference laboratories: ICAR–National Institute on Foot-and-Mouth Disease (NIFMD),
Bhubaneswar (FMD, including sandwich ELISA serotyping, NSP c-ELISA for DIVA and VP1
sequencing); ICAR–Indian Veterinary Research Institute (IVRI), Izatnagar (PPR, capripox, rabies,
many viruses and vaccine testing); ICAR–National Institute of High Security Animal Diseases
(NIHSAD), Bhopal — the WOAH reference laboratory for avian influenza and the designated
laboratory for exotic and high-consequence viruses (ASF, Nipah), operating high-containment (BSL-3
and BSL-4) facilities.
▪ Public health and zoonoses: ICMR–National Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune and its regional
units, the network of Virus Research and Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDLs) established across the
Chapter 6 | Page 73



VMC 503: General Virology
country, and the National Centre for Disease Control support diagnosis of rabies, Japanese encephalitis,
Nipah, KFD, CCHF, avian influenza and COVID-19 under a One Health approach.
▪ Methods in routine use: real-time RT-PCR and antigen ELISA for FMD, PPR, LSD, CSF and avian
influenza; the fluorescent antibody test and molecular tests for rabies at designated centres; HI for
NDV and avian influenza; c-ELISA for FMD serosurveillance (DIVA); and increasingly whole-
genome sequencing for outbreak investigation of ASF, LSD and avian influenza.
▪ Field diagnosis: lateral-flow pen-side kits (e.g. for canine parvovirus and, in outbreaks, FMD and PPR)
and LAMP assays developed by Indian institutes support rapid diagnosis in the field, though positives
are confirmed at reference laboratories.
▪ Biosafety and regulation follow national guidelines; work with exotic transboundary agents is
restricted to notified high-containment laboratories, and import of diagnostic specimens and reference
material is regulated.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Selection, collection, labelling, preservation and packaging of specimens for viral diagnosis
(respiratory, enteric, vesicular, neurological and abortion cases); preparation of viral transport medium.
▪ Demonstration of negative-stain electron microscopy and of inclusion bodies in stained sections (Negri,
Bollinger, Cowdry type A).
▪ Fluorescent antibody test for rabies (demonstration) and antigen-capture ELISA (e.g. rotavirus or
FMD).
▪ Haemagglutination and haemagglutination-inhibition tests for Newcastle disease virus.
▪ Setting up and interpreting RT-PCR and real-time PCR; reading agarose gels and amplification plots;
contamination-control practices.
▪ Indirect and competitive ELISA for antibody detection; calculation of sensitivity, specificity and
predictive values from given data.
6.8 Summary
• Viral diagnosis uses direct methods (detecting the virus, its antigen or nucleic acid) and indirect
methods (detecting antibody); the choice depends on the stage of infection and the purpose.
• Specimens must be collected early from acutely ill animals, from the appropriate site, kept cold,
transported in viral transport medium with correct packaging and biosafety, and accompanied by
a full history.
• Direct detection includes electron microscopy, histopathology and inclusion bodies, and antigen
detection by FAT (gold standard for rabies), IHC, antigen-capture ELISA, lateral-flow tests,
AGID and haemagglutination.
• Virus isolation in cell cultures, eggs or animals proves the presence of infectious virus and
provides isolates for characterisation, but is slow and demanding.
• Nucleic acid methods — conventional and real-time PCR, multiplex and nested PCR, isothermal
amplification (LAMP, RPA), sequencing and metagenomics — are rapid, sensitive and specific
and are now central to diagnosis and surveillance, with strict contamination control.
• Serology (ELISA, virus neutralisation, HI, AGID, CFT) detects antibodies for diagnosis (paired
sera, IgM), serosurveillance, vaccine monitoring, freedom certification and DIVA.
• Tests are evaluated by sensitivity, specificity and predictive values (which depend on
prevalence), with quality assured by controls, reference reagents, proficiency testing and
accreditation.
Chapter 6 | Page 74



VMC 503: General Virology
• India has a network of state, ICAR and ICMR laboratories, with NIFMD, IVRI, NIHSAD,
NIVEDI and NIV/VRDLs as reference and high-containment centres for animal and zoonotic
viral diagnosis.
6.9 Glossary
Table 6.6: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Direct detection Demonstration of virus, antigen or nucleic acid in a specimen.
Indirect detection Demonstration of the host antibody response.
Viral transport medium Protein-containing, antibiotic-supplemented medium that preserves virus
during transport.
Inclusion body Intracellular mass of viral material or altered host structures.
Fluorescent antibody test Detection of antigen using fluorochrome-labelled antibody.
Immunohistochemistry Detection of antigen in fixed tissue with enzyme-labelled antibody.
Antigen-capture ELISA Sandwich ELISA in which antigen is trapped between two antibodies.
Lateral flow assay Rapid immunochromatographic pen-side test.
RT-PCR Reverse-transcription polymerase chain reaction for RNA viruses.
Real-time PCR PCR in which product accumulation is monitored by fluorescence (Ct value).
LAMP Loop-mediated isothermal amplification.
Metagenomics Sequencing of all nucleic acids in a sample to identify viruses.
Virus neutralisation test Serological test measuring antibody that blocks virus infectivity.
DIVA Differentiating infected from vaccinated animals.
Seroconversion Appearance of, or a rise in, specific antibody.
Sensitivity Proportion of truly infected animals that test positive.
Specificity Proportion of truly uninfected animals that test negative.
Predictive value Probability that a test result reflects the true status, given the prevalence.

LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Define subviral agents and distinguish viroids, satellites, hepatitis delta virus and prions from
conventional viruses.
▪ Describe the structure, classification, replication and pathogenesis of viroids.
▪ Explain the prion concept, the difference between PrPC and PrPSc, and the mechanism of prion
propagation.
▪ Describe the transmissible spongiform encephalopathies of animals and humans, their pathogenesis,
diagnosis and control.
▪ Explain the unusual resistance of prions to inactivation and the measures used for decontamination and
disease prevention, including India’s BSE status.
LECTURE 11: VIROIDS, SATELLITES AND PRIONS
7.1 Subviral Agents
Subviral agents are infectious entities that are simpler than viruses: they lack one or more of the
defining features of a virus, such as a protein coat or a genome that codes for proteins. They include
viroids (naked, non-coding infectious RNA), satellites (nucleic acids that depend on a helper virus),
hepatitis delta virus-like agents (ribozyme-containing satellite viruses), and prions (infectious
proteins without nucleic acid). Their discovery broadened the concept of infectious agents and revealed
new mechanisms of replication and disease.

7.2 Viroids
7.2.1 Discovery and Structure
• Discovered by Theodor O. Diener (1971) as the cause of potato spindle tuber disease; he
named the agent a viroid. Viroids are the smallest known infectious agents.
• Structure: a single-stranded, covalently closed circular RNA of 246–401 nucleotides (e.g.
coconut cadang-cadang viroid, 246 nt, among the smallest; potato spindle tuber viroid
(PSTVd), 359 nt), with extensive intramolecular base pairing giving a rod-like or quasi-rod-
like secondary structure (or branched structure in some); no protein coat, no lipid.
• Viroids do not code for any protein — they depend entirely on host enzymes for replication, and
their pathogenicity is determined by the RNA sequence and structure itself.
• Hosts: only higher plants (no animal viroids are known). Economically important in potato,
tomato, citrus, coconut, avocado, apple, hop, grapevine and chrysanthemum.
7.2.2 Classification
Table 7.2: Families of viroids

7.2.3 Replication and Pathogenesis
• Rolling-circle replication: the circular (+) RNA serves as template for synthesis of multimeric
(−) strands of concatenated units; these either serve directly as template for multimeric (+)
strands (asymmetric) or are first cleaved and circularised (symmetric); multimeric (+) strands are
then cleaved to unit length and ligated into mature circles.
• Pathogenesis: viroid RNA is processed by the plant’s RNA silencing machinery (Dicer-like
enzymes) into viroid-derived small RNAs (vd-sRNAs) that can guide silencing of host
mRNAs, altering expression of genes involved in development and defence; interactions of the
Chapter 7 | Page 80



VMC 503: General Virology
viroid structure with host proteins also contribute. Single nucleotide changes in the
pathogenicity domain can convert mild strains to severe strains.
• Symptoms: stunting, leaf distortion and epinasty, chlorosis, necrosis, bark scaling (citrus
exocortis), elongated misshapen tubers (PSTVd), reduced yield; many infections are latent.
• Transmission: mainly mechanical (contaminated tools, grafting, handling), vegetative
propagation (tubers, cuttings, budwood), and in some cases seed and pollen; occasionally by
aphids in mixed infection with viruses.
• Diagnosis: biological indexing on indicator plants (e.g. Etrog citron for CEVd, tomato for
PSTVd), return (bidirectional) polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (R-PAGE) exploiting
circularity, dot-blot/northern hybridisation with probes, and RT-PCR / RT-qPCR (routine in
certification).
• Control: use of viroid-free certified planting material, disinfection of tools (e.g. with sodium
hypochlorite), quarantine, eradication of infected plants; cross-protection has limited use.
7.3 Satellites and Hepatitis Delta Virus
• Satellites are subviral agents that depend on a helper virus for replication and/or encapsidation
but lack sequence similarity to the helper. They include satellite viruses (encode their own coat
protein, e.g. satellite tobacco necrosis virus), satellite nucleic acids (use the helper’s coat, e.g.
satellite RNAs of cucumber mosaic virus) and virophages (Chapter 2). Adeno-associated
viruses (AAV) (Parvoviridae, genus Dependoparvovirus) depend on adenovirus or herpesvirus
helpers for productive replication and are widely used as gene therapy vectors.
• Hepatitis delta virus (HDV) (realm Ribozyviria, family Kolmioviridae, genus Deltavirus) was
discovered by Mario Rizzetto (1977) as the "delta antigen" in hepatitis B patients. It has a ~1.7
kb circular, negative-sense RNA with extensive base pairing (viroid-like) and ribozymes,
replicates by a rolling-circle mechanism using host RNA polymerase II, and encodes a single
Chapter 7 | Page 81



VMC 503: General Virology
protein, the hepatitis delta antigen (HDAg), in small and large forms (RNA editing). HDV
requires the hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) of the helper HBV for its envelope and entry;
co-infection or superinfection causes the most severe form of viral hepatitis in humans. Related
HDV-like agents have recently been found in birds, snakes, fish, rodents and deer, suggesting
broader evolutionary origins.
7.4 Prions
7.4.1 History of the Prion Concept
Table 7.3: Milestones in prion research
Year Milestone
1732 Scrapie of sheep described in England (sheep "scraping" against fences)
1936 Cuillé and Chelle transmitted scrapie experimentally, showing it was infectious, with a long
incubation period
1957–1959 Kuru described among the Fore people of Papua New Guinea (Gajdusek and Zigas), linked to
ritual cannibalism; Hadlow (1959), a veterinary pathologist, noted its resemblance to scrapie
1966–1968 Gajdusek, Gibbs and colleagues transmitted kuru and Creutzfeldt–Jakob disease (CJD) to
chimpanzees (Gajdusek: Nobel Prize 1976)
1967
Tikvah Alper showed that the scrapie agent was highly resistant to UV and ionising radiation,
suggesting it lacked nucleic acid; J.S. Griffith proposed a "protein-only" mechanism of self-
replication
1982 Stanley Prusiner purified the scrapie agent and coined the term prion (proteinaceous
infectious particle) (Nobel Prize 1997)
1985–1986 The host PRNP gene encoding the prion protein was identified; bovine spongiform
encephalopathy (BSE) was first recognised in the UK (Wells et al., 1987)
1988–1996 Ban on feeding ruminant-derived protein to ruminants in the UK (1988); variant CJD (vCJD)
recognised in 1996 and linked to BSE
2010s–2020s RT-QuIC and PMCA seeding assays for sensitive detection; chronic wasting disease
(CWD) found in Europe (Norway, 2016); camel prion disease described in Algeria (2018)
7.4.2 The Prion Protein: PrPC and PrPSc
• The cellular prion protein (PrPC) is a normal host glycoprotein (~250 amino acids; mature form
~209 residues) encoded by the PRNP gene (on chromosome 20 in humans; chromosome 13 in
cattle and sheep). It is anchored to the outer surface of the plasma membrane by a
glycosylphosphatidylinositol (GPI) anchor, has up to two N-linked glycans, and is most
abundant in neurons, also in lymphoid tissue. Its functions are not fully understood (roles in
copper binding, synaptic function, myelin maintenance and cell signalling have been proposed);
PrP-knockout mice develop almost normally but are completely resistant to prion infection.
• The disease-associated prion protein (PrPSc, "scrapie isoform") has the same amino acid
sequence as PrPC but a different conformation: PrPC is rich in α-helices (~40% α-helix, little β-
sheet), whereas PrPSc is rich in β-sheet (~40–50% β-sheet).
• Consequences of the conformational change: PrPSc is insoluble in detergents, aggregates into
oligomers, fibrils and amyloid plaques, and is partially resistant to proteinase K — digestion
removes about 67 N-terminal amino acids, leaving a protease-resistant core (PrPres, PrP 27–30,
~27–30 kDa), which is the basis of diagnostic western blot and ELISA tests.
• Because the host protein is "self", prion infection induces no inflammation and no specific
immune response.
Chapter 7 | Page 82



VMC 503: General Virology
Table 7.4: Properties of PrPC and PrPSc
Property PrPC (normal) PrPSc (disease-associated)
Amino acid sequence Encoded by host PRNP Same as PrPC
Secondary structure Mostly α-helix Mostly β-sheet
Solubility in detergents Soluble Insoluble; aggregates
Proteinase K Completely digested Partially resistant (PrP 27–30 core)
Location Cell surface (GPI-anchored), recycled Accumulates in cells, extracellularly
and in plaques
Infectivity Not infectious Infectious; seeds conversion of PrPC
Half-life Short (hours) Long (days); stable
7.4.3 Mechanism of Prion Propagation
• Template-directed refolding: PrPSc binds PrPC and induces it to adopt the PrPSc
conformation; each newly converted molecule can in turn recruit more PrPC, producing an
exponential, self-propagating chain reaction without any nucleic acid.
• Seeded (nucleation–polymerisation) model: PrPSc exists as oligomeric seeds that grow by
recruiting PrPC into ordered β-sheet-rich fibrils; fragmentation of fibrils creates new seeds,
amplifying the process.
• Prion strains: different prion strains produce distinct incubation periods, lesion profiles and
glycoform patterns in the same host genotype; strain information is thought to be encoded in the
conformation of PrPSc rather than in nucleic acid (e.g. classical, H-type and L-type BSE;
classical and atypical/Nor98 scrapie).
• Species barrier: transmission between species is inefficient (long first-passage incubation, low
attack rate) because of differences in PrP sequence and in strain conformation; the barrier can be
crossed (BSE to humans as vCJD; BSE to cats as feline spongiform encephalopathy) and
adaptation occurs on serial passage.
• Host genetics: polymorphisms in PRNP strongly influence susceptibility — in sheep, codons
136 (A/V), 154 (R/H) and 171 (Q/R/H): ARR/ARR sheep are highly resistant to classical
scrapie and VRQ alleles confer highest susceptibility; in humans, codon 129 (M/V)
homozygosity (especially MM) predisposes to sporadic CJD, and almost all clinical vCJD cases
have been MM.
• Sporadic prion diseases may arise from spontaneous misfolding; familial forms from PRNP
mutations; acquired forms from exposure to infectious prions.
Chapter 7 | Page 83



VMC 503: General Virology
7.5 Transmissible Spongiform Encephalopathies (Prion Diseases)
7.5.1 General Features
• Long incubation periods (months to many years), followed by a progressive, invariably fatal
neurodegenerative disease.
• Lesions confined mainly to the CNS: spongiform change (vacuolation of neuronal perikarya
and neuropil), neuronal loss, astrocytosis (gliosis) and microglial activation, with or without
amyloid plaques; no inflammatory reaction and no detectable antibody response.
• Clinical signs reflect CNS dysfunction: behavioural change (apprehension, nervousness,
aggression), pruritus (scrapie), ataxia, hyperaesthesia, tremors, weight loss and eventual
recumbency.
• Transmissible by inoculation and, for some diseases, by ingestion or natural contact.

7.5.2 Pathogenesis
• After oral exposure, prions cross the intestinal epithelium through M cells over Peyer’s patches,
and replicate on follicular dendritic cells (FDCs) in gut-associated and other lymphoid tissues
(tonsil, spleen, lymph nodes) — prominent in scrapie, CWD and vCJD, minimal in BSE in cattle.
• Neuroinvasion occurs via the enteric nervous system and autonomic nerves — the vagus
nerve to the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus in the medulla oblongata (at the obex), and
splanchnic nerves to the thoracic spinal cord — then spreads throughout the brain.
• This explains why the obex region of the brainstem is the target tissue for BSE and scrapie
diagnosis, and why specified risk materials (SRM) such as brain, spinal cord, eyes, tonsils and
distal ileum are removed from the food chain.
Chapter 7 | Page 85



VMC 503: General Virology
• Neuronal damage results from accumulation of toxic PrPSc oligomers, disturbed protein
homeostasis (unfolded protein response), loss of synaptic function and neuronal death.
7.5.3 Diagnosis
• No serological test is possible (no antibody response); diagnosis relies on detecting PrPSc or its
effects, mostly post-mortem.
• Histopathology — spongiform lesions in characteristic brain areas (obex).
• Immunohistochemistry (IHC) for PrPSc in brain and lymphoid tissues (after pretreatment to
remove PrPC).
• Western blot (SAF immunoblot) and rapid tests (ELISA-based, lateral flow) detecting
protease-resistant PrPres in brainstem — used for large-scale active surveillance of cattle and
small ruminants at slaughter; western blot also discriminates classical, H- and L-type BSE and
scrapie strains by molecular mass and glycoform ratio.
• Seeding amplification assays: real-time quaking-induced conversion (RT-QuIC) and
protein misfolding cyclic amplification (PMCA) amplify minute amounts of PrPSc by using it
to seed conversion of recombinant or normal PrP — extremely sensitive; used for ante-mortem
diagnosis of CJD (CSF, nasal brushings) and CWD, and for research.
• Ante-mortem tests in animals: biopsy of third eyelid lymphoid tissue or recto-anal mucosa-
associated lymphoid tissue (RAMALT) for scrapie and CWD (IHC or RT-QuIC); tonsil biopsy
for vCJD in humans.
• Bioassay in conventional or transgenic mice (expressing bovine, ovine or cervid PrP) —
sensitive and used for strain typing, but takes months to years.
• Genotyping of the PRNP gene (sheep breeding programmes).
Chapter 7 | Page 86



VMC 503: General Virology
7.5.4 Resistance and Inactivation of Prions
• Prions are exceptionally resistant to procedures that inactivate conventional microorganisms:
formaldehyde and glutaraldehyde (actually stabilise infectivity), ethanol, ultraviolet and
ionising radiation, boiling, dry heat and standard autoclaving (121 °C for 15 min) are
ineffective; infectivity can survive in soil for years.
• Recommended methods (WHO and WOAH guidance): autoclaving at 134–138 °C for 18
minutes (porous-load cycle); sodium hydroxide (1–2 N) for 1 hour; sodium hypochlorite with
at least 20,000 ppm available chlorine for 1 hour; combinations (NaOH followed by
autoclaving) for maximum safety; incineration for carcasses and waste; alkaline hydrolysis of
carcasses.
• Rendering processes: 133 °C, 3 bar pressure for 20 minutes (particle size ≤ 50 mm) is required
for animal by-products to reduce infectivity.
• In laboratories, single-use instruments and dedicated equipment are used for suspected TSE
material.
7.5.5 Prevention and Control
• Feed bans: prohibition of feeding mammalian meat-and-bone meal to ruminants (UK 1988;
later extended across Europe and elsewhere) — the most important measure, which reversed the
BSE epidemic.
• Removal of specified risk materials (SRM) from carcasses at slaughter and their safe
destruction; restrictions on mechanically recovered meat.
• Surveillance: passive (reporting of clinical suspects — BSE and scrapie are notifiable) and
active testing of fallen stock and slaughtered animals; WOAH official BSE risk status
(negligible, controlled, undetermined) governs international trade in cattle and bovine products.
• Culling of affected animals and their cohorts (BSE) or of affected flocks (scrapie).
• Genetic selection for resistance: breeding programmes selecting ARR alleles to eliminate
classical scrapie (national scrapie plans in Europe).
• CWD control: restrictions on movement of live cervids and carcasses, surveillance, and
population management; no vaccine or treatment is available.
• Human health: exclusion of blood donors who lived in the UK during the BSE period in some
countries, leucodepletion of blood, single-use instruments and strict decontamination in
neurosurgery, and replacement of cadaveric pituitary hormones by recombinant products.
• No vaccine or effective treatment exists for any prion disease.
INDIA FOCUS: VIROIDS AND PRION DISEASES — THE INDIAN SITUATION
▪ BSE status: India has never reported a case of BSE and was officially recognised by WOAH as
having a "negligible BSE risk" status in 2010, which is maintained through surveillance, a ban on
feeding ruminant-derived meat-and-bone meal to ruminants, and import controls on animal products
and live animals. This status is important for India’s large exports of buffalo meat (carabeef).
▪ Low-risk factors: the Indian cattle and buffalo production system has historically relied on crop
residues, green fodder and oilseed cakes rather than rendered animal protein, and most states restrict
cattle slaughter — factors that reduce the risk of a feed-borne prion cycle; nevertheless, TSE
surveillance of neurological cases and fallen stock is required to sustain the WOAH status.
▪ Scrapie and other TSEs: India has no recent confirmed cases of classical scrapie, and chronic wasting
disease has not been reported in Indian deer; camel prion disease, reported from North Africa, is
relevant for countries with large dromedary populations such as Rajasthan, and awareness among
Chapter 7 | Page 87



VMC 503: General Virology
veterinarians of neurological wasting syndromes in camels is desirable.
▪ Human prion disease: sporadic CJD occurs in India at rates comparable to other countries and is
diagnosed at neurology centres (e.g. NIMHANS, Bengaluru); no variant CJD has been reported from
India.
▪ Viroids in Indian agriculture: viroids such as citrus exocortis viroid, hop stunt viroid and potato
spindle tuber viroid have been reported from Indian crops; the ICAR–Central Potato Research
Institute (CPRI), Shimla and certification schemes for citrus and other planting material use RT-PCR
and hybridisation to ensure viroid-free stock. Quarantine by the National Bureau of Plant Genetic
Resources (NBPGR) screens imported germplasm for viroids.
▪ Hepatitis delta virus occurs in a proportion of hepatitis B carriers in India; control of HBV by
vaccination (included in India’s Universal Immunization Programme) also prevents HDV infection.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Comparison chart of viruses, viroids, satellites and prions.
▪ Demonstration of return-PAGE or RT-PCR for detection of a viroid in plant tissue (collaboration with
plant pathology laboratory).
▪ Study of histopathological slides and photographs of spongiform encephalopathy; identification of the
obex region for brainstem sampling.
▪ Demonstration of brainstem sampling technique for TSE surveillance (using an obex spoon) and safe
handling of suspected TSE material.
▪ Discussion of decontamination procedures for prions (NaOH, hypochlorite, autoclaving at 134 °C).
7.6 Summary
• Subviral agents — viroids, satellites, HDV and prions — are simpler than viruses and lack a
protein coat, a coding genome or nucleic acid altogether.
• Viroids (Diener, 1971) are small (246–401 nt), circular, non-coding, rod-like RNAs that infect
only plants; Pospiviroidae replicate in the nucleus by an asymmetric rolling-circle mechanism
with host RNA polymerase II, and Avsunviroidae in chloroplasts by a symmetric mechanism
using hammerhead ribozymes; pathogenesis involves RNA silencing.
• Satellites depend on helper viruses; HDV (Rizzetto, 1977) has a viroid-like ribozyme-containing
RNA, encodes HDAg and requires HBsAg from hepatitis B virus.
• Prions (Prusiner, 1982) are infectious proteins: the α-helical host PrPC is converted by template-
directed misfolding into β-sheet-rich, aggregated, partially protease-resistant PrPSc; strains are
encoded by conformation, and host PRNP genotype determines susceptibility.
• Transmissible spongiform encephalopathies include scrapie, BSE, CWD, TME, FSE and camel
prion disease in animals, and kuru, CJD (sporadic, familial, iatrogenic, variant), GSS and FFI in
humans; they have long incubation periods, spongiform lesions, no inflammation or immune
response, and are always fatal.
• Oral prions replicate in lymphoid tissue and reach the brainstem via autonomic nerves; the obex is
the key diagnostic sample; diagnosis uses histopathology, IHC, western blot, rapid tests and
seeding assays (RT-QuIC, PMCA).
• Prions resist standard sterilisation; autoclaving at 134 °C for 18 minutes, 1–2 N NaOH, high-
concentration hypochlorite and incineration are required; control relies on feed bans, SRM
removal, surveillance, culling and breeding for resistance.
• India has never reported BSE and has held WOAH negligible BSE risk status since 2010.
Chapter 7 | Page 88



VMC 503: General Virology
7.7 Glossary
Table 7.6: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Subviral agent Infectious agent simpler than a virus (viroid, satellite, prion).
Viroid Small, circular, non-coding infectious RNA of plants.
Central conserved region Conserved sequence in the centre of Pospiviroidae viroids.
Hammerhead ribozyme Self-cleaving RNA structure in Avsunviroidae and some satellites.
Rolling-circle replication Synthesis of multimeric strands by copying a circular template.
Satellite Subviral agent requiring a helper virus.
Hepatitis delta virus Satellite-like human virus requiring HBsAg of hepatitis B virus.
Prion Proteinaceous infectious particle lacking nucleic acid.
PrPC Normal cellular prion protein.
PrPSc Disease-associated, misfolded prion protein.
PrP 27–30 Protease-resistant core of PrPSc detected in diagnostic tests.
Species barrier Reduced efficiency of prion transmission between species.
Spongiform change Vacuolation of neurons and neuropil in prion diseases.
Obex Region of the medulla oblongata sampled for TSE diagnosis.
Specified risk material Tissues likely to carry prions, removed from the food chain.
RT-QuIC Real-time quaking-induced conversion, a seeding assay for prions.
Meat-and-bone meal Rendered animal protein that spread BSE when fed to cattle.

VMC 503: General Virology
UNIT II — REPLICATION AND INTERACTIONS OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 8
Replication of DNA Viruses
Lectures 12 and 13
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Describe the general steps of the virus replication cycle — attachment, entry, uncoating, gene
expression, genome replication, assembly, maturation and release.
▪ Explain how viruses recognise receptors and enter cells, with veterinary examples.
▪ Describe the general strategies of DNA virus replication, including temporal regulation of gene
expression and the mechanisms used to initiate DNA synthesis.
▪ Describe the replication of herpesviruses, adenoviruses, papillomaviruses, parvoviruses, circoviruses,
poxviruses, asfarviruses and hepadnaviruses.
▪ Relate replication strategies to pathogenesis, tissue tropism, latency and antiviral and vaccine design.
LECTURE 12: THE VIRUS REPLICATION CYCLE AND GENERAL STRATEGIES OF
DNA VIRUSES
8.1 Introduction
Viruses replicate only inside living cells, using the host’s ribosomes, energy, nucleotides and many of
its enzymes. Although the details vary enormously, all viruses follow the same broad sequence of
events, first defined for bacteriophages and later for animal viruses: attachment → entry
(penetration) → uncoating → expression of viral genes → replication of the genome → assembly
→ maturation → release. The outcome of this cycle — how fast it proceeds, where it occurs in the
cell, whether the cell dies or survives, and whether the genome persists — underlies viral pathogenesis,
persistence, immune evasion and the design of antiviral drugs and vaccines. This chapter first describes
the general cycle, then the strategies of DNA viruses (Baltimore classes I, II and VII); RNA viruses are
described in Chapter 9.
Chapter 8 | Page 93



VMC 503: General Virology
8.2 Steps of the Replication Cycle
8.2.1 Attachment (Adsorption)
• Attachment is the specific binding of a viral attachment protein (capsid protein of naked
viruses; envelope glycoprotein of enveloped viruses) to a cellular receptor. Many viruses first
bind low-affinity attachment factors (e.g. heparan sulphate proteoglycans, sialic acids) and then
high-affinity receptors and co-receptors.
• Receptor distribution is a major determinant of host range and tissue tropism; a change of a
single amino acid in the attachment protein can alter host range (e.g. VP2 mutations allowing
canine parvovirus to bind the canine transferrin receptor).
• Attachment is temperature-independent, but later steps require energy; it is the target of
neutralising antibodies and some entry inhibitors.
Table 8.1: Examples of cellular receptors used by DNA viruses of veterinary importance

8.2.2 Entry (Penetration) and Uncoating
• Direct fusion at the plasma membrane (pH-independent) — many herpesviruses
(glycoproteins gB, gD, gH/gL), paramyxoviruses.
• Receptor-mediated endocytosis — clathrin-mediated (adenoviruses, parvoviruses, ASFV,
influenza), caveolin/lipid-raft-mediated (polyomaviruses), macropinocytosis (poxviruses,
ASFV, some herpesviruses). Enveloped viruses fuse with the endosomal membrane when low
pH triggers conformational change in fusion proteins; naked viruses lyse or form pores in the
endosomal membrane (adenovirus protein VI; parvovirus VP1 phospholipase A2 domain).
• Uncoating is the release of the genome from the capsid, often stepwise: DNA virus capsids are
transported along microtubules by dynein to the nuclear pore complex (herpes, adeno), where
the genome is released into the nucleus; small parvoviruses may enter the nucleus intact; poxvirus
cores release DNA in the cytoplasm after early transcription.
• The eclipse period begins with uncoating: the infecting virion has lost its identity and infectious
virus cannot be recovered from the cell.
8.2.3 Gene Expression, Genome Replication, Assembly and Release
• Gene expression is usually temporally regulated: immediate-early/early genes encode
regulatory proteins and enzymes for genome replication and for manipulation of the host (e.g.
forcing the cell into S phase, blocking apoptosis and interferon); late genes, expressed after DNA
replication begins, encode structural proteins.
• Genome replication uses host DNA polymerases (small DNA viruses) or virus-encoded
polymerases (large DNA viruses); it occurs in the nucleus except for poxviruses and ASFV
(cytoplasmic "virus factories").
• Assembly — capsid proteins self-assemble (sometimes with scaffolding proteins) into
procapsids into which the genome is packaged by an ATP-driven packaging motor (terminase)
(herpes, adeno) or around which the capsid condenses.
• Maturation — proteolytic cleavage by viral proteases (adenovirus, retroviruses), envelopment
and glycoprotein processing.
• Release — cell lysis (adeno-, parvoviruses), budding through nuclear and cytoplasmic
membranes (herpesviruses), exocytosis and actin-driven projection into neighbouring cells
(poxviruses), or cell-to-cell spread that avoids antibodies (herpesviruses).
Chapter 8 | Page 95



VMC 503: General Virology
