• Small DNA viruses (parvo-, circo-, papilloma-, polyomaviruses) encode few proteins and
depend on host DNA polymerases; they therefore need cells that are dividing or must drive
resting cells into S phase (papilloma E6/E7, polyoma large T antigen). This explains their
tropism for proliferating tissues and their oncogenic potential.
• Large DNA viruses (herpes-, adeno-, pox-, asfarviruses) encode their own DNA polymerase
and many enzymes of nucleotide metabolism (thymidine kinase, ribonucleotide reductase,
dUTPase), so they can replicate in non-dividing cells; these virus-specific enzymes are the
targets of antiviral drugs such as aciclovir.
• Temporal cascade: in herpesviruses, immediate-early (α) proteins are regulatory, early (β)
proteins are enzymes for DNA synthesis, and late (γ) proteins are structural. Adenoviruses have
E1A/E1B and other early regions and a major late transcription unit; poxviruses have early,
intermediate and late classes, each with its own promoters and transcription factors.
Chapter 8 | Page 96



VMC 503: General Virology
LECTURE 13: REPLICATION OF INDIVIDUAL DNA VIRUS FAMILIES
8.4 Herpesviruses
Herpesviruses (e.g. bovine herpesvirus 1, pseudorabies virus, equine herpesvirus 1, Marek’s
disease virus, infectious laryngotracheitis virus) have a large linear dsDNA genome, an icosahedral
capsid, a tegument and an envelope. They are the classic example of temporally regulated gene
expression and of latency.
1. Attachment and entry: glycoprotein C (or B) binds heparan sulphate; glycoprotein D binds a
specific receptor (nectin-1, HVEM); the fusion machinery gB and gH/gL then fuses the envelope
with the plasma membrane (or endosomal membrane), releasing the capsid and tegument into the
cytoplasm.
2. Transport and uncoating: tegument proteins are released — some shut off host protein
synthesis (vhs) and others (VP16) travel to the nucleus; the capsid moves along microtubules to a
nuclear pore and injects the DNA into the nucleus, where it circularises.
3. Immediate-early (α) transcription: VP16, with host factors, activates α genes; the α proteins
(e.g. ICP4, ICP0 in herpes simplex virus; homologues in animal herpesviruses) regulate the
cascade and counteract host defences.
4. Early (β) proteins: DNA polymerase, helicase–primase, thymidine kinase, ribonucleate
reductase — the enzymes of DNA synthesis and the targets of aciclovir (which is phosphorylated
by viral thymidine kinase and then blocks the viral DNA polymerase).
5. DNA replication: begins at origins (oriL, oriS) and switches to a rolling-circle mechanism
producing long head-to-tail concatemers.
6. Late (γ) proteins: capsid, tegument and envelope glycoproteins; capsids assemble in the nucleus
around a scaffold, and unit-length genomes are cut from concatemers and packaged by the
terminase motor.
7. Envelopment and release: the capsid buds through the inner nuclear membrane (primary
envelopment), the primary envelope fuses with the outer nuclear membrane (de-envelopment),
and the capsid acquires tegument and its final envelope by budding into trans-Golgi vesicles;
virions are released by exocytosis or spread directly cell to cell.
8. Latency: in neurons (alphaherpesviruses — trigeminal and sacral ganglia) or lymphocytes
(gamma- and betaherpesviruses), the genome persists as a circular episome with only latency-
associated transcripts expressed; reactivation follows stress, corticosteroids, transport or
parturition, and leads to recurrent shedding — the basis of the carrier state in IBR, pseudorabies
and Marek’s disease.
Chapter 8 | Page 97



VMC 503: General Virology
8.5 Adenoviruses
• Entry: the fibre knob binds CAR (or sialic acid/CD46 in some types) and the penton base
engages αv integrins, triggering clathrin-mediated endocytosis; protein VI lyses the endosome
and the partly disassembled capsid docks at the nuclear pore, delivering the DNA into the
nucleus.
• Early transcription: E1A is the first gene expressed; it drives resting cells into S phase by
binding retinoblastoma protein (Rb) and releasing E2F, and transactivates the other early
regions. E1B blocks p53-mediated apoptosis. E2 encodes the DNA polymerase, preterminal
protein (pTP) and DNA-binding protein; E3 proteins counter host immunity (downregulation
of MHC class I, inhibition of TNF); E4 assists mRNA transport and shut-off. VA RNAs
(transcribed by RNA polymerase III) block the interferon-induced PKR response.
• DNA replication: a protein-primed mechanism — the terminal protein provides a hydroxyl
group (on a serine residue) from which the viral DNA polymerase starts synthesis at each end,
with strand displacement; the displaced strand forms a panhandle and is copied.
• Late phase: the major late promoter produces a single long transcript processed by alternative
splicing into five families of mRNAs encoding structural proteins; host protein synthesis is shut
off.
• Assembly and release: capsids assemble in the nucleus, DNA is packaged, the adenovirus
protease cleaves precursor proteins to mature the virion, and progeny are released by cell lysis,
often leaving basophilic intranuclear inclusion bodies (seen in inclusion body hepatitis of
poultry and infectious canine hepatitis).
• Applications: replication-defective (E1-deleted) adenoviruses are widely used as vaccine
vectors (including veterinary rabies and human COVID-19 vaccines) and in gene therapy.
Chapter 8 | Page 98



VMC 503: General Virology
8.6 Papillomaviruses and Polyomaviruses
• Papillomaviruses (bovine papillomaviruses, equine sarcoid-associated BPV-1/2) infect basal
keratinocytes through micro-abrasions. The genome is maintained as a low-copy episome in
basal cells; the viral life cycle is tied to keratinocyte differentiation — early proteins (E1
helicase, E2 regulator, E5, E6, E7) are expressed in the basal and parabasal layers, vegetative
DNA amplification and late proteins (L1, L2 capsid) only in differentiated upper layers, and
virions are shed with squames. This makes papillomaviruses impossible to grow in conventional
cell culture and explains the long incubation and wart formation.
• E6 and E7 (and E5) drive cell proliferation — E7 binds Rb, releasing E2F, and E6 promotes
degradation of p53 — which underlies the oncogenic potential of papillomaviruses (Chapter
14), including bovine papillomavirus in bracken-fern-associated cancers and equine sarcoid.
• Polyomaviruses (e.g. avian polyomavirus) express large T antigen, which binds Rb and p53,
unwinds the origin as a helicase and recruits host DNA polymerase; early and late transcription
proceeds in opposite directions from a common regulatory region; capsids (VP1 pentamers, T =
7) assemble in the nucleus and are released by lysis.
• Both families produce virus-like particles (VLPs) by expressing L1 (or VP1) alone — the basis
of modern VLP vaccines.
8.7 Parvoviruses and Circoviruses
• Parvoviruses (canine parvovirus 2, feline panleukopenia virus, porcine parvovirus) have a linear
ssDNA genome with terminal hairpins. After entry (clathrin-mediated endocytosis; the VP1
phospholipase A2 domain helps escape from the endosome), the intact capsid enters the nucleus.
Because the virus encodes no polymerase, it depends absolutely on host S-phase enzymes —
hence replication only in rapidly dividing cells (intestinal crypts, bone marrow, lymphoid tissue,
and the foetal myocardium in pups infected in utero).
• Rolling-hairpin replication: the 3′ hairpin acts as a self-primer; host DNA polymerase extends
it to a duplex; the viral NS1 protein nicks the origin, remains covalently attached, and acts as a
helicase; hairpin "flip-flop" and strand displacement generate new genomes, which are packaged
(predominantly the negative strand) into preformed capsids.
• Consequences: panleukopenia and enteritis (destruction of dividing cells), myocarditis in
neonates, cerebellar hypoplasia in kittens infected in utero (destruction of dividing Purkinje cell
precursors), and SMEDI in pigs.
• Circoviruses (porcine circovirus 2, beak and feather disease virus) have a tiny circular ssDNA
genome with two main ORFs in ambisense orientation: Rep (replication initiator) and Cap
(capsid). The genome is converted to a double-stranded replicative form by host enzymes and
replicated by rolling-circle replication: Rep nicks a conserved nonanucleotide motif in the stem-
loop origin, host polymerase displaces the strand, and Rep religates unit-length circles. Again,
dividing cells are required — PCV2 replicates in lymphoid tissue and causes lymphoid depletion
and immunosuppression.
8.8 Poxviruses and African Swine Fever Virus
• Poxviruses (sheeppox, goatpox, lumpy skin disease, orf, fowlpox) are unique among DNA
viruses of vertebrates in replicating entirely in the cytoplasm, in discrete virus factories
Chapter 8 | Page 99



VMC 503: General Virology
(viroplasm). They therefore carry a complete transcription system in the virion: a DNA-
dependent RNA polymerase, capping and methylating enzymes and a poly(A) polymerase.
• Cycle: entry by macropinocytosis or fusion → first-stage uncoating releases the core → early
transcription within the core produces functional mRNAs (including proteins for second-stage
uncoating, DNA replication, and immune evasion — "virokines" and "viroceptors" that mimic or
bind cytokines and complement) → second-stage uncoating releases the DNA → DNA
replication in factories (self-priming from nicked hairpin termini, producing concatemers that
are later resolved) → intermediate and late transcription → assembly of crescent membranes
into immature virions → maturation into brick-shaped mature virions (MV) → some are
wrapped by Golgi membranes and transported on microtubules to the surface as enveloped
virions (EV), which are released by exocytosis and can be propelled to neighbouring cells on
actin tails.
• The cytoplasmic site of replication and the large coding capacity explain the eosinophilic
intracytoplasmic inclusion bodies (Bollinger and Guarnieri bodies), the ability to replicate in
enucleated cells, and the use of poxviruses (vaccinia, capripox, fowlpox) as recombinant
vaccine vectors.
• African swine fever virus is likewise cytoplasmic (with a brief nuclear phase), forms perinuclear
factories in monocytes and macrophages, packages its own transcription enzymes, and encodes
an extensive set of proteins that block interferon and apoptosis — features that underlie its severe
pathogenesis and the long-standing difficulty of developing a vaccine.
8.9 Hepadnaviruses: A DNA Virus that Reverse Transcribes
• Hepatitis B virus and duck hepatitis B virus (Baltimore class VII) have a small, partly double-
stranded circular DNA genome.
• After entry (via NTCP in hepatocytes), the genome is completed and converted in the nucleus
into covalently closed circular DNA (cccDNA), a stable minichromosome that is the basis of
chronic infection and of the difficulty of cure.
• Host RNA polymerase II transcribes cccDNA into several mRNAs, including the pregenomic
RNA (pgRNA).
• The pgRNA is packaged with the viral polymerase (reverse transcriptase) into assembling core
particles, where it is reverse transcribed into the DNA genome; capsids are then enveloped with
HBsAg and released, or recycle DNA back to the nucleus to amplify cccDNA.
• Consequences: antiviral nucleoside analogues target the reverse transcriptase but do not
eliminate cccDNA; the surface antigen is produced in vast excess and forms the basis of the
recombinant HBsAg vaccine; the virus is the helper for hepatitis delta virus (Chapter 7).
Chapter 8 | Page 100



VMC 503: General Virology

INDIA FOCUS: DNA VIRUS REPLICATION — RELEVANCE TO INDIAN VETERINARY
PRACTICE
▪ Capripoxviruses and LSD: the cytoplasmic replication and large immune-evasion gene repertoire of
capripoxviruses underlie the persistence of sheeppox, goatpox and lumpy skin disease in India.
Because the three viruses are closely related, live attenuated goatpox vaccine strains have been used
Chapter 8 | Page 101



VMC 503: General Virology
against LSD; the indigenous homologous vaccine Lumpi-ProVacInd was developed by ICAR–NRCE,
Hisar with ICAR–IVRI after the 2022 epidemic. Capripox and fowlpox viruses are also being used in
India as recombinant vector platforms for experimental vaccines (e.g. expressing PPRV antigens).
▪ Marek’s disease: the herpesvirus life cycle explains why only the feather follicle epithelium produces
fully enveloped, environmentally stable virus — the source of dander that spreads infection in Indian
poultry houses — and why vaccination (HVT, CVI988 Rispens) prevents tumours but not infection, so
shedding continues.
▪ IBR (BoHV-1): latency in trigeminal and sacral ganglia and reactivation at parturition or transport
underlie the persistence of infection in Indian organised dairy herds; serological surveys report
widespread seropositivity, and gE-deleted marker vaccines with companion DIVA ELISA are the
internationally used control tool.
▪ Canine parvovirus: dependence on dividing cells explains the classic picture of haemorrhagic enteritis
and leucopenia in pups seen daily in Indian small-animal practice, and the importance of maternal
antibody in deciding vaccination schedules; Indian studies have reported the circulation of CPV-2a, 2b
and 2c antigenic variants.
▪ PCV2 and poultry adenoviruses: porcine circovirus 2 is reported from Indian pig herds including the
north-eastern states, and fowl adenoviruses cause inclusion body hepatitis and hydropericardium
syndrome (Angara disease), a major problem in Indian broilers — its intranuclear inclusion bodies are
a direct reflection of nuclear adenovirus replication.
▪ Duck hepatitis B virus, a hepadnavirus, has been used as a laboratory model; hepatitis B control in
humans in India relies on the recombinant HBsAg vaccine included in the Universal Immunization
Programme.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Drawing and labelling the generalised replication cycle and the herpesvirus cascade.
▪ Demonstration of a one-step growth experiment: infection at high multiplicity, sampling at intervals,
titration, and plotting the eclipse and rise periods (Chapter 5).
▪ Observation of intranuclear (adenovirus, herpesvirus) and intracytoplasmic (poxvirus) inclusion bodies
in stained sections.
▪ Demonstration of pock formation on the chorioallantoic membrane as evidence of poxvirus replication.
▪ Exercise: given a virus family, predict the site of replication, the enzymes carried in the virion, whether
the purified genome is infectious, and a rational antiviral target.
8.11 Summary
• All viruses replicate through attachment, entry, uncoating, gene expression, genome replication,
assembly, maturation and release; the eclipse period is the interval when no infectious virus can
be recovered.
• Receptors determine host range and tissue tropism; entry occurs by fusion at the plasma
membrane, by receptor-mediated endocytosis or by macropinocytosis.
• DNA viruses replicate in the nucleus except poxviruses and ASFV, which build cytoplasmic
factories and carry their own transcription enzymes.
• Small DNA viruses depend on host polymerases and dividing cells; large DNA viruses encode
their own polymerases and can replicate in resting cells, providing selective antiviral targets.
• Herpesviruses express genes in an α → β → γ cascade, replicate by a rolling circle, mature by
successive envelopment, and establish latency with reactivation.
• Adenoviruses use E1A to force cells into S phase and replicate by protein-primed strand
displacement; papillomaviruses tie their cycle to keratinocyte differentiation; parvoviruses use
rolling-hairpin and circoviruses rolling-circle replication.
Chapter 8 | Page 102



VMC 503: General Virology
• Hepadnaviruses are DNA viruses that replicate through reverse transcription of a pregenomic
RNA and persist as nuclear cccDNA.
• Replication strategy explains tropism, inclusion bodies, latency, oncogenesis, the feasibility of
cell culture, and the design of vaccines, vectors and antiviral drugs.
8.12 Glossary
Table 8.4: Glossary of important terms

UNIT II — REPLICATION AND INTERACTIONS OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 9
Replication of RNA Viruses
Lectures 14, 15 and 16
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Explain the general features of RNA virus replication, including the role of the RNA-dependent RNA
polymerase, replication organelles and the high mutation rate.
▪ Describe the replication strategies of positive-sense RNA viruses — picornaviruses, flaviviruses,
togaviruses, coronaviruses and arteriviruses.
▪ Describe the replication of non-segmented and segmented negative-sense RNA viruses, including
rhabdoviruses, paramyxoviruses, orthomyxoviruses and bunyaviruses.
▪ Describe the replication of double-stranded RNA viruses (reoviruses, orbiviruses, rotaviruses,
birnaviruses) and retroviruses.
▪ Relate RNA virus replication strategies to pathogenesis, evolution, diagnosis, vaccines, reverse genetics
and antiviral therapy.
LECTURE 14: GENERAL FEATURES AND POSITIVE-SENSE RNA VIRUSES
9.1 General Features of RNA Virus Replication
Host cells cannot copy RNA from an RNA template, so every RNA virus (except retroviruses) must
encode an RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (RdRp) — the only protein conserved across the whole
realm Riboviria and a prime antiviral target. Most RNA viruses replicate entirely in the cytoplasm;
exceptions that use the nucleus are orthomyxoviruses (influenza), bornaviruses and retroviruses.
• Replication organelles: RNA synthesis takes place on modified host membranes — vesicles,
invaginations (spherules) or double-membrane vesicles (DMVs) derived from the ER, Golgi,
endosomes or mitochondria. These concentrate viral and host factors and shield replication
intermediates, especially double-stranded RNA, from host sensors (RIG-I, MDA5, PKR) that
would trigger interferon.
• Strategy depends on genome type: positive-sense genomes are translated first; negative-sense
and double-stranded genomes must first be transcribed by a polymerase carried in the virion;
retroviruses reverse-transcribe into DNA.
• Error-prone replication: RdRps lack proofreading (except the coronavirus nsp14
exonuclease), producing about one mutation per genome per replication cycle. Populations
exist as quasispecies — clouds of related variants — which enables rapid adaptation, escape
from antibodies and drugs, and host-range change, but also limits genome size.
• Genome ends as promoters: conserved sequences and structures at the 5′ and 3′ ends act as
promoters for replication and packaging signals.
• Speed and yield: many RNA viruses complete a cycle within 6–12 hours and produce thousands
of particles per cell, often causing rapid cell death and acute disease.
Chapter 9 | Page 107



VMC 503: General Virology
9.2 Positive-Sense (+ssRNA) Viruses: Common Strategy
• The genome is mRNA-like and is translated immediately on entry, producing the RdRp and
other replication proteins; hence the naked genome is infectious.
• The RdRp synthesises a full-length negative-strand (antigenome) intermediate, which is the
template for many new positive strands (asymmetric: positive strands greatly outnumber
negative strands).
• New positive strands serve three roles: mRNA for translation, templates for more negative
strands, and genomes to be packaged.
• Two main approaches to making several proteins from one RNA: polyprotein translation and
proteolytic cleavage (picorna-, flaviviruses) or production of subgenomic mRNAs (toga-,
corona-, arteriviruses, caliciviruses).
9.2.1 Picornaviruses (e.g. Foot-and-Mouth Disease Virus)
1. Attachment and entry: FMDV binds integrins (αvβ6, αvβ1, αvβ3, αvβ8) through an Arg-Gly-
Asp (RGD) motif in the G–H loop of VP1 (cell-culture-adapted strains also use heparan
sulphate); after endocytosis, the acid-labile capsid dissociates into pentamers in endosomes and
the RNA is released.
2. Translation: the uncapped genome carries a VPg at its 5′ end and an internal ribosome entry
site (IRES); ribosomes initiate at the IRES independently of a cap. A single polyprotein is
produced and cleaved by viral proteases (Lpro, 2A and 3Cpro) into structural (P1 → VP0, VP3,
VP1) and non-structural (2B, 2C, 3A, 3B/VPg, 3C, 3D polymerase) proteins.
3. Host shut-off: FMDV Lpro (and enterovirus 2A) cleaves the host translation factor eIF4G,
stopping cap-dependent translation of host mRNAs while viral IRES-driven translation
continues; this also blocks interferon synthesis.
4. RNA replication: the 3D polymerase uses uridylylated VPg (VPg-pUpU) as a protein primer
to copy positive into negative strands and back, on membrane vesicles induced by 2B, 2C and 3A.
5. Assembly and release: capsid proteins form protomers and pentamers, which assemble around
newly made RNA; cleavage of VP0 → VP2 + VP4 matures the virion; release is by cell lysis
within 4–8 hours.
6. Practical relevance: antibodies to non-structural proteins (3ABC) distinguish infected from
vaccinated animals (DIVA) because purified vaccines lack them; the G–H loop is the dominant
neutralising site; the high mutation rate generates antigenic variation within serotypes.
9.2.2 Flaviviruses (Pestiviruses and Orthoflaviviruses)
• Enveloped viruses (e.g. BVDV, classical swine fever virus, Japanese encephalitis virus, West
Nile virus) that enter by endocytosis and low-pH fusion (class II fusion protein E in
orthoflaviviruses).
• The genome (capped in orthoflaviviruses; IRES in pestiviruses and hepaciviruses) is translated
into a single polyprotein embedded in the ER membrane, cleaved by host signal peptidases
and the viral NS2B–NS3 protease into structural (C, prM, E) and non-structural (NS1–NS5)
proteins; NS5 is the RdRp and methyltransferase.
• Replication occurs in ER-derived vesicle packets; virions bud into the ER, pass through the
secretory pathway, and are matured by furin cleavage of prM before release by exocytosis.
Chapter 9 | Page 108



VMC 503: General Virology
• Pestivirus biotypes: BVDV exists as non-cytopathic (ncp) and cytopathic (cp) biotypes; cp
strains arise from ncp strains by mutations or RNA recombination (insertion of host ubiquitin
sequences) that increase NS3 release. In a persistently infected (PI) calf carrying ncp BVDV,
the emergence of a homologous cp virus precipitates fatal mucosal disease.
9.2.3 Togaviruses (Alphaviruses)
• Enveloped, icosahedral (T = 4) viruses such as eastern, western and Venezuelan equine
encephalitis viruses, Getah virus and chikungunya virus.
• The 5′ two-thirds of the genome is translated into non-structural polyproteins (nsP1–4) forming
the replicase; this synthesises a negative strand, from which two positive RNAs are made — the
full-length genome and a 26S subgenomic mRNA (from an internal promoter) encoding the
structural polyprotein (capsid, E3, E2, 6K, E1).
• Replication occurs in spherules at the plasma membrane and endosomes; virions bud from the
plasma membrane. The subgenomic promoter is exploited to build self-amplifying RNA
(replicon) vaccines.
9.2.4 Coronaviruses and Arteriviruses (Order Nidovirales)
• Entry: coronavirus spike (S) binds receptors (e.g. aminopeptidase N for TGEV, PEDV and
canine and feline coronaviruses; ACE2 for SARS-CoV-2; sialic acid for bovine coronavirus and
IBV attachment); fusion follows protease cleavage (furin, TMPRSS2, cathepsins).
• Replicase expression: the 5′ two-thirds of the genome (ORF1a and ORF1b) is translated into
pp1a and, by a −1 ribosomal frameshift, pp1ab, cleaved by the papain-like (PLpro) and main
(3CLpro/Mpro) proteases into 15–16 non-structural proteins (nsps), including the RdRp
(nsp12), helicase (nsp13) and the proofreading exonuclease (nsp14) that allows genomes of 26–
32 kb.
• Replication organelles: double-membrane vesicles derived from the ER.
• Discontinuous transcription: during negative-strand synthesis, the polymerase pauses at
transcription-regulatory sequences (TRS) before each gene and jumps to the leader TRS at the
5′ end, producing a nested set of subgenomic mRNAs with common 5′ leaders and 3′ ends
("nido" = nest); each is translated mainly from its 5′-most ORF (S, E, M, N and accessory
proteins).
• High recombination rate — through template switching — generates new variants (e.g.
emergence of porcine respiratory coronavirus from TGEV by deletion in S; recombinant IBV
strains).
• Assembly: in the ER–Golgi intermediate compartment (ERGIC); release by exocytosis.
• Arteriviruses (PRRSV, equine arteritis virus) follow the same nested strategy with smaller
genomes (13–16 kb) and replicate in macrophages (PRRSV uses CD163 as a key receptor).
Chapter 9 | Page 109



VMC 503: General Virology
LECTURE 15: NEGATIVE-SENSE RNA VIRUSES
9.3 Negative-Sense (−ssRNA) Viruses: Common Strategy
• The genome is complementary to mRNA and cannot be translated; it is never naked in the cell
but always coated with nucleoprotein (N or NP) as a ribonucleoprotein (RNP), which is the
active template. Purified genomic RNA is therefore not infectious.
• Virions carry their own RNA polymerase complex (large protein L with phosphoprotein P in
Mononegavirales; PB1, PB2, PA in influenza) to carry out primary transcription immediately
after entry.
• The polymerase performs two distinct activities on the same template: transcription (producing
capped, polyadenylated mRNAs) and replication (producing full-length positive-sense
antigenomes, which are encapsidated and then copied into new genomes). The switch depends
on the accumulation of N protein.
• Genomes may be non-segmented (order Mononegavirales: rhabdo-, paramyxo-, pneumo-, filo-,
bornaviruses) or segmented (orthomyxo-, bunya-, arenaviruses); segmentation permits
reassortment.
9.3.1 Non-Segmented Negative-Strand Viruses (Mononegavirales)
• Rhabdoviruses (rabies virus, bovine ephemeral fever virus, vesicular stomatitis virus): the G
glycoprotein binds receptors (for rabies, including the nicotinic acetylcholine receptor, NCAM
and p75 neurotrophin receptor on neurons) and mediates low-pH fusion in endosomes. The
genome encodes five genes in the order 3′–N–P–M–G–L–5′.
• Paramyxoviruses (Newcastle disease virus, PPRV, canine distemper virus, Nipah virus): the
attachment protein (HN, H or G) binds sialic acid or protein receptors (SLAM/CD150 on
Chapter 9 | Page 110



VMC 503: General Virology
lymphocytes and nectin-4 on epithelial cells for morbilliviruses; ephrin-B2/B3 for Nipah), and
the F protein fuses the envelope directly with the plasma membrane at neutral pH — which
also allows syncytium formation.
• Sequential ("stop–start") transcription: the polymerase enters only at the 3′ end and
transcribes each gene in turn, stopping at a gene-end signal (where it adds a poly(A) tail by
stuttering on a run of U residues) and re-starting at the next gene-start signal. Because it
sometimes falls off at the junctions, a gradient of mRNA abundance results — genes near the 3′
end (N) are transcribed most, the L gene least. Gene order thus regulates expression.
• Cytoplasmic replication in inclusion bodies (e.g. rabies Negri bodies are viral replication
factories), followed by assembly at the plasma membrane: M protein links RNPs to the envelope
glycoproteins and drives budding.
• RNA editing (paramyxoviruses): the polymerase inserts extra G residues at a specific site of the
P gene, producing mRNAs for P, V and W proteins; V is a potent interferon antagonist. Some
genes are also expressed from overlapping ORFs (C proteins).
• Rule of six: replication of paramyxovirus genomes is efficient only when the total length is a
multiple of six nucleotides, reflecting binding of each N protein to six nucleotides — an
important consideration in designing reverse genetics systems (e.g. recombinant NDV vector
vaccines).
• Virulence determinant of NDV: the F0 cleavage site — multiple basic amino acids (velogenic,
cleaved by ubiquitous furin, systemic infection) versus few basic residues (lentogenic, cleaved
only by trypsin-like enzymes in respiratory and intestinal tracts).
9.3.2 Segmented Negative-Strand Viruses: Influenza Viruses (Orthomyxoviridae)
1. Attachment: haemagglutinin (HA) binds sialic acid on host glycoproteins — α2,3-linked
(avian-type, abundant in the avian intestine and lower human respiratory tract) or α2,6-linked
(human-type, upper respiratory tract of humans; pigs have both). Receptor preference is a key
determinant of host range.
2. Entry and uncoating: endocytosis; low pH in the endosome triggers HA conformational change
and fusion; the M2 ion channel acidifies the virion interior, releasing the eight vRNPs from M1
(M2 is the target of amantadine).
3. Nuclear import: vRNPs are imported into the nucleus — influenza is unusual among RNA
viruses in replicating in the nucleus.
4. Cap-snatching: the polymerase subunit PB2 binds the 5′ cap of host pre-mRNAs and PA cleaves
10–13 nucleotides downstream; this capped fragment primes viral mRNA synthesis by PB1 (the
target of baloxavir is the PA endonuclease). Polyadenylation occurs by stuttering on a U-tract.
5. Splicing: segments 7 and 8 are spliced by host machinery to produce M2 and NEP/NS2
respectively; some segments encode extra proteins by alternative reading frames (e.g. PB1-F2,
PA-X). NS1 is the major interferon antagonist.
6. Replication: full-length complementary RNAs (cRNA) are made (unprimed) and copied into
new vRNAs, which are encapsidated by NP and exported from the nucleus with M1 and NEP.
7. Assembly and release: HA, NA and M2 travel through the secretory pathway to the apical
plasma membrane; vRNPs are packaged (one of each of the eight segments, guided by packaging
signals); virions bud, and neuraminidase (NA) removes sialic acid so that progeny are released
and do not aggregate (target of oseltamivir and zanamivir).
Chapter 9 | Page 111



VMC 503: General Virology
8. HA activation: HA0 must be cleaved into HA1 and HA2 by host proteases for infectivity; HPAI
H5 and H7 viruses have a multibasic cleavage site cleaved by furin in many tissues, producing
systemic disease.
9. Reassortment of segments between two viruses co-infecting a cell (e.g. in pigs, the "mixing
vessel") causes antigenic shift; point mutations in HA and NA cause antigenic drift (Chapter
10).
9.3.3 Bunyaviruses and Arenaviruses
• Bunyaviruses (order Bunyavirales — e.g. Crimean–Congo haemorrhagic fever virus, Rift
Valley fever virus, Akabane and Schmallenberg viruses) have three genome segments — L
(polymerase), M (glycoprotein precursor Gn/Gc) and S (nucleoprotein, and in some genera a
non-structural protein) — encapsidated as circular-looking RNPs because the ends are
complementary.
• They replicate in the cytoplasm but also use cap-snatching (from cytoplasmic host mRNAs);
virions bud into the Golgi and are released by exocytosis. Some segments use an ambisense
strategy (one gene transcribed from the genome and another from the antigenome).
• Segment reassortment has produced new viruses (e.g. Schmallenberg virus is related to
reassortants within the Simbu serogroup).
• Arenaviruses (lymphocytic choriomeningitis virus, Lassa virus) have two ambisense segments
(L and S), each encoding two proteins in opposite orientation separated by an intergenic hairpin
that terminates transcription.
Chapter 9 | Page 112



VMC 503: General Virology
LECTURE 16: DOUBLE-STRANDED RNA VIRUSES AND RETROVIRUSES
9.4 Double-Stranded RNA Viruses
9.4.1 Reoviruses, Orbiviruses and Rotaviruses
Members of Sedoreoviridae and Spinareoviridae — including bluetongue virus (BTV), African
horse sickness virus, epizootic haemorrhagic disease virus, rotaviruses and avian orthoreoviruses
— have 10–12 segments of dsRNA inside multilayered, non-enveloped icosahedral capsids. Their
strategy is designed so that dsRNA is never exposed to the cytoplasm, where it would activate
interferon and PKR.
1. Entry and partial uncoating: the outer capsid proteins attach and mediate entry (BTV VP2
attachment and VP5 membrane penetration; rotavirus VP4 — activated by trypsin cleavage into
VP8 and VP5 — and VP7). The outer layer is removed in endosomes, releasing the core (BTV)
or double-layered particle (rotavirus) into the cytoplasm.
2. Transcription inside the core: the core is a transcriptionally active "molecular machine" —
each segment is transcribed by a core-associated RdRp (BTV VP1; rotavirus VP1), and the
transcripts are capped by core enzymes (BTV VP4; rotavirus VP3) and extruded through
channels at the five-fold vertices into the cytoplasm. Parental dsRNA never leaves the core.
3. Translation and viroplasm formation: the capped (non-polyadenylated) mRNAs are
translated; non-structural proteins (BTV NS2; rotavirus NSP2 and NSP5) form viral inclusion
bodies (viroplasms) where assembly occurs.
4. Assortment and packaging: one copy of each of the 10–12 positive-sense mRNAs is selected
and packaged with polymerase into a new subviral particle, where each is copied into dsRNA
(negative-strand synthesis occurs within the particle).
5. Secondary transcription from progeny cores amplifies the infection; outer capsid proteins are
then added.
6. Release: by cell lysis, and for BTV also by budding involving the NS3 protein. Rotaviruses
acquire a transient envelope when immature particles bud into the ER, guided by NSP4 (which
also acts as a viral enterotoxin, causing secretory diarrhoea); the envelope is lost and VP7 is
added to give mature triple-layered particles.
7. Reassortment between co-infecting strains is frequent and important in the evolution of BTV
(including reassortment between field and live vaccine strains) and of rotaviruses.
9.4.2 Birnaviruses
• Infectious bursal disease virus (IBDV) and infectious pancreatic necrosis virus have two
segments (A and B) of dsRNA in a single-shelled T = 13 capsid.
• Segment A encodes a polyprotein (pVP2–VP4–VP3) processed by the VP4 protease and a small
VP5 protein; segment B encodes VP1, the RdRp, which is covalently linked to the genome ends
(a VPg-like role) and primes replication — an unusual feature for dsRNA viruses.
• IBDV targets dividing IgM-bearing B lymphocytes in the bursa of Fabricius, causing
immunosuppression; the hypervariable region of VP2 determines antigenicity and virulence
(very virulent IBDV).
Chapter 9 | Page 113



VMC 503: General Virology
9.5 Retroviruses
Retroviruses (Baltimore class VI) — including avian leukosis virus, bovine leukaemia virus
(BLV), feline leukaemia virus, Jaagsiekte sheep retrovirus, and the lentiviruses equine infectious
anaemia virus, maedi–visna virus, caprine arthritis–encephalitis virus, feline immunodeficiency
virus and HIV — carry a diploid, positive-sense RNA genome but replicate through a DNA
intermediate that integrates into the host chromosome.
1. Attachment and fusion: the envelope glycoprotein (SU attachment and TM fusion subunits)
binds receptors (and for lentiviruses, co-receptors) and fuses at the plasma membrane or in
endosomes.
2. Reverse transcription in the cytoplasm within the partially uncoated core: the virion reverse
transcriptase (RT), primed by a host tRNA bound to the primer-binding site, copies the RNA
into minus-strand DNA; RNase H degrades the RNA template; plus-strand DNA is synthesised;
two strand-transfer ("jump") steps duplicate the terminal sequences into long terminal
repeats (LTRs) at each end. RT has no proofreading activity, so mutation rates are high.
3. Nuclear entry and integration: the pre-integration complex enters the nucleus (simple
retroviruses need cell division; lentiviruses can infect non-dividing cells) and the viral
integrase inserts the linear DNA into host chromosomal DNA as a provirus — a permanent part
of the cell genome, transmitted to all daughter cells.
4. Transcription of the provirus by host RNA polymerase II from the 5′ LTR promoter/enhancer;
full-length transcripts serve as genomic RNA and as mRNA for Gag (matrix, capsid,
nucleocapsid) and Gag–Pol (by ribosomal frameshifting or stop-codon read-through); spliced
mRNAs encode Env and, in complex retroviruses, regulatory proteins.
5. Complex retroviruses encode regulatory proteins: BLV and HTLV encode Tax (transactivator
of the LTR, also oncogenic) and Rex (exports unspliced RNA); lentiviruses encode Tat, Rev and
accessory proteins (e.g. Vif, Nef) that counteract host restriction factors.
6. Assembly and budding: Gag polyproteins assemble at the plasma membrane, packaging two
copies of genomic RNA (hence diploid — which permits recombination); virions bud, and the
viral protease cleaves Gag and Gag–Pol after release, converting immature to infectious mature
particles (target of protease inhibitors).
7. Consequences: lifelong infection because of integration and latency; oncogenesis by insertional
activation of cellular proto-oncogenes, by transduced viral oncogenes (acute transforming
retroviruses) or by transactivating proteins (BLV Tax) (Chapter 14); endogenous retroviruses in
the germ line (Chapter 2); retroviral and lentiviral vectors for gene transfer.
Chapter 9 | Page 114



VMC 503: General Virology
9.6 Comparative Overview of RNA Virus Strategies
Table 9.1: Summary of replication features of RNA virus groups

Genome infectious
alone Yes No No
No (RNA);

• Reverse genetics: because infectious virus can be recovered from cloned cDNA (directly for
+ssRNA viruses; by co-expressing N, P and L proteins for −ssRNA viruses; by eight-plasmid
systems for influenza), RNA viruses can be engineered to make attenuated vaccines, marker
(DIVA) vaccines, vector vaccines (e.g. recombinant NDV expressing HA of avian influenza)
and reassortant seed strains.

• Antiviral targets include RdRps (e.g. nucleoside analogues such as favipiravir, remdesivir,
molnupiravir), proteases (coronavirus Mpro), the influenza cap-snatching endonuclease and
neuraminidase, and retroviral RT, integrase and protease (Chapter 18).
INDIA FOCUS: RNA VIRUS REPLICATION — RELEVANCE TO INDIAN LIVESTOCK
▪ Most of India’s major transboundary animal diseases are caused by RNA viruses: FMD (picornavirus),
PPR (paramyxovirus), bluetongue (orbivirus), avian influenza (orthomyxovirus), Newcastle disease
(paramyxovirus), infectious bursal disease (birnavirus), classical swine fever and BVD (pestiviruses),
PRRS (arterivirus), rabies (rhabdovirus), and Japanese encephalitis (flavivirus, with pigs as amplifying
hosts).
▪ For FMD, the fact that non-structural proteins are made only during replication is the basis of the NSP
DIVA ELISA used by ICAR–NIFMD in serosurveillance for the National Animal Disease Control
Programme; the antigenic drift caused by error-prone replication explains why Indian vaccine strains
must be matched periodically against field isolates.
▪ India is pursuing PPR control in line with the global goal of eradication by 2030, using the homologous
live attenuated Sungri/96 vaccine adapted to Vero cells; reverse-genetics and recombinant NDV-
vectored constructs for PPRV have been developed in Indian laboratories as experimental marker
vaccines.
▪ Bluetongue is highly endemic in India, and reassortment among the many co-circulating serotypes —
including between field and vaccine strains — is a recognised risk that informs the design of the
indigenous inactivated multivalent BTV vaccine.
▪ Newcastle disease control uses live lentogenic vaccines (LaSota, F1) and the mesogenic Mukteswar
strain; the F0 cleavage site distinguishes the velogenic field viruses (genotype XIII predominant in
India) from vaccine strains, and this molecular basis of virulence is confirmed by sequencing at Indian
reference laboratories.
▪ Avian influenza surveillance in India (WOAH reference laboratory ICAR–NIHSAD, Bhopal) relies on
RT-qPCR and on sequencing of the HA cleavage site to separate highly pathogenic from low-
pathogenic viruses; pigs are watched as potential "mixing vessels" for reassortment.
▪ Rabies remains a major zoonosis in India; the cytoplasmic replication of the virus and its Negri-body
inclusions underlie the fluorescent-antibody and histological diagnosis used at designated centres, and
the surface glycoprotein is the antigen of the cell-culture rabies vaccines produced in India.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Drawing and labelling the replication cycles of a picornavirus, a paramyxovirus, influenza virus and a
retrovirus.
▪ Demonstration of syncytium formation by a paramyxovirus (e.g. PPRV or NDV) in cell culture.
▪ Demonstration of haemagglutination by influenza or Newcastle disease virus as evidence of surface
haemagglutinin.
▪ RT-PCR and real-time RT-PCR for an RNA virus (e.g. FMDV, PPRV or avian influenza), illustrating
the reverse-transcription step.
▪ NSP (3ABC) DIVA ELISA for FMD (demonstration) contrasted with a structural-protein antibody test.
▪ Exercise: given an RNA virus, predict whether the genome is infectious, whether a polymerase is
packaged, the site of replication, and a rational antiviral or vaccine strategy.
9.7 Summary
• RNA viruses encode an RNA-dependent RNA polymerase, replicate mostly in the cytoplasm on
modified membranes that hide double-stranded intermediates, and mutate rapidly (quasispecies)
because the polymerase lacks proofreading (except the coronavirus nsp14 exonuclease).
Chapter 9 | Page 116



VMC 503: General Virology
• Positive-sense RNA genomes are infectious and are translated first; they make several proteins by
polyprotein processing (picorna-, flaviviruses) or subgenomic mRNAs (toga-, corona-,
arteriviruses); coronaviruses use nested subgenomic mRNAs and can recombine.
• Negative-sense RNA genomes are not infectious, are always coated with nucleoprotein and carry
a polymerase in the virion; non-segmented viruses use stop–start sequential transcription with a
gene-order gradient, and paramyxoviruses use RNA editing and follow the rule of six.
• Influenza virus is segmented, replicates in the nucleus using cap-snatching, and undergoes
antigenic drift (mutation) and shift (reassortment); the HA cleavage site determines virulence.
• Double-stranded RNA viruses transcribe their segments inside the intact core to avoid host
sensors and reassort readily; rotavirus NSP4 is an enterotoxin.
• Retroviruses reverse-transcribe their diploid RNA into DNA that integrates as a provirus, giving
lifelong infection, oncogenesis and endogenous retroviruses; RT, integrase and protease are drug
targets.
• Reverse genetics allows rational design of attenuated, marker and vector vaccines and of
reassortant seed strains, and the RNA replication machinery provides many antiviral targets.
9.8 Glossary
Table 9.2: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
RNA-dependent RNA
polymerase
Enzyme that copies RNA from an RNA template; the hallmark enzyme of
RNA viruses.
Quasispecies Population of closely related viral variants generated by error-prone
replication.
Replication organelle Modified host membrane compartment where viral RNA is synthesised.
IRES Internal ribosome entry site allowing cap-independent translation.

Polyprotein Single large protein cleaved into several functional proteins.
Subgenomic mRNA Shorter mRNA transcribed from part of the genome to express downstream
genes.
Nested set Coterminal subgenomic mRNAs of coronaviruses sharing 5′ leader and 3′ end.
Ribonucleoprotein (RNP) Complex of viral RNA with nucleoprotein and polymerase, the template of
negative-strand viruses.

bunyaviruses).

nucleotides.
RNA editing Insertion of extra nucleotides to access alternative reading frames
(paramyxovirus P/V/W).
Antigenic drift Gradual antigenic change by point mutation.
Antigenic shift Abrupt antigenic change by reassortment of genome segments.
Viroplasm Cytoplasmic inclusion where dsRNA viruses assemble.
Reverse transcriptase Enzyme that synthesises DNA from an RNA template.

Long terminal repeat Repeated sequence at the ends of proviral DNA containing the promoter.

and vaccines.

UNIT II — REPLICATION AND INTERACTIONS OF VIRUSES
CHAPTER 10
Genetic and Non-genetic Interactions between Viruses
Lectures 17 and 18
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Explain the sources of genetic variation in viruses — mutation, recombination and reassortment — and
their consequences.
▪ Describe the types of mutations and mutants and the concept of quasispecies.
▪ Distinguish recombination and reassortment and give veterinary examples of each.
▪ Describe non-genetic interactions — complementation, phenotypic mixing, transcapsidation,
interference and defective interfering particles.
▪ Relate these interactions to virus evolution, emergence, vaccine safety, attenuation and diagnosis.
LECTURE 17: GENETIC INTERACTIONS — MUTATION, RECOMBINATION AND
REASSORTMENT
10.1 Introduction
Viruses evolve faster than any other group of organisms. When two viruses infect the same cell, or
when a single virus replicates its error-prone genome, their genetic material can change in ways that
create new phenotypes — altered virulence, host range, antigenicity or drug resistance. These changes
are grouped into genetic interactions (which alter the viral genome itself and are heritable) and non-
genetic interactions (which alter the phenotype of the progeny without changing their genotype, and
are therefore not heritable). Understanding them is essential for explaining the emergence of new
viruses, the evolution of vaccine and drug resistance, the attenuation of vaccine strains, and certain
pitfalls in vaccine production and diagnosis.
Table 10.1: Overview of virus–virus interactions
Category Interaction Heritable? Basis
Genetic Mutation Yes Change in nucleotide sequence
Genetic Recombination Yes Exchange of nucleic acid segments
within a genome
Genetic Reassortment Yes Exchange of whole genome segments
(segmented viruses)
Non-genetic Complementation No One virus supplies a functional
protein for another
Non-genetic Phenotypic mixing /
transcapsidation No Progeny acquire proteins of another
virus
Non-genetic Interference No One virus blocks replication of
another
Non-genetic Defective interfering particles No (defective) Deleted genomes that interfere with
standard virus
Chapter 10 | Page 121



VMC 503: General Virology
10.2 Mutation
• A mutation is a heritable change in the nucleotide sequence of the genome. Mutations arise
spontaneously through polymerase errors or can be induced by physical and chemical
mutagens.
• Mutation rates: RNA viruses mutate at about 10−3–10−5 substitutions per nucleotide per
replication because their RdRps lack proofreading (except coronaviruses with nsp14); DNA
viruses, using proofreading polymerases, mutate at about 10−6–10−8. High rates create the
quasispecies — a cloud of related variants on which selection acts (Chapter 9).
• Consequences: antigenic drift (influenza HA and NA), escape from neutralising antibodies and
vaccines, emergence of antiviral drug resistance (e.g. influenza resistance to amantadine and
oseltamivir), changes in host range and virulence, and attenuation used in live vaccines.
Table 10.2: Types of mutations and mutants
Type Description / example
Point mutation Single nucleotide change — substitution (transition or transversion), or silent,
missense or nonsense at the protein level
Insertion / deletion
Addition or loss of nucleotides; may cause frameshift (e.g. insertion of a multibasic
HA cleavage site converting LPAI to HPAI; ubiquitin insertion generating cytopathic
BVDV)
Deletion mutants Loss of a genome segment — basis of gene-deleted marker (DIVA) vaccines (e.g.
gE-deleted BoHV-1 and pseudorabies vaccines) and of defective interfering particles
Conditional-lethal
mutants
Temperature-sensitive (ts) mutants replicate at a low (permissive) but not a high
(restrictive) temperature — used to attenuate vaccines (e.g. ts NDV and influenza
vaccines) and to study gene function; cold-adapted mutants grow at low
temperatures and are attenuated in vivo
Host-range mutants Altered ability to infect particular hosts or cells (e.g. cell-culture adaptation of
FMDV to use heparan sulphate)
Plaque / cytopathology
mutants Altered plaque size or CPE
Attenuated mutants Reduced virulence, selected by serial passage in unnatural hosts or cell culture (e.g.
rinderpest, PPR, CDV vaccine strains)
Drug-resistant mutants Reduced susceptibility to antivirals
Revertants Mutants that regain the original (usually wild-type) phenotype — a safety concern
for live vaccines (reversion to virulence, e.g. oral polio vaccine)
• Mutagens: physical — ultraviolet light (pyrimidine dimers), X-rays; chemical — nitrous acid
(deamination), alkylating agents, base analogues (5-bromouracil, 2-aminopurine), intercalating
dyes (proflavine); and base-analogue antivirals (e.g. favipiravir, molnupiravir) that act by
lethal mutagenesis (error catastrophe) — pushing the mutation rate beyond the error threshold
so the virus can no longer produce viable progeny.
• Reversion and suppression: a back mutation restores the original sequence (true reversion); a
suppressor mutation at a second site restores function without correcting the first mutation.
10.3 Recombination
Recombination is the exchange of nucleic acid sequences between the genomes of two different
viruses (or between viral and host sequences) infecting the same cell, producing a recombinant
genome with a new combination of genes. It is a within-molecule exchange, distinct from reassortment
of whole segments.
Chapter 10 | Page 122



VMC 503: General Virology
Table 10.3: Mechanisms of recombination
Mechanism Occurs in Basis
Homologous recombination
Most DNA viruses (herpes,
pox, adeno); many +ssRNA
viruses (corona-, picorna-,
pestiviruses); retroviruses

choice template switching by the
polymerase (RNA) between similar
sequences
Non-homologous (illegitimate)
recombination
DNA viruses; some RNA
viruses
Joining of unrelated sequences; can capture
host genes (e.g. viral oncogenes, ubiquitin
insertion in cytopathic BVDV)
Copy-choice (template
switching) RNA viruses
The RdRp releases the nascent strand from
one template and resumes on another — the
main mechanism for RNA viruses, which
lack DNA-style recombination enzymes
Reactivation DNA viruses
Multiplicity reactivation — reconstitution
of infectious virus from two or more
inactivated (UV-damaged) genomes with
lesions at different sites; cross-reactivation
— recombination between a live and an
inactivated virus
• RNA viruses recombine much less readily than they mutate, and negative-sense, non-
segmented RNA viruses recombine very rarely (their genomes are always coated with
nucleoprotein), whereas positive-sense RNA viruses (especially coronaviruses, picornaviruses
and pestiviruses) recombine relatively often.
• Retroviruses recombine efficiently: because two RNA genomes are packaged (diploid), reverse
transcriptase switches between them during DNA synthesis.
• Veterinary examples:
◦ Coronaviruses — emergence of porcine respiratory coronavirus from transmissible
gastroenteritis virus by deletion in the spike gene; recombinant infectious bronchitis virus
field strains; feline enteric coronavirus recombining to give FIP-associated virus.
◦ Western equine encephalitis virus arose by recombination between eastern equine
encephalitis virus and a Sindbis-like alphavirus.
◦ Cytopathic BVDV arising from non-cytopathic BVDV by recombination with host
(ubiquitin) or viral sequences → mucosal disease.
◦ Live vaccine recombination — recombinant infectious laryngotracheitis virus (ILTV)
field viruses have arisen from recombination between two live attenuated vaccine strains in
Australia; recombination among live poultry vaccine and field herpesviruses and
coronaviruses.
◦ Poxvirus and herpesvirus recombination is exploited to construct recombinant vector
vaccines.
10.4 Reassortment
• Reassortment is the exchange of whole genome segments when two strains of a segmented
virus co-infect a cell, so that progeny receive a mixture of parental segments. It is a special,
highly efficient form of genetic shuffling unique to viruses with segmented genomes.
• Segmented viruses that reassort: orthomyxoviruses (influenza — 8 segments), reoviruses,
orbiviruses (bluetongue, AHS — 10 segments) and rotaviruses (11 segments), bunyaviruses
(3 segments), birnaviruses, arenaviruses.
Chapter 10 | Page 123



VMC 503: General Virology
• Antigenic shift in influenza is the classic example: reassortment between an animal (avian or
swine) and a human, or between two animal strains, in a co-infected cell (the pig as a "mixing
vessel" carrying both α2,3- and α2,6-sialic acid receptors) produces a virus with a novel HA (±
NA) to which the population has no immunity — the origin of pandemic influenza (e.g. the 2009
H1N1 pandemic virus, a reassortant of avian, human and swine lineages).
• Other examples: reassortment among bluetongue virus serotypes and between field and live
vaccine strains (a reason for caution with polyvalent modified-live BTV vaccines); reassortment
generating novel rotavirus genotype combinations (G and P types); reassortment within the
Simbu serogroup of bunyaviruses related to the emergence of Schmallenberg virus.
• Reassortment is used deliberately to make influenza vaccine seed viruses — the desired HA
and NA genes are combined with the high-yielding internal genes of a laboratory strain (e.g. PR8)
by classical reassortment or reverse genetics.
LECTURE 18: NON-GENETIC INTERACTIONS
10.5 Non-genetic Interactions
Non-genetic interactions change the phenotype of progeny viruses or the outcome of a mixed infection
without altering the genotype. The changes are therefore not inherited: on further passage the
progeny express only their own genes. These interactions are important in vaccine production, in the
biology of persistent infections, and as tools in the laboratory.
10.6 Complementation
• Complementation occurs when one virus supplies a functional gene product (protein) that
another, defective, virus cannot make, allowing the defective virus to replicate in that cell. The
genotypes are unchanged; each virus keeps its own genome.
Chapter 10 | Page 124



VMC 503: General Virology
• Non-allelic (intergenic) complementation: two viruses defective in different genes help each
other (each supplies the protein the other lacks).
• Allelic (intragenic) complementation: two viruses with mutations in the same gene
occasionally restore function when their (multimeric) proteins interact.
• Helper-dependent viruses: a defective virus depends absolutely on a helper — e.g. adeno-
associated virus needs adenovirus or herpesvirus helper functions; hepatitis delta virus needs
hepatitis B virus for its envelope; satellite viruses need their helper; defective acute
transforming retroviruses (carrying an oncogene in place of essential genes) need a replication-
competent helper virus.
• Uses: complementation is the basis of helper cell lines (e.g. cells expressing adenovirus E1 to
grow E1-deleted vector vaccines) and of complementation tests to assign mutants to functional
groups.
• Complementation must be distinguished from recombination: complementation is transient
(progeny remain defective), whereas recombination produces a stable, heritable new genome.
10.7 Phenotypic Mixing and Transcapsidation
• Phenotypic mixing occurs when a cell is co-infected by two related viruses and progeny particles
acquire structural (usually surface) proteins encoded by both genomes, while still containing
only one parental genome. The phenotype (e.g. antigenicity, host range, receptor use) is mixed,
but the genotype is that of the single enclosed genome; on the next round of infection the progeny
express only their own proteins — so the mixed phenotype is not inherited.
• Transcapsidation (genotypic mixing / pseudotype formation): the genome of one virus is
enclosed completely within the capsid or envelope of another virus. Such pseudotypes take on
the host range and antigenicity of the donor coat.
• Examples and uses: phenotypic mixing among retroviruses and among enteroviruses;
pseudotyping is widely used in the laboratory — e.g. lentiviral and retroviral vectors coated with
the vesicular stomatitis virus G glycoprotein (VSV-G) for broad host range, and pseudotyped
viruses expressing the surface protein of a dangerous virus (e.g. rabies, highly pathogenic avian
influenza, SARS-CoV-2) to allow safe neutralisation assays at lower biosafety levels.
• Significance for vaccines: phenotypic mixing between a vaccine and a field virus could
theoretically alter host range transiently, and pseudotyping is a consideration in the safety
assessment of some vectored vaccines.
10.8 Interference and Interferon
• Interference is the inhibition of replication of one virus by another. Mechanisms include:
◦ Interferon-mediated interference — infection by one virus induces interferons
(IFN-α/β), which establish an antiviral state that inhibits a second (challenge) virus (Chapter
16). This is broad and not virus-specific.
◦ Receptor blockade or destruction — the first virus occupies or removes the receptor (e.g.
destruction of sialic acid by neuraminidase) so the second cannot attach.
◦ Competition for host or viral replication machinery.
◦ Defective interfering (DI) particles (see below).
◦ Intrinsic/superinfection exclusion — an established infection (e.g. by a pestivirus or a
herpesvirus) prevents superinfection by a closely related virus.
Chapter 10 | Page 125



VMC 503: General Virology
• Homologous interference is between related viruses (often via DI particles or superinfection
exclusion); heterologous interference is between unrelated viruses (usually interferon-
mediated).
• Practical importance:
◦ Diagnosis: the exaltation of Newcastle disease virus (END) test and interference tests
detect non-cytopathic BVDV and classical swine fever virus (a non-cytopathic pestivirus
is revealed because it interferes with, or exalts, a challenge virus).
◦ Vaccination: simultaneous administration of two live vaccines may cause interference and
reduced take of one component; live vaccines and maternal or field virus may interfere;
interference is used positively when a benign virus protects against a virulent one.
◦ Interferon inducers and interference are being explored for antiviral prophylaxis.
10.9 Defective Interfering (DI) Particles
• DI particles are progeny virions with grossly deleted genomes that have lost genes essential for
independent replication but retain the sequences needed for replication and packaging (origins,
packaging signals).
• Origin: they arise during replication at high multiplicity of infection, especially on serial
undiluted passage ("von Magnus phenomenon", first described for influenza).
• Properties: they are defective (cannot replicate alone) but interfere with the standard (helper)
virus by competing for polymerase and other resources; because they are shorter, they replicate
and are packaged faster, progressively reducing the yield of infectious virus.
• Consequences: DI particles can moderate the severity of infection, contribute to the
establishment of persistent infections, and are a nuisance in vaccine production (loss of
infectious titre on repeated high-multiplicity passage — avoided by passaging at low
multiplicity). They are also being investigated as antiviral agents ("therapeutic interfering
particles").
Chapter 10 | Page 126



VMC 503: General Virology
10.10 Significance of Virus Interactions
Table 10.4: Practical significance of virus–virus interactions
Interaction Significance / example
Mutation Antigenic drift; drug and vaccine resistance; attenuation of vaccines; lethal
mutagenesis antivirals
Recombination Emergence of new viruses (PRCV from TGEV, cytopathic BVDV, recombinant
ILTV and IBV); construction of vector vaccines; caution with live vaccines
Reassortment Antigenic shift and influenza pandemics; new rotavirus and bluetongue genotypes;
deliberate production of influenza vaccine seed viruses
Complementation Helper systems for defective viruses and vector vaccines; AAV and HDV biology;
complementation tests
Phenotypic mixing /
pseudotyping
Safe neutralisation assays for dangerous viruses; broad-host-range gene-therapy
vectors
Interference / DI particles END and interference tests for BVDV and CSFV; interference between live
vaccines; DI particles in persistence and vaccine yield; interferon-based antivirals
INDIA FOCUS: VIRUS INTERACTIONS — RELEVANCE TO INDIAN VETERINARY
PRACTICE
▪ Antigenic drift driven by mutation in foot-and-mouth disease virus is the reason the vaccine strains
used in the National Animal Disease Control Programme must be reviewed and updated by ICAR–
NIFMD, Bhubaneswar against circulating field lineages (e.g. serotype O lineage Ind-2001 sublineages).
▪ Bluetongue is highly endemic in India with many co-circulating serotypes, so reassortment —
including potential reassortment between field viruses and live vaccine strains — is a real concern and a
reason for preferring inactivated multivalent BTV vaccines.
▪ Non-cytopathic BVDV and classical swine fever virus are detected in Indian diagnostic laboratories
using interference-based tests (the exaltation of Newcastle disease virus principle) alongside antigen
ELISA and RT-PCR.
Chapter 10 | Page 127



VMC 503: General Virology
▪ The possibility of influenza reassortment in pigs, which carry both avian- and human-type sialic acid
receptors, is watched in India’s avian-influenza surveillance because of the country’s large poultry and
pig populations and their close contact with people.
▪ Recombination and reversion are relevant to the safety of the many live vaccines used in India (PPR,
capripox, Newcastle disease, IBD, classical swine fever); passaging vaccine viruses at low multiplicity
and quality control help to limit defective-interfering particles and reversion to virulence.
▪ Pseudotype (VSV-G-based) neutralisation assays are increasingly used in Indian research
laboratories to measure neutralising antibodies against high-containment viruses such as highly
pathogenic avian influenza and SARS-CoV-2 without handling the live agent.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Demonstration of the isolation of temperature-sensitive or plaque-size mutants.
▪ Demonstration of interference: the exaltation of Newcastle disease virus (END) test for non-cytopathic
BVDV or classical swine fever virus.
▪ Illustration of reassortment and recombination using coloured models or worked problems (e.g.
predicting progeny genotypes).
▪ Discussion of the von Magnus phenomenon and the effect of high- versus low-multiplicity passage on
virus yield.
▪ Case study: tracing the origin of a pandemic influenza virus or of porcine respiratory coronavirus.
10.11 Summary
• Virus–virus interactions are genetic (heritable — mutation, recombination, reassortment) or non-
genetic (not heritable — complementation, phenotypic mixing, transcapsidation, interference, DI
particles).
• Mutations arise from polymerase errors or mutagens; RNA viruses mutate far more than DNA
viruses, creating quasispecies and driving drift, drug resistance and attenuation; many types of
mutants exist, and revertants are a vaccine-safety concern.
• Recombination exchanges sequences within genomes (by breakage–rejoining or copy-choice); it
is common in DNA and positive-sense RNA viruses and rare in non-segmented negative-sense
RNA viruses, and has produced new viruses such as PRCV and cytopathic BVDV.
• Reassortment exchanges whole segments of segmented viruses and causes influenza antigenic
shift and pandemics, new rotavirus and bluetongue genotypes, and is used to make influenza
vaccine seed viruses.
• Complementation is the transient supply of a functional protein to a defective virus; phenotypic
mixing and transcapsidation give progeny the coat proteins of another virus without changing the
genotype.
• Interference (by interferon, receptor blockade, superinfection exclusion or DI particles) inhibits a
second virus and is exploited in the END test; DI particles are deleted genomes that reduce virus
yield and aid persistence.
• These interactions explain viral emergence and evolution and are important in vaccine design,
safety and production and in diagnosis.
Chapter 10 | Page 128



VMC 503: General Virology
10.12 Glossary
Table 10.5: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Mutation Heritable change in the nucleotide sequence of the genome.
Quasispecies Cloud of related genome variants produced by error-prone replication.
Temperature-sensitive mutant Mutant that replicates at a permissive but not a restrictive temperature.
Revertant Mutant that regains the wild-type phenotype.
Lethal mutagenesis Error catastrophe induced by mutagenic antivirals.
Recombination Exchange of nucleic acid sequences within a genome.
Copy-choice Template switching by the polymerase, the main recombination mechanism of
RNA viruses.
Multiplicity reactivation Recovery of infectious virus from two or more inactivated genomes.
Reassortment Exchange of whole genome segments between segmented viruses.
Antigenic drift Gradual antigenic change by mutation.
Antigenic shift Abrupt antigenic change by reassortment.
Complementation Supply of a functional protein by one virus to another, defective, virus.
Phenotypic mixing Acquisition of coat proteins of two viruses by a particle with one genome.
Transcapsidation / pseudotype Enclosure of one virus genome in the coat of another.
Interference Inhibition of one virus by another.
Defective interfering particle Deleted, non-replicating genome that interferes with the standard virus.
von Magnus phenomenon Fall in infectious yield on serial high-multiplicity passage due to DI particles.

UNIT III — VIRUS–HOST INTERACTIONS
CHAPTER 11
Virus–Cell Interactions
Lecture 19
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Classify the outcomes of virus infection of a cell — abortive, cytocidal, non-cytocidal (persistent), latent
and transforming.
▪ Distinguish permissive, semi-permissive and non-permissive cells and explain the cellular determinants
of susceptibility.
▪ Describe the mechanisms by which viruses damage and kill cells, including host shut-off, membrane
damage, syncytium formation and programmed cell death.
▪ Explain how cells sense viral infection and how viruses counteract these cellular defences.
▪ Relate virus–cell interactions to cytopathic effects in the laboratory and to lesions and disease in the
animal.
LECTURE 19: VIRUS–CELL INTERACTIONS
11.1 Introduction
The interaction between a virus and an individual cell is the foundation of viral disease. Whether a cell
is killed, altered, or apparently unaffected depends on both the virus (its replication strategy and the
proteins it makes) and the cell (its type, state of differentiation and division, and its defences). The same
virus may kill one cell type, persist in another and fail to replicate in a third. Understanding these
outcomes explains the cytopathic effects seen in cell culture (Chapter 5), the lesions seen at post-
mortem, and phenomena such as persistence, latency and transformation that are treated in later
chapters.
11.2 Susceptibility and Permissiveness
• Susceptible cell: has the receptors (and co-receptors) that allow a virus to attach and enter.
• Permissive cell: supports the complete replication cycle, producing infectious progeny. A cell
may be susceptible but non-permissive (the virus enters but cannot complete replication) or
semi-permissive (low yields).
• A cell must be both susceptible and permissive for a productive infection. For example, a
mouse cell engineered to express a viral receptor may become susceptible to a virus that it could
not previously enter; conversely, lack of an essential intracellular factor can block replication
after entry.
• Determinants of permissiveness: presence of transcription factors and splicing machinery, the
cell-cycle state (parvoviruses and papillomaviruses need dividing or differentiating cells),
proteases needed to activate viral proteins (e.g. trypsin-like proteases for low-pathogenic avian
influenza and lentogenic NDV), and the strength of the cell’s intrinsic and innate antiviral
defences (e.g. interferon responses).
• Tropism — the range of cells, tissues and hosts a virus infects — follows from receptor
distribution plus intracellular permissiveness (Chapter 12).
Chapter 11 | Page 134



VMC 503: General Virology

These categories are not rigid: a virus can cause cytocidal infection in one cell type and latent or
persistent infection in another (e.g. herpesviruses — lytic in epithelium, latent in neurons), and the
balance may shift with the host’s immune status.
Chapter 11 | Page 135



VMC 503: General Virology
11.4 Mechanisms of Virus-Induced Cell Damage
11.4.1 Inhibition of Host Macromolecular Synthesis ("Shut-off")
• Inhibition of host protein synthesis: many viruses shut down host translation to favour their
own mRNAs — e.g. the FMDV leader protease (Lpro) and enterovirus 2A protease cleave
eIF4G, blocking cap-dependent host translation while viral IRES-driven translation continues;
herpesvirus vhs protein degrades host mRNAs; influenza "cap-snatching" and NS1 impair host
mRNA processing.
• Inhibition of host transcription and RNA processing: some viruses degrade RNA polymerase
II or block mRNA splicing and export.
• Inhibition of host DNA synthesis and degradation of host chromosomal DNA (e.g. by some
poxviruses and herpesviruses).
• Host shut-off deprives the cell of essential proteins and prevents it from making interferon and
other defence molecules — so shut-off is both cytopathic and an immune-evasion strategy.
11.4.2 Damage to Cell Membranes and Cytoskeleton
• Syncytium (giant cell) formation: viral fusion proteins expressed on the surface of infected cells
fuse them with neighbouring cells to form multinucleated syncytia — a hallmark of
paramyxoviruses (PPRV, CDV, NDV), pneumoviruses (bovine RSV), herpesviruses and
some coronaviruses and retroviruses. Syncytia allow cell-to-cell spread that avoids
neutralising antibody.
• Altered membrane permeability: viral viroporins (small pore-forming proteins such as
influenza M2, picornavirus 2B, coronavirus E, rotavirus NSP4) change ion fluxes, disrupting
cellular homeostasis.
• Insertion of viral glycoproteins into the plasma membrane makes the cell a target for antibody-
dependent complement lysis and cytotoxic T lymphocytes — immune-mediated destruction
(Chapter 16).
• Cytoskeletal disruption — rounding and detachment of cells in culture (the classic CPE of
enteroviruses and FMDV) results from breakdown of actin filaments and microtubules.
11.4.3 Inclusion Bodies and Structural Changes
• Inclusion bodies are sites of viral replication or assembly, or accumulations of viral components,
that can be seen with the light microscope; they are diagnostically useful (Chapter 6).
• Intracytoplasmic: poxviruses (Guarnieri, Bollinger), rabies (Negri bodies — replication
factories), reoviruses and orbiviruses (viroplasm), paramyxoviruses.
• Intranuclear: herpesviruses (Cowdry type A), adenoviruses (basophilic in inclusion body
hepatitis), parvoviruses (CPV in crypt cells).
• Both: morbilliviruses (CDV, PPRV).
• Other structural changes include chromosomal aberrations (breaks, pulverisation), margination
of chromatin, nuclear pyknosis, cytoplasmic vacuolation and swelling of mitochondria and ER.
Chapter 11 | Page 136



VMC 503: General Virology
11.4.4 Programmed Cell Death
Much virus-induced cell death is not simple bursting but regulated (programmed) cell death, which
can be a host defence (killing an infected cell before the virus can spread) or be exploited by the virus to
release progeny.

11.5 Cellular Sensing of Viruses and Viral Countermeasures
Every cell has intrinsic and innate defences that detect viral components — especially nucleic acids in
unusual forms or locations — and trigger an antiviral response dominated by type I interferons
(detailed in Chapter 16).

VMC 503: General Virology
• Intrinsic restriction factors — host proteins that block specific steps of replication (e.g.
APOBEC3, tetherin, TRIM5α and SAMHD1 against retroviruses; Mx proteins against influenza)
— are constitutive or interferon-induced.
• Viral countermeasures are central to virulence: sequestering or modifying dsRNA (e.g.
replication inside membranes; dsRNA viruses transcribing within the core), cleaving or
degrading sensors and adaptors (e.g. picornavirus and coronavirus proteases cleaving MAVS),
blocking interferon induction or signalling (influenza NS1, paramyxovirus V and C proteins,
ASFV multigene-family proteins), inhibiting PKR (adenovirus VA RNA, vaccinia E3L and
K3L, reovirus σ3), and blocking apoptosis.
• Species specificity of antagonists — interferon antagonists often work best in the natural host,
contributing to host restriction (e.g. the paramyxovirus V protein).
11.6 Non-cytocidal Effects on Cell Function
• Some infections do not kill cells but impair specialised ("luxury") functions — e.g. hormone
or neurotransmitter synthesis — producing disease without visible cell damage. Rabies virus
causes severe neurological dysfunction with little neuronal death; persistent lymphocytic
choriomeningitis virus infection of mice reduces growth-hormone production.
• Immune tolerance: non-cytopathic BVDV infecting a foetus before about 120 days of gestation
establishes persistent infection (PI) with immunotolerance — the calf is seronegative and sheds
large amounts of virus throughout life; later superinfection with a homologous cytopathic strain
precipitates mucosal disease.
• Cellular transformation: oncogenic viruses alter growth control (loss of contact inhibition,
immortalisation, anchorage independence) — Chapter 14.
Chapter 11 | Page 138



VMC 503: General Virology
• Altered antigen expression: viral antigens on the cell surface, down-regulation of MHC class I
(a common immune-evasion strategy), and expression of Fc receptors or complement
regulators by some herpes- and poxviruses.

INDIA FOCUS: VIRUS–CELL INTERACTIONS IN INDIAN VETERINARY DISEASES
▪ Syncytia and lymphocyte apoptosis caused by PPR virus explain the erosive lesions and
immunosuppression that make PPR so damaging to India’s large goat and sheep population; syncytia in
Vero-SLAM cultures are used in Indian laboratories to isolate the virus.
▪ Canine parvoviral enteritis, a daily presentation in Indian small-animal clinics, reflects cytocidal
infection of dividing crypt cells and bone-marrow precursors; understanding this explains the
leucopenia used as a prognostic sign.
▪ Rabies kills thousands of animals and humans in India each year; the virus’s largely non-cytocidal
interaction with neurons explains why there are few gross lesions and why diagnosis relies on antigen
detection (FAT) rather than gross pathology.
▪ Persistently infected BVDV animals have been reported from Indian cattle and buffalo herds; because
PI animals are immunotolerant and seronegative, their detection requires antigen or RT-PCR testing
rather than serology.
▪ Infectious bursal disease (including very virulent strains) and Marek’s disease cause major losses in
Indian poultry through cytocidal and transforming effects on lymphoid cells, leading to
immunosuppression and vaccination failures against other diseases.
▪ Lumpy skin disease, first reported in India in 2019, produces cytoplasmic inclusion bodies and
extensive skin and mucosal necrosis — the cellular basis of the nodular lesions seen in the 2022
epidemic.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Observation of different types of CPE (rounding, syncytia, inclusion bodies) in infected cell cultures and
comparison with uninfected controls.
▪ Staining of infected monolayers to demonstrate inclusion bodies (H&E, Giemsa) and syncytia.
▪ Demonstration of apoptosis in virus-infected cells (e.g. by DNA fragmentation or acridine
Chapter 11 | Page 139



VMC 503: General Virology
orange/ethidium bromide staining).
▪ Comparison of cytocidal and non-cytocidal (e.g. non-cytopathic BVDV) infections — detection of the
latter by immunostaining.
▪ Study of histopathological slides showing virus-induced cell damage (parvoviral crypt necrosis, PPR
syncytia, Negri bodies).
11.8 Summary
• A productive infection requires a cell that is both susceptible (has receptors) and permissive
(supports replication); tropism reflects receptors and intracellular factors.
• Outcomes of infection are abortive, cytocidal, non-cytocidal persistent, latent or transforming,
and the same virus may produce different outcomes in different cells.
• Viruses damage cells by shutting off host synthesis, altering membranes (syncytia, viroporins),
disrupting the cytoskeleton, forming inclusion bodies and causing chromosomal damage.
• Programmed cell death — apoptosis, necroptosis, pyroptosis — and autophagy can be host
defences or be manipulated by viruses; lymphocyte apoptosis underlies virus-induced
immunosuppression.
• Cells sense viral nucleic acids through TLRs, RIG-I/MDA5, cGAS–STING and PKR, leading to
interferon; viruses counter these sensors with specific antagonists that determine virulence and
host range.
• Some infections impair specialised cell functions or induce immune tolerance without killing
cells (e.g. rabies, persistent BVDV).
• Virus–cell interactions explain CPE in the laboratory and the lesions and diseases seen in
animals.
11.9 Glossary
Table 11.5: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Susceptible cell Cell with receptors allowing virus attachment and entry.
Permissive cell Cell that supports complete virus replication.
Abortive infection Infection that yields no infectious progeny.
Cytocidal infection Productive infection that kills the cell.
Persistent infection Continuous virus production without cell death.
Latent infection Persistence of the viral genome without production of virus.
Host shut-off Inhibition of host macromolecular synthesis by a virus.
Syncytium Multinucleated cell formed by virus-induced fusion.
Viroporin Small viral protein forming ion channels in membranes.
Apoptosis Caspase-dependent, non-inflammatory programmed cell death.
Pyroptosis Inflammasome-driven inflammatory cell death.
Autophagy Cellular pathway that degrades cytoplasmic components.
Pattern-recognition receptor Host sensor that detects conserved microbial molecules.
Restriction factor Host protein that blocks a step of virus replication.
Immunotolerance Failure to respond immunologically to an antigen present during foetal life.
