UNIT III — VIRUS–HOST INTERACTIONS
CHAPTER 12
Viral Pathogenesis
Lectures 20 and 21
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Explain the stages of viral pathogenesis — entry, primary replication, spread, tissue tropism, secondary
replication, cell and tissue damage, and shedding.
▪ Describe the routes of entry and shedding of viruses and their epidemiological significance.
▪ Explain the mechanisms of viral spread — local, lymphatic, haematogenous (viraemia) and neural —
and the determinants of tropism.
▪ Describe the mechanisms of viral virulence and of tissue damage, including immunopathology and
immunosuppression.
▪ Analyse host and viral factors (age, genetics, nutrition, immunity, stress, virulence genes) that determine
the outcome of infection.
LECTURE 20: ENTRY, SPREAD AND TROPISM
12.1 Introduction
Pathogenesis is the process by which a virus causes disease in the whole animal. It builds on virus–cell
interactions (Chapter 11) but adds the complexity of the body — anatomical barriers, circulation, tissue
architecture and the immune system. The pathogenicity of a virus is its ability to cause disease;
virulence is the degree of pathogenicity, usually measured quantitatively (e.g. lethal dose 50, LD50;
intracerebral pathogenicity index for NDV). Viral pathogenesis follows a broadly predictable
sequence: entry → primary replication → spread → secondary replication in target organs → cell
and tissue injury (disease) → shedding and transmission → recovery, persistence or death.
Chapter 12 | Page 144



VMC 503: General Virology
12.2 Routes of Entry
To infect an animal, a virus must first breach the barriers that normally protect the body — intact
keratinised skin, mucus, cilia, acid in the stomach, bile, proteolytic enzymes, and local antibodies and
innate defences.
Table 12.1: Routes of entry of viruses
Route Barriers and how viruses overcome them Examples
Respiratory tract
Mucociliary clearance, alveolar macrophages, IgA.
Viruses that bind epithelial receptors infect before
clearance; droplet size determines site (large →
upper tract; small aerosols → lower tract)
Influenza, NDV, IBV, BoHV-1,
PPRV, CDV, FMDV (aerosol),
bovine RSV

usually non-enveloped and acid- and bile-

(rotavirus VP4 cleavage by trypsin)

Chapter 12 | Page 145



VMC 503: General Virology
12.3 Primary Replication and Local Infection
• After entry, viruses replicate in cells at the portal of entry (primary replication). Some infections
remain localised to the epithelial surface — e.g. many respiratory (rhinoviruses, many
respiratory coronaviruses) and enteric viruses (rotaviruses, coronaviruses of the gut) and
papillomaviruses of the skin — producing disease without systemic spread. These typically
have short incubation periods (1–4 days).
• Local spread occurs cell to cell and via secretions over the mucosal surface.
• Polarised release from epithelial cells determines spread: viruses released from the apical
surface (e.g. influenza) tend to stay local and are shed, while those released from the basolateral
surface can reach the lymph and blood and cause systemic infection (e.g. some morbilliviruses).
• The protease environment can restrict infection to certain sites: low-pathogenic avian influenza
and lentogenic NDV have HA/F proteins cleavable only by trypsin-like enzymes of the
respiratory and intestinal tracts, so infection stays local; HPAI and velogenic NDV with
multibasic cleavage sites are activated by furin in most tissues and spread systemically.
12.4 Systemic Spread
12.4.1 Lymphatic Spread
• Viruses crossing the epithelium enter lymphatics and reach regional lymph nodes, where they
may be trapped by macrophages and dendritic cells — a key defence — or may replicate there
and spread onwards.
• Many viruses infect or are carried by dendritic cells, macrophages and lymphocytes ("Trojan
horse"), e.g. morbilliviruses (SLAM-positive lymphocytes), PRRSV and ASFV (macrophages),
BLV and MDV (lymphocytes).
12.4.2 Haematogenous Spread (Viraemia)
• Viraemia is the presence of virus in the blood. Virus may be free in plasma (e.g. picornaviruses,
flaviviruses, togaviruses) or cell-associated — in leucocytes (morbilliviruses, herpesviruses,
ASFV, CSFV, BLV, EIAV) or erythrocytes (bluetongue virus in RBC invaginations, which
prolongs viraemia and allows uptake by Culicoides).
• Primary viraemia — virus from the site of primary replication and lymph nodes enters the
blood, is often low and brief, and seeds the reticuloendothelial system (liver, spleen, lymph
nodes, bone marrow).
• Secondary viraemia — after further replication in these organs, larger amounts of virus enter the
blood and reach target organs (skin, brain, lung, placenta). The classic model (Fenner’s study of
mousepox/ectromelia) established this primary–secondary viraemia sequence.
• Free virus is removed from blood by the reticuloendothelial system; persistence of viraemia
depends on the rate of virus production versus clearance, and on whether the virus is cell-
associated.
• Crossing blood vessel walls into tissues occurs by infection of endothelial cells, by passage
between them (e.g. at sites of inflammation), or within infected leucocytes that migrate into
tissues.
• Plasma viraemia is essential for transmission by arthropod vectors (arboviruses must reach a
high titre to infect a feeding insect or tick).
Chapter 12 | Page 146



VMC 503: General Virology
12.4.3 Neural Spread
• Some viruses travel within peripheral nerves to and from the CNS by axonal transport:
retrograde (towards the cell body — entry into the CNS) and anterograde (away from the cell
body — spread to epithelium during reactivation).
• Rabies virus enters peripheral nerve endings at the bite site (often after local muscle replication),
moves centripetally by retrograde axonal transport (tens of millimetres per day) to the spinal cord
and brain, then centrifugally to the salivary glands, where it is shed. The long and variable
incubation period reflects the distance from bite to brain.
• Alphaherpesviruses (BoHV-1, pseudorabies virus, HSV) use axons to establish latency in
sensory ganglia and return to epithelium on reactivation; pseudorabies virus in non-porcine
hosts ascends nerves to cause "mad itch".
• Other routes to the CNS: across the blood–brain barrier (via infected endothelium, choroid
plexus, or infected leucocytes — e.g. flaviviruses such as JEV and West Nile virus, CDV,
CAEV, MVV) and via the olfactory nerve (e.g. some influenza and Nipah viruses).
12.5 Tissue and Host Tropism
• Tropism is the specificity of a virus for particular host species, tissues or cells. It is determined
by:
◦ Receptors (e.g. SLAM on lymphocytes and nectin-4 on epithelia for morbilliviruses;
transferrin receptor on dividing cells for CPV-2; α2,3 vs α2,6 sialic acid for avian vs
mammalian influenza; CD163 on macrophages for PRRSV).
◦ Intracellular permissiveness (transcription factors, cell cycle state, differentiation — e.g.
papillomaviruses only in differentiating keratinocytes).
◦ Proteases that activate viral glycoproteins (HPAI vs LPAI; velogenic vs lentogenic NDV).
◦ Temperature — some respiratory viruses replicate best at the cooler temperature of the
upper airway (a principle used in cold-adapted live vaccines).
◦ Local innate immunity (interferon responses differ between tissues).
◦ Route of entry and anatomical access.
• Changes in tropism through mutation can dramatically alter disease — e.g. feline enteric
coronavirus → FIP virus (gain of macrophage tropism), transmissible gastroenteritis virus →
porcine respiratory coronavirus (loss of enteric tropism by spike deletion), and emergence of
CPV-2 from a feline panleukopenia-like virus through capsid changes allowing use of the canine
transferrin receptor.
LECTURE 21: TISSUE INJURY, SHEDDING AND DETERMINANTS OF OUTCOME
12.6 Mechanisms of Tissue Injury and Disease
12.6.1 Direct Viral Damage
• Cytocidal infection of cells in the target organ is often the main cause of disease — e.g.
destruction of crypt epithelium by CPV-2 (loss of villi, haemorrhagic enteritis, malabsorption),
villous enterocytes by rotaviruses and coronaviruses (malabsorptive and secretory diarrhoea),
keratinocytes by FMDV (vesicles), B lymphocytes of the bursa by IBDV.
• Functional impairment without cell death — e.g. rabies neuronal dysfunction.
Chapter 12 | Page 147



VMC 503: General Virology
• Loss of epithelial barrier allows secondary bacterial infection — a major contributor to
disease in viral respiratory infections (bovine respiratory disease complex, where BoHV-1,
BRSV, PI-3 and BVDV predispose to Mannheimia haemolytica and Pasteurella multocida; and
PPR with secondary pasteurellosis).
• Viral toxins — rotavirus NSP4 acts as an enterotoxin, inducing chloride secretion and
diarrhoea.
• Vascular damage — infection of endothelium causes haemorrhage, oedema and thrombosis in
haemorrhagic fevers — e.g. ASF, CSF, bluetongue (oedema, haemorrhage, cyanotic tongue),
African horse sickness (pulmonary oedema), CCHF in humans.
12.6.2 Immunopathology
Much tissue damage in viral disease is caused not by the virus itself but by the host immune response
to it.

12.6.3 Virus-Induced Immunosuppression
• Many viruses infect or destroy immune cells, leaving the animal susceptible to secondary
infections and vaccine failure.
• Examples: IBDV (destruction of bursal B cells — chicks fail to respond to vaccines), Marek’s
disease virus, chicken anaemia virus, CDV and PPRV (lymphocyte depletion via SLAM),
BVDV (leucopenia, thymic atrophy), PRRSV (macrophage impairment), PCV2 (lymphoid
depletion), FIV and FeLV (retroviral immunodeficiency), rinderpest historically.
• Mechanisms: lysis or apoptosis of lymphocytes, infection of antigen-presenting cells, production
of immunosuppressive cytokines (e.g. IL-10), MHC down-regulation, and destruction of
lymphoid architecture.
Chapter 12 | Page 148



VMC 503: General Virology
12.7 Shedding and Transmission
• Shedding — release of infectious virus from the host — is essential for the virus to persist in the
population. The route of shedding usually mirrors the route of entry for localised infections, but
systemic infections may be shed by several routes.
Table 12.3: Routes of shedding
Route Examples
Respiratory secretions and
aerosols Influenza, NDV, FMDV (large quantities from pigs), PPRV, CDV, IBV
Faeces Rotaviruses, coronaviruses, CPV-2 (billions of particles per gram),
enteroviruses, IBDV
Saliva Rabies (via bite), FMDV, feline calicivirus
Skin lesions and scabs Poxviruses (sheeppox, goatpox, LSD, orf), FMDV vesicles
Urine Leptospirosis-like pattern in some viruses; Nipah and Hendra (bats), canine
adenovirus 1
Milk FMDV (before clinical signs), BLV (in infected lymphocytes), CAEV, MVV,
tick-borne encephalitis virus
Semen BoHV-1, EAV (carrier stallions), BVDV, BTV, PRRSV
Blood (to arthropods and via
iatrogenic routes) Arboviruses (BTV, JEV, AHSV), EIAV (via biting flies and needles), BLV
Vertical (placenta, egg) BVDV, porcine parvovirus, avian leukosis virus and chicken anaemia virus
(egg)
• Shedding may begin before clinical signs (e.g. FMD, rabies — days before symptoms) and
continue after recovery, which complicates control.
• Carriers — animals that shed without disease (persistent or latent infections) — are critical
reservoirs (Chapter 13).
Chapter 12 | Page 149



VMC 503: General Virology
12.8 Determinants of the Outcome of Infection
12.8.1 Viral Factors (Virulence Determinants)
• Receptor usage and tropism (e.g. host adaptation of influenza HA).
• Protease cleavage sites of fusion glycoproteins (HPAI H5/H7 HA; NDV F0).
• Replication speed and yield; polymerase fidelity.
• Immune-evasion genes — interferon antagonists (influenza NS1, paramyxovirus V, ASFV
multigene families), MHC down-regulation, cytokine mimics (poxvirus virokines), anti-
apoptotic genes.
• Cell-to-cell spread and latency functions (herpesvirus gE, TK genes — deletion of which
attenuates vaccine strains).
• Viral dose and route of exposure.
• Virulence is multigenic and often the result of interaction between viral genes and host
factors; attenuated vaccine strains usually carry mutations in several genes.
12.8.2 Host Factors
Table 12.4: Host factors influencing susceptibility and severity
Factor Effect and examples
Species and genetics
Host range; breed differences (e.g. indigenous vs exotic cattle to bluetongue;
resistance of certain chicken lines (B21 MHC haplotype) to Marek’s disease); genetic
polymorphisms (e.g. PRNP in prion diseases, Chapter 7)
Age
Neonates are highly susceptible to enteric and systemic viruses (immature immunity,
lack of maternal antibody); foetal infection causes malformations or
immunotolerance (BVDV, BTV, Akabane); old age increases severity of some
infections
Immune status
Maternal antibodies protect young but interfere with vaccination; prior infection or
vaccination; immunosuppression (other viruses, corticosteroids, stress) increases
severity and reactivates latent viruses
Nutrition
Malnutrition (protein, vitamin A, zinc, selenium deficiencies) impairs immunity;
selenium deficiency can increase virulence of some RNA viruses by favouring
mutation
Hormones and
pregnancy
Pregnancy increases susceptibility to some viruses and leads to abortion;
corticosteroids reactivate herpesviruses
Stress Transport, overcrowding, weaning and cold stress precipitate outbreaks (e.g. bovine
respiratory disease complex — "shipping fever")
Concurrent infections Viral–bacterial and viral–viral synergy (e.g. PCV2 with PRRSV; IBDV predisposing
to other poultry infections)
Temperature and fever Fever may inhibit replication of some viruses; body temperature restricts cold-
adapted strains

The "iceberg" concept: for many viral infections, clinical cases represent only the tip of the iceberg,
with most infections being subclinical — important for understanding epidemiology and the value of
serosurveillance (Chapter 15).
INDIA FOCUS: VIRAL PATHOGENESIS AND DISEASE CONTROL IN INDIA
▪ Rabies: India bears a large share of the global burden of human rabies, almost all from dog bites;
understanding neural spread underpins India’s emphasis on wound washing, post-exposure
vaccination and rabies immunoglobulin, and the national goal of eliminating dog-mediated human
rabies by 2030 under the National Action Plan for Dog-Mediated Rabies Elimination.
▪ Viraemia and vectors: bluetongue, Japanese encephalitis and other arboviruses depend on viraemia for
transmission by Culicoides and mosquitoes; seasonal outbreaks after the monsoon in India reflect vector
abundance. Bluetongue’s association with erythrocytes explains the long viraemia in cattle, which act as
silent reservoirs for sheep.
▪ Immunosuppression: diseases such as PPR, IBD, Marek’s disease and PCV2 explain many apparent
vaccine failures and secondary bacterial infections seen in Indian flocks and herds; vaccination against
the immunosuppressive disease is often a prerequisite for successful control of others.
▪ Host factors: crossbred and exotic cattle in India tend to suffer more severe disease from several viral
infections (e.g. bluetongue in exotic sheep breeds, LSD and FMD in high-yielding crossbreds) than
indigenous zebu breeds — relevant to breeding and vaccination priorities.
▪ Shedding before signs: FMD virus is shed in milk and breath before lesions appear, and animal
movements through markets (haats) and seasonal migration of small ruminants in states such as
Rajasthan spread FMD and PPR — an important consideration in movement control and vaccination
timing.
Chapter 12 | Page 151



VMC 503: General Virology
▪ Vertical and congenital infection: abortion and congenital defects caused by BVDV, bluetongue and
Akabane-like viruses are reported in India and may be under-recognised because foetal infections are
rarely investigated.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Determination of the LD50 of a virus (demonstration or worked problem) and the intracerebral
pathogenicity index (ICPI) of NDV.
▪ Study of histopathological slides illustrating pathogenesis: crypt necrosis (CPV-2), villous atrophy
(rotavirus), vasculitis (BTV), syncytia and lymphoid depletion (PPR), Negri bodies (rabies).
▪ Tracing virus spread in an experimental model (demonstration of virus titres in different organs at
different times after infection).
▪ Collection of samples for demonstrating viraemia (EDTA blood) and shedding (swabs, faeces).
▪ Case discussions: relating clinical signs and lesions to the pathogenesis of FMD, PPR, rabies,
bluetongue and IBD.
12.10 Summary
• Pathogenesis is the process by which a virus causes disease; virulence is the degree of
pathogenicity (e.g. LD50, ICPI).
• Viruses enter through the respiratory, alimentary, urogenital and conjunctival mucosae, through
the skin (wounds, bites, arthropods), vertically across the placenta, or iatrogenically.
• After primary replication at the portal of entry, infections may remain localised or spread by
lymphatics, blood (primary and secondary viraemia, free or cell-associated) or nerves (retrograde
and anterograde axonal transport).
• Tropism depends on receptors, intracellular permissiveness, activating proteases, temperature
and local immunity; changes in tropism can create new diseases (FIP, PRCV, CPV-2).
• Tissue injury results from direct cytopathology, vascular damage, viral toxins, secondary
bacterial infection, immunopathology (CTL damage, immune complexes, ADE, cytokine storm)
and immunosuppression.
• Viruses are shed in respiratory secretions, faeces, saliva, skin lesions, urine, milk, semen, blood
and vertically; shedding may precede signs and continue in carriers.
• Outcome depends on viral virulence determinants and host factors — species, genetics, age,
immunity, nutrition, pregnancy, stress and concurrent infection.
12.11 Glossary
Table 12.6: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Pathogenicity Ability of a virus to cause disease.
Virulence Degree of pathogenicity of a virus strain.
LD50 Dose that kills 50% of inoculated animals.
Portal of entry Site where a virus first enters and replicates in the host.
Viraemia Presence of virus in the blood.
Primary viraemia Initial, low-level viraemia seeding the reticuloendothelial system.
Secondary viraemia Larger viraemia carrying virus to target organs.
Retrograde axonal transport Movement along an axon towards the neuronal cell body.
Tropism Specificity of a virus for particular hosts, tissues or cells.
Chapter 12 | Page 152



VMC 503: General Virology
Term Meaning
Immunopathology Tissue damage caused by the host immune response.
Antibody-dependent
enhancement Increased infection due to non-neutralising antibody.
Cytokine storm Excessive, damaging release of inflammatory cytokines.
Immunosuppression Reduced immune function caused by a virus or other factor.
Shedding Release of infectious virus from an infected host.
Carrier Animal that sheds virus without showing disease.
Iceberg concept Clinical cases represent only a small proportion of all infections.

UNIT III — VIRUS–HOST INTERACTIONS
CHAPTER 13
Viral Persistence
Lecture 22
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Define persistent infection and distinguish chronic, latent and slow infections from acute infections.
▪ Describe the viral and host mechanisms that allow viruses to persist and evade immunity.
▪ Explain latency and reactivation, with herpesviruses and retroviruses as models.
▪ Describe important persistent infections of animals — BVDV persistent infection, lentiviral infections,
carrier states in FMD and others.
▪ Discuss the epidemiological, diagnostic and control implications of persistent infections, including
carriers and trade.
LECTURE 22: PERSISTENT, LATENT AND SLOW INFECTIONS
13.1 Introduction
Most acute viral infections end within days or weeks, either with recovery and clearance of the virus
by the immune system or with death. In persistent infections, however, the virus is not eliminated
and remains in the host for months, years or life, with or without disease and with or without shedding.
Persistence is important because persistently infected animals are reservoirs that maintain viruses in
populations, spread infection to susceptible animals (including across borders through trade), may
develop late disease (tumours, immune-mediated disease, degenerative disease), and complicate
diagnosis and eradication.

13.3 Mechanisms of Viral Persistence
To persist, a virus must (1) avoid killing its host cell (or infect cells that are continuously renewed), (2)
evade immune clearance, and (3) maintain its genome in a stable form.
13.3.1 Restricted Cytopathology
• Many persistent viruses are non-cytocidal in the cells they persist in — e.g. non-cytopathic
BVDV, lentiviruses in macrophages, latent herpesviruses in neurons.
• Restricted gene expression: latent herpesviruses express only a few latency-associated
transcripts or proteins; proviral retroviruses may be transcriptionally silent.
• Defective interfering particles and temperature-sensitive variants may reduce cytopathology
and favour persistence (Chapter 10).
13.3.2 Evasion of Immune Responses
Table 13.2: Mechanisms by which persistent viruses evade immunity
Mechanism Examples
Infection of immunologically
privileged sites
Neurons (herpesvirus latency, rabies), CNS, kidney, testes and semen (EAV in
carrier stallions), oropharyngeal epithelium (FMD carriers)
Immune tolerance BVDV infection of the foetus before immune competence (~<120 days) →
lifelong specific tolerance; congenital avian leukosis virus infection
Antigenic variation EIAV and maedi–visna virus generate new envelope variants that escape
neutralising antibody, producing recurrent episodes (EIA fever cycles)

VMC 503: General Virology

13.3.3 Maintenance of the Viral Genome
• Episomes — circular genomes in the nucleus (herpesviruses, papillomaviruses) replicated with
the host chromosome or silently maintained in non-dividing cells (neurons).
• Integrated proviruses — retroviruses (and some herpesviruses such as MDV at telomeres)
integrate into host DNA.
• Cytoplasmic persistence — RNA viruses such as BVDV and some paramyxoviruses persist by
continuous low-level replication in the cytoplasm.
• cccDNA — hepadnaviruses maintain covalently closed circular DNA in hepatocyte nuclei.
13.4 Latency and Reactivation: Herpesviruses
• Alphaherpesviruses (BoHV-1, pseudorabies virus, EHV-1, FeHV-1, CaHV-1) establish latency
in sensory ganglia (trigeminal ganglia after respiratory/ocular infection; sacral ganglia after
genital infection) after travelling up sensory axons. Gammaherpesviruses (e.g. ovine
herpesvirus 2 of malignant catarrhal fever, EHV-2) establish latency in lymphocytes;
betaherpesviruses in monocytes and other cells.
• During latency the genome persists as a circular episome; lytic genes are silenced (host
chromatin/histone modifications), and only latency-associated transcripts (LATs) or latency-
related (LR) gene products are expressed; these promote neuronal survival (anti-apoptosis) and
help maintain latency. No infectious virus is produced, so latently infected animals are
seropositive but not shedding and are invisible to the immune system.
• Reactivation is triggered by stress, corticosteroids (natural or administered), transport,
parturition, intercurrent disease, weaning and immunosuppression. Lytic genes are re-
expressed, virus is transported anterogradely down the axon to the epithelium, and is shed —
often with mild or no clinical signs — infecting in-contact animals.
• Consequences: "once infected, always infected" — every seropositive animal is a potential
carrier; herd infection is maintained through generations; introduction of seropositive animals
can introduce infection; and dexamethasone treatment is used experimentally (and in some
regulatory testing) to reactivate latent virus.
• Control implications: gE-deleted marker vaccines with companion DIVA ELISA allow
detection and removal of field-virus carriers (BoHV-1 and PRV eradication in Europe);
Chapter 13 | Page 158



VMC 503: General Virology
vaccination reduces shedding on reactivation but does not prevent latency by field virus;
serological testing of breeding bulls and semen for BoHV-1 at semen stations.
13.5 Important Persistent Infections of Animals
13.5.1 Bovine Viral Diarrhoea Virus: Persistent Infection and Mucosal Disease
• Infection of a pregnant cow with non-cytopathic (ncp) BVDV between about 40 and 120 days
of gestation, before the foetal immune system is competent, results in the birth of a persistently
infected (PI) calf that is immunotolerant to that virus strain.
• PI animals shed enormous amounts of virus in all secretions and excretions throughout life and
are the main source of BVDV in herds; they are usually seronegative (antibody-negative) and
may appear normal or be ill-thrifty.
• A PI animal that later becomes superinfected with, or generates by mutation/recombination, a
cytopathic (cp) BVDV closely related to its own ncp virus develops mucosal disease — a fatal
disease with erosions throughout the alimentary tract.
• A PI heifer always gives birth to a PI calf — creating PI families.
• Diagnosis: because PI animals are seronegative, they are detected by antigen ELISA (Erns) on
ear-notch samples or serum, RT-PCR (including bulk-milk testing), confirmed by retesting
after 3–4 weeks (to distinguish from transient infection).
• Control (as in Scandinavian and several European eradication schemes): identification and
removal of PI animals, biosecurity, and vaccination of breeding females to prevent foetal
infection.
Chapter 13 | Page 159



VMC 503: General Virology
13.5.2 Lentiviruses: Slow Infections
• Maedi–visna virus (MVV) of sheep and caprine arthritis–encephalitis virus (CAEV) of goats
(together small ruminant lentiviruses, SRLVs) infect monocytes/macrophages; the provirus is
carried in monocytes, and virus expression increases when monocytes mature into macrophages
in tissues. Infection is lifelong; disease develops after a long incubation (months to years) as
progressive inflammatory lesions — interstitial pneumonia (maedi), leucoencephalomyelitis
(visna), arthritis and indurative mastitis ("hard udder"). Transmission is mainly via colostrum
and milk and respiratory secretions. Control relies on serological testing (ELISA, AGID) and
culling, and snatching kids/lambs at birth and feeding heat-treated colostrum.
• Equine infectious anaemia virus (EIAV) — lifelong infection with recurrent febrile episodes
as antigenic variants escape neutralising antibody; immune complexes and complement-
mediated destruction of erythrocytes cause anaemia; transmitted by biting flies (tabanids) and
iatrogenically. Inapparent carriers are detected by the Coggins (AGID) test and ELISA; control
is by test-and-removal (or strict isolation). No vaccine is used in most countries.
• Feline immunodeficiency virus and bovine immunodeficiency virus — lifelong infection with
slowly progressive immune dysfunction.

13.6 Implications of Persistent Infections
Table 13.4: Consequences of persistence for disease control
Area Implication
Epidemiology Persistently infected and latent animals are reservoirs that maintain viruses in herds
and introduce them into new ones; they explain outbreaks without an obvious source
Diagnosis
Immunotolerant PI animals are seronegative (need antigen or PCR tests); latent
animals are seropositive but not shedding (serology or PCR on ganglia); carriers may
need special samples (probang for FMD, semen for EAV)
Chapter 13 | Page 160



VMC 503: General Virology
Area Implication
Trade and movement International rules require testing, quarantine or certification to prevent movement of
carriers (FMD, EIA, BoHV-1, BVDV, EAV in semen)
Vaccination Vaccines rarely prevent establishment of persistence completely; marker vaccines
and DIVA tests are needed for eradication of latent infections
Eradication Requires identification and removal of carriers (test-and-remove), not just
vaccination (e.g. BVDV and BoHV-1 eradication in Europe, EIA control)
Late disease Persistence can lead to tumours (Chapter 14), immune-complex disease, degenerative
CNS disease or recrudescence years after infection
Biologicals safety Persistent contaminants (e.g. ncp BVDV in foetal bovine serum and cell lines) can
contaminate vaccines and diagnostic reagents
INDIA FOCUS: PERSISTENT VIRAL INFECTIONS IN INDIA
▪ IBR (BoHV-1): serological surveys in India have repeatedly reported high seroprevalence in cattle and
buffaloes, reflecting lifelong latency; because breeding bulls can reactivate and shed virus in semen,
Minimum Standards for semen production in India require testing of bulls at semen stations for IBR
and other infections.
▪ BVDV: persistently infected animals and BVDV antibodies have been detected in Indian cattle, buffalo,
sheep and goat populations; ncp BVDV is also a recognised contaminant of foetal bovine serum used
in cell culture and vaccine production, so Indian laboratories and manufacturers screen serum and cell
lines.
▪ FMD carriers: the FMD carrier state in cattle and buffaloes (India has the world’s largest buffalo
population) is an important consideration in the NADCP; NSP-based DIVA serosurveillance by ICAR–
NIFMD helps identify areas of continuing virus circulation in vaccinated populations.
▪ Equine infectious anaemia was recorded in India in the late 1980s and 1990s; ICAR–National
Research Centre on Equines (NRCE), Hisar, conducts national surveillance using ELISA and the
Coggins (AGID) test, and India has pursued freedom from EIA through test-and-removal of positive
equids.
▪ Small ruminant lentiviruses (CAEV/MVV) have been reported in Indian goats and sheep, mainly in
organised and exotic-crossbred flocks; given the size of India’s small-ruminant population, including in
Rajasthan, serological screening before introduction of breeding stock is advisable.
▪ Bovine leukaemia virus infection has been reported in Indian dairy herds, and Marek’s disease and
avian leukosis remain important in commercial poultry because of their persistent, lifelong nature.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Detection of BVDV persistent infection by antigen-capture ELISA on ear-notch samples and by RT-
PCR; importance of repeat testing.
▪ Coggins (AGID) test for equine infectious anaemia (demonstration).
▪ Serological testing (ELISA) for BoHV-1 and interpretation in latently infected herds; gE DIVA ELISA.
▪ Demonstration of probang sampling for detection of FMD carriers (video/demonstration).
▪ Discussion of screening of foetal bovine serum and cell lines for adventitious BVDV.
13.7 Summary
• Persistent infections are those in which the virus is not cleared; they include chronic (productive),
latent, slow and transforming infections and late complications of acute infection.
• Persistence requires restricted cytopathology, immune evasion and stable maintenance of the
genome (episomes, proviruses, cytoplasmic replication, cccDNA).
Chapter 13 | Page 161



VMC 503: General Virology
• Immune evasion mechanisms include infection of privileged sites, immune tolerance, antigenic
variation, latency, MHC down-regulation, infection of immune cells, cytokine interference, cell-
to-cell spread and integration.
• Alphaherpesviruses establish latency in sensory ganglia and reactivate with stress,
corticosteroids, transport or parturition, making every seropositive animal a potential carrier.
• Foetal infection with ncp BVDV produces immunotolerant PI calves that shed virus continuously
and may develop mucosal disease; lentiviruses (MVV, CAEV, EIAV) cause slow, lifelong
infections.
• Carrier states in FMD, EAV and others affect trade and disease-free status.
• Persistence has major implications for epidemiology, diagnosis, vaccination, eradication and
biologicals safety.
13.8 Glossary
Table 13.5: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Persistent infection Infection in which the virus is not eliminated from the host.
Chronic infection Persistent infection with continuous production of virus.
Latent infection Persistence of the viral genome without production of infectious virus.
Slow infection Infection with a very long incubation and slowly progressive disease.
Reactivation Resumption of virus production from a latent genome.
Latency-associated transcript RNA expressed from latent herpesvirus genomes.
Immune tolerance Specific unresponsiveness to an antigen encountered in foetal life.
PI animal Persistently infected, immunotolerant animal (e.g. BVDV).
Mucosal disease Fatal disease of BVDV PI cattle following exposure to homologous cytopathic
virus.
Carrier Animal harbouring and potentially transmitting virus without clinical disease.
Probang sample Oesophageal–pharyngeal fluid sample used to detect FMD carriers.
Antigenic variation Change in viral antigens allowing escape from immunity.
Episome Circular viral genome maintained in the nucleus.
Small ruminant lentivirus Maedi–visna virus and caprine arthritis–encephalitis virus.

CHAPTER 14
Oncogenic and Oncolytic Viruses

LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Trace the history of tumour virology and its contribution to understanding cancer.
▪ Describe the properties of virus-transformed cells and the concepts of oncogenes and tumour suppressor
genes.

▪ Describe important virus-induced tumours of animals and their control.

LECTURE 23: TUMOUR VIROLOGY AND ONCOGENIC DNA VIRUSES
14.1 Introduction
About 10–15% of human cancers worldwide are attributed to viral infections, and viruses cause
economically important tumours in poultry, cattle, sheep, goats, cats and horses. Viruses that can
cause tumours are called oncogenic (or tumour) viruses. Study of these viruses — especially the avian
and murine retroviruses — led to the discovery of oncogenes and tumour suppressor genes, which
underlie almost all cancers, including those not caused by viruses. Conversely, some viruses
preferentially infect and kill tumour cells; these oncolytic viruses are now used as cancer therapies.
Viral oncogenesis is usually a rare outcome of infection: most infected hosts do not develop tumours,
the latent period is long, and additional host genetic changes, co-factors (e.g. bracken fern with bovine
papillomavirus) and immunosuppression are usually required. Tumour induction is a "dead end" for the
virus and is not part of its normal replication strategy.
14.2 Milestones in Tumour Virology
Table 14.1: Landmarks in the history of tumour virology
Year Milestone
1908 Ellermann and Bang transmitted chicken leukaemia with cell-free filtrates — the first virus-
induced tumour
1911 Peyton Rous transmitted a chicken sarcoma by cell-free filtrate (Rous sarcoma virus); Nobel
Prize 1966
1933 Richard Shope described the rabbit (cottontail) papillomavirus — the first mammalian
tumour virus
1936 Bittner discovered the mouse mammary tumour virus transmitted through milk
1951 Ludwik Gross described murine leukaemia virus transmitted vertically in mice
1964 Epstein, Achong and Barr discovered Epstein–Barr virus in Burkitt’s lymphoma cells
1967–1970 Marek’s disease shown to be caused by a herpesvirus; turkey herpesvirus (HVT) vaccine
introduced (~1970) — the first vaccine against a naturally occurring cancer
1970 Temin and Baltimore independently discovered reverse transcriptase (Nobel Prize 1975,
with Dulbecco)
1976 Bishop and Varmus showed that v-src of RSV was derived from a normal cellular gene

VMC 503: General Virology
Year Milestone
(proto-oncogene) (Nobel Prize 1989)
1979 Discovery of p53 as a cellular protein bound to SV40 large T antigen
1983–1984 zur Hausen identified HPV-16 and HPV-18 in cervical cancer (Nobel Prize 2008)
2006 First HPV vaccine licensed for prevention of cervical cancer
2015 Talimogene laherparepvec (T-VEC), an oncolytic HSV-1, approved by US FDA for
melanoma
14.3 Virus-Induced Cell Transformation
Transformation is the heritable change in the growth properties of a cell produced by a virus (or other
agent). Transformed cells in culture show many properties of tumour cells and may form tumours when
inoculated into suitable animals.
Table 14.2: Properties of virus-transformed cells in culture
Property Description
Loss of contact inhibition Cells continue to divide after reaching confluence, piling up to form foci
(basis of the focus assay)
Anchorage independence Ability to grow in soft agar as colonies
Reduced serum (growth
factor) requirement Autocrine production of growth factors or constitutive signalling
Immortalisation Unlimited division (loss of senescence; telomerase activation)
Altered morphology Rounded or spindle shapes, disorganised cytoskeleton
Genomic instability Chromosomal abnormalities
Tumorigenicity Formation of tumours in immunodeficient (nude) mice or syngeneic animals
Viral genome and antigens Persistence of viral DNA (integrated or episomal) and expression of viral
tumour (T) antigens or oncoproteins
14.3.1 Oncogenes and Tumour Suppressor Genes
• Proto-oncogenes (c-onc) are normal cellular genes that regulate growth and differentiation
(growth factors, receptors, signal transducers, transcription factors). When mutated, over-
expressed or captured by a retrovirus (v-onc) they become oncogenes that drive uncontrolled
proliferation — they act dominantly ("accelerator stuck on").
• Tumour suppressor genes (e.g. p53 and retinoblastoma protein, pRb) restrain the cell cycle,
repair DNA or trigger apoptosis. Their loss or inactivation permits proliferation — they act
recessively ("brakes removed").
• Oncogenic DNA viruses mainly act by inactivating tumour suppressors with viral
oncoproteins; retroviruses mainly act by introducing or activating oncogenes.
14.4 Oncogenic DNA Viruses
Small DNA tumour viruses need the host cell to be in S phase to replicate their DNA. Their early
proteins therefore push resting cells into the cell cycle by binding pRb and blocking p53-mediated
apoptosis. In permissive cells this leads to virus replication and death; in non-permissive cells (or
when the genome integrates or persists without completing replication) the cell survives with the cell
cycle permanently "switched on" — transformation.

14.4.1 Bovine Papillomatosis and Enzootic Haematuria
• Bovine papillomatosis (warts) is common in young cattle; lesions on the head, neck, teats and
genitalia usually regress spontaneously through cell-mediated immunity within months. At least
30 BPV types are now recognised in several genera (Deltapapillomavirus: BPV-1, -2, -13, -14;
Xipapillomavirus: BPV-3, -4, -6, -9, -10, -11, -12; Epsilonpapillomavirus: BPV-5, -8, and
others).
• Co-carcinogenesis with bracken fern (Pteridium aquilinum): cattle grazing bracken in hilly
areas ingest immunosuppressive and mutagenic compounds (e.g. ptaquiloside). Persistent BPV-
2 infection of the bladder mucosa then progresses to bladder tumours with chronic enzootic
haematuria; BPV-4 papillomas of the oesophagus and forestomachs progress to squamous cell
carcinoma.
• Equine sarcoid — the commonest skin tumour of horses and donkeys — is associated with
BPV-1 and BPV-2, a rare example of cross-species infection causing a non-productive,
transforming infection.
• Treatment of extensive papillomatosis includes surgical removal, cryotherapy and autogenous
wart vaccines (formalinised tumour suspension), whose efficacy is variable.
14.4.2 Marek’s Disease
• Marek’s disease virus (MDV, Gallid alphaherpesvirus 2, genus Mardivirus) causes T-cell
lymphomas in chickens, with lymphomatous infiltration of nerves (enlarged sciatic nerve,
"range paralysis"), viscera, skin, muscle and eye ("grey eye").
• Pathogenesis: inhalation of feather-follicle dander → early cytolytic infection of B cells →
activated T cells infected → latency in CD4 T cells →	⁺ transformation of some T cells into
lymphoma. Virus is shed lifelong from feather follicle epithelium, where it is fully productive.
• Oncogenesis: the viral Meq oncoprotein (bZIP) dimerises with c-Jun and dysregulates host
genes; the MDV genome integrates near telomeres; viral telomerase RNA (vTR) and viral
miRNAs (e.g. miR-M4, an orthologue of cellular miR-155) contribute.
• Control by vaccination at hatch or in ovo with HVT (Meleagrid alphaherpesvirus 1), SB-1
(serotype 2) and CVI988/Rispens (attenuated serotype 1). Vaccines prevent tumours but not
infection or shedding of virulent virus ("imperfect" or "leaky" vaccines), which has been linked
to the evolution of increasing virulence of MDV pathotypes (v → vv → vv+).
LECTURE 24: ONCOGENIC RETROVIRUSES AND ONCOLYTIC VIRUSES
14.5 Oncogenic Retroviruses
Retroviruses integrate a DNA copy of their genome (the provirus) into host chromosomes, where it
persists for the life of the cell and its descendants. They cause tumours by three main mechanisms.

14.7 Control of Virus-Induced Tumours
• Vaccination: Marek’s disease (the first anti-cancer vaccine, ~1970); FeLV; human HPV and
hepatitis B vaccines are the most successful human anti-cancer vaccines.
• Eradication of vertically transmitted viruses from breeding stock: ALV-free primary breeder
flocks through testing and removal; BLV test-and-removal schemes.
• Management: prevent iatrogenic transmission of BLV (single-use needles, gloves), control
biting flies, avoid bracken-infested pastures, and use specific-pathogen-free (SPF) eggs for
production of poultry vaccines to avoid REV and ALV contamination.
• Genetic resistance: MHC B21 haplotype chickens are resistant to Marek’s disease; lines
resistant to ALV subgroups via receptor variants.
Chapter 14 | Page 171



VMC 503: General Virology
14.8 Oncolytic Viruses
Oncolytic viruses are viruses that preferentially replicate in and kill tumour cells while sparing
normal cells, and that also stimulate anti-tumour immunity. The idea arose from early 20th-century
observations of tumour regression in patients after natural virus infections (e.g. a leukaemia remission
after presumed influenza in 1904). Early trials with wild viruses in the 1950s–1970s were limited by
toxicity and immunity, but genetic engineering, together with an understanding of tumour-cell defects,
revived the field from the 1990s.
14.8.1 Basis of Tumour Selectivity
• Defective innate antiviral responses in tumour cells — many tumours have impaired
interferon signalling (e.g. PKR, STAT1 defects), so interferon-sensitive viruses (NDV, VSV,
vaccinia, measles) replicate in tumour cells but are controlled in normal cells.
• Activated oncogenic pathways — e.g. activated Ras signalling inhibits PKR, favouring
reovirus replication.
• Loss of tumour suppressors — viruses lacking genes that normally inactivate p53 or pRb (e.g.
E1B-55K-deleted or E1A-mutated adenoviruses) can only replicate in cells where these
pathways are already defective.
• Over-expressed receptors on tumour cells (e.g. CD46 for measles vaccine strains, nectin-4,
ICAM-1 for coxsackievirus A21).
• Engineered selectivity — deletion of virulence genes needed only in normal cells (HSV
ICP34.5 deletion; vaccinia TK deletion), tumour-specific promoters, and microRNA target sites.
14.8.2 Mechanisms of Anti-Tumour Action
1. Direct oncolysis — virus replication and lysis of tumour cells, with spread of progeny to
neighbouring tumour cells.
2. Immunogenic cell death — release of tumour antigens and danger signals (DAMPs, PAMPs)
that convert an immunologically "cold" tumour into a "hot", inflamed one, priming tumour-
specific T cells (in situ vaccination).
3. Expression of therapeutic transgenes — e.g. GM-CSF (in T-VEC and JX-594), cytokines,
checkpoint antibodies or prodrug-converting enzymes ("armed" viruses).
4. Vascular effects — infection of tumour endothelium and disruption of tumour blood supply.
5. Synergy with immune checkpoint inhibitors (anti-PD-1, anti-CTLA-4), radiotherapy and
chemotherapy.

14.8.4 Oncolytic Virotherapy in Veterinary Medicine
• Companion animals with spontaneous tumours (canine osteosarcoma, lymphoma, melanoma,
mammary tumours; feline fibrosarcoma) are valuable models for human cancer (comparative
oncology), and can themselves benefit from therapy.
• Viruses evaluated include canine adenovirus-based vectors, CDV, reovirus, vaccinia, NDV
and VSV in dogs, and oncolytic myxoma virus in several species.
• Veterinary virologists contribute the expertise and strains (e.g. NDV, CDV, avian and animal
adenoviruses) used in this field; biosafety is essential — e.g. avoiding release of velogenic NDV,
a notifiable poultry pathogen, and vaccine-derived viruses into animal populations.
14.8.5 Challenges
• Neutralising antibodies (pre-existing or induced) and rapid clearance after systemic delivery;
most products are therefore given intratumourally.
• Limited spread in dense tumour stroma; hypoxic, immunosuppressive tumour micro-
environment.
• Balancing safety (attenuation) with potency; environmental shedding and regulation as
genetically modified organisms.
• Manufacturing, standardisation and cost.
INDIA FOCUS: VIRAL TUMOURS AND ONCOLYTIC RESEARCH IN INDIA
▪ Marek’s disease remains a concern in the Indian poultry industry; outbreaks in vaccinated flocks have
been reported, attributed to very virulent MDV strains, vaccination failures (poor cold-chain
maintenance of cell-associated vaccines, early exposure) and immunosuppression. Bivalent HVT + SB-
1 and CVI988/Rispens vaccines are used in commercial chicks.
▪ Avian leukosis, including ALV-J, has been detected by Indian researchers in commercial and
indigenous chickens since the 2000s; because there is no vaccine, control depends on eradication
programmes by primary breeders. Contamination of poultry vaccines with REV and ALV is avoided by
using SPF eggs.
▪ Bovine papillomatosis is common in crossbred cattle in many states, and several BPV types have been
identified by molecular studies. Enzootic haematuria associated with bracken fern grazing and BPV-
2 has been described from hilly regions such as Himachal Pradesh (e.g. Kangra) and the north-eastern
and Darjeeling hills.
▪ Bovine leukaemia virus antibodies and proviral DNA have been reported in Indian cattle, generally at
lower prevalence than in intensive dairy systems elsewhere; testing of imported cattle and bulls is
important.
▪ Ovine pulmonary adenocarcinoma and enzootic nasal tumours have been recorded in Indian sheep
and goats, largely from pathology studies.
▪ Indian veterinary institutes have studied NDV strains for oncolytic activity against tumour cell lines,
contributing to comparative oncology research.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Gross and histopathological examination of Marek’s disease and lymphoid leukosis lesions; differential
diagnosis (nerve involvement, age, bursal tumours).
▪ Demonstration of PCR for MDV (Meq gene) and differentiation of vaccine and field strains.
▪ ELISA for ALV p27 antigen and AGID/ELISA for BLV antibodies.
Chapter 14 | Page 174



VMC 503: General Virology
▪ Demonstration of focus formation by transformed cells in culture (video/demonstration).
▪ Preparation of an autogenous wart vaccine (demonstration).
14.9 Summary
• Oncogenic viruses cause tumours in animals and humans, but tumour formation is a rare, late
outcome requiring co-factors.
• Tumour virology led to the discovery of oncogenes (Bishop and Varmus) and tumour suppressors
such as p53.
• Small DNA tumour viruses inactivate p53 and pRb (E6/E7, large T, E1A/E1B); BPV E5 activates
the PDGF receptor; MDV Meq drives T-cell lymphoma.
• Retroviruses cause tumours by transduction of v-onc, insertional activation of c-onc, trans-
activation (BLV Tax) or oncogenic Env (JSRV).
• Important animal viral tumours include Marek’s disease, avian leukosis, reticuloendotheliosis,
enzootic bovine leukosis, FeLV, OPA, papillomatosis and enzootic haematuria.
• Control is by vaccination (MD, FeLV), eradication from breeding stock (ALV, BLV) and
management.
• Oncolytic viruses selectively kill tumour cells and stimulate anti-tumour immunity; T-VEC,
H101 and G47Δ are approved products.
14.10 Glossary
Table 14.8: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Oncogenic virus A virus capable of causing tumours.
Transformation Heritable change of a cell to unregulated, tumour-like growth.
Proto-oncogene Normal cellular growth-regulating gene (c-onc).
Oncogene Altered or over-expressed gene that drives uncontrolled growth; v-onc if
carried by a virus.
Tumour suppressor gene Gene restraining cell proliferation, e.g. p53, Rb.
T antigen Tumour antigen expressed by polyomaviruses in transformed cells.
Insertional activation Activation of a cellular oncogene by an adjacent integrated provirus.
Acute transforming virus Retrovirus carrying a v-onc; causes tumours rapidly.
Tax Trans-activating protein of BLV and HTLV-1.
Meq Oncoprotein of Marek’s disease virus.
Enzootic haematuria Bladder tumours with haematuria in cattle grazing bracken, associated with
BPV-2.
Oncolytic virus Virus that selectively replicates in and kills tumour cells.
Immunogenic cell death Cell death that activates anti-tumour immunity.
Armed virus Oncolytic virus engineered to express a therapeutic transgene.
Comparative oncology Study of naturally occurring animal cancers as models for human cancer.

UNIT III — VIRUS–HOST INTERACTIONS
CHAPTER 15
Epidemiology of Viral Diseases
Lectures 25–26
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Define the basic terms and measures used in the epidemiology of viral infections, including R₀ and herd
immunity.
▪ Describe the sources of infection, modes of transmission and the maintenance of viruses in populations,
including arthropod-borne and zoonotic cycles.
▪ Interpret epidemic curves and explain the factors that determine the spread of viral epidemics.
▪ Explain the factors responsible for the emergence and re-emergence of viral diseases, with Indian
examples.
▪ Describe surveillance, molecular epidemiology and strategies for the control and eradication of viral
diseases.
LECTURE 25: CONCEPTS, TRANSMISSION AND MAINTENANCE OF VIRUSES IN
POPULATIONS
15.1 Introduction
Epidemiology is the study of the distribution and determinants of disease (and infection) in
populations, and the application of this knowledge to control. Viral epidemiology asks how viruses
enter, spread through and persist in populations of animals and humans, why disease appears in
particular places, seasons and host groups, and which interventions will stop transmission. Because
viruses are obligate parasites, their survival depends entirely on continual transmission from infected
to susceptible hosts — or on persistence in a host, a vector or the environment.
Table 15.1: Basic epidemiological terms
Term Meaning
Endemic (enzootic) Constant presence of a disease or infection in a population or area at a usual,
expected level
Epidemic (epizootic) Occurrence of cases clearly in excess of normal expectation
Pandemic (panzootic) Epidemic spreading over several countries or continents
Sporadic Irregular, scattered cases without a pattern
Outbreak Occurrence of cases linked in time and place (often a localised epidemic)
Zoonosis Infection naturally transmitted between vertebrate animals and humans
Reservoir Host population (or environment) in which a virus is maintained and from
which it is transmitted
Amplifying host Host in which virus multiplies to high titre and infects vectors efficiently (e.g.
pigs for JEV)
Dead-end host Host that is infected but does not transmit further (e.g. humans and horses for
JEV and West Nile virus)
Spillover Transmission of a virus from its reservoir host to a new host species
Chapter 15 | Page 179



VMC 503: General Virology
15.2 Measures of Disease and Transmission
Table 15.2: Common epidemiological measures
Measure Definition / formula
Prevalence Number of cases (or infected/seropositive animals) at a point in time ÷
population at risk; seroprevalence measures past exposure
Incidence (rate) Number of new cases in a period ÷ animal-time at risk
Attack rate Proportion of the exposed population that becomes ill during an outbreak
Morbidity rate Proportion of animals showing disease
Mortality rate Deaths from the disease ÷ total population
Case fatality rate Deaths ÷ number of clinical cases (e.g. very high in rabies, low in FMD in
adults)
Basic reproduction number
(R₀)
Average number of secondary infections produced by one infectious
individual in a fully susceptible population. If R₀ > 1 the infection can spread;
if R₀ < 1 it dies out
Effective reproduction
number (Rₑ or R )	ₜ
Secondary cases per case in the actual, partly immune population at time t; Rₑ
≈ R₀ × proportion susceptible
Herd immunity threshold Proportion of the population that must be immune to stop sustained
transmission = 1 − 1/R₀
Serial interval / generation
time Time between onset in a case and in the cases it infects
• R₀ depends on the contact rate, the probability of transmission per contact and the duration of
infectiousness (R₀ = β × c × D). It is a property of virus + host population + environment, so the
same virus has different R₀ in different farming systems.
• Worked example: if R₀ of a virus in a herd is 5, the herd immunity threshold is 1 − 1/5 = 0.8 — at
least 80% of animals must be immune (and, in practice, vaccination coverage must be higher still
because vaccine efficacy is below 100%).
• Critical community size: acute, immunising infections without a carrier state (e.g. measles,
rinderpest) need a large, continuously replenished population of susceptibles (births) to persist; in
small populations they fade out.
• Diagnostic test performance (sensitivity, specificity and predictive values) influences estimates
of prevalence: in populations with low prevalence, even a highly specific test yields many false
positives — important for surveillance to prove freedom from disease.
15.3 The Chain of Infection
Transmission requires a source (infected host, carrier, vector or contaminated environment), exit of
virus (shedding), survival in the environment or a vehicle, transmission to a new host, entry through a
portal and a susceptible host. Breaking any link interrupts the chain.
15.3.1 Sources of Infection
• Clinically affected animals — usually shed the largest amounts of virus.
• Animals in the incubation period — e.g. FMD and rabies (shedding before clinical signs) — are
important because they are not recognised.
• Subclinically infected animals — the "iceberg" of infection (Chapter 12).
• Carriers and persistently infected animals (Chapter 13) — BVDV PI animals, latent BoHV-1
carriers, FMD carriers, EAV carrier stallions.
Chapter 15 | Page 180



VMC 503: General Virology
• Wildlife and domestic reservoirs — bats (Nipah, rabies-related lyssaviruses), wild birds (avian
influenza), wild boar (ASF), rodents (hantaviruses), dogs (rabies in India).
• Vectors — arthropods in which arboviruses replicate (biological vectors).
• Environment and fomites — resistant non-enveloped viruses (CPV-2, FMDV in cool, humid
conditions, rotavirus, IBDV) survive for weeks to months on surfaces, feed, bedding and
vehicles.
15.3.2 Modes of Transmission

15.4 Maintenance of Viruses in Nature
15.4.1 Acute Infections and Population Size
• Viruses causing acute infections followed by lifelong immunity (e.g. rinderpest, PPR, CDV)
depend on a constant supply of new susceptibles through births; epidemics recur when the
proportion of susceptibles rises above the threshold (epidemic cycles).
• Viruses with persistent or latent infections (herpesviruses, retroviruses, BVDV) can survive in
small, isolated populations because each infected host remains a source for a long time.
• Viruses that survive long in the environment (CPV-2, IBDV, FMDV in some conditions) or
have multiple host species (FMD, rabies, influenza) are harder to eradicate.
15.4.2 Arthropod-Borne Viruses (Arboviruses)
Arboviruses are maintained in cycles between vertebrate hosts and haematophagous arthropods.
The vector becomes infectious only after the extrinsic incubation period (virus replication in the
midgut, dissemination and infection of salivary glands), which is shortened at higher temperatures —
one reason why arboviral diseases are seasonal (monsoon and post-monsoon in India) and why climate
strongly influences their distribution.
Table 15.4: Transmission cycles of important arboviruses

15.5 The Course of Epidemics
15.5.1 Epidemic Curves
An epidemic curve is a histogram of the number of new cases by date of onset. Its shape gives clues to
the source and mode of spread.
Table 15.5: Types of epidemic curve
Type Shape Interpretation / example
Point-source (common-
source)
Single sharp peak; all cases within
one incubation period

contaminated batch of vaccine or feed
Continuing common-
source
Plateau lasting as long as exposure
continues
Contaminated water supply or persistent

Successive waves separated by
roughly one incubation period

Animal-to-animal spread — e.g. FMD,

Arboviral diseases (BTV, JE) after

accumulate
Chapter 15 | Page 183



VMC 503: General Virology
15.5.2 Factors Determining Spread
• Virus factors: stability in the environment, amount and duration of shedding, route of shedding,
antigenic variation (escape from existing immunity), host range.
• Host factors: population density and size, proportion susceptible (births, waning maternal and
vaccine immunity), age structure, species mix, breed resistance, nutrition and stress.
• Management and social factors: animal movement and markets (a major driver of FMD, PPR
and LSD spread in India), shared grazing and watering points, communal breeding bulls,
transhumance and nomadic flocks, fairs (pashu melas), trade in animal products, and poor
biosecurity.
• Environmental factors: temperature, humidity and rainfall (vector abundance; virus survival),
wind (airborne FMD), season.
LECTURE 26: EMERGING VIRUSES, SURVEILLANCE AND CONTROL
15.6 Emerging and Re-emerging Viral Diseases
An emerging disease is one that has newly appeared in a population, or has existed but is rapidly
increasing in incidence or geographic range. About 60% of emerging infectious diseases of humans
are zoonotic, and most of these originate in wildlife; the majority of recently emerged pathogens are
RNA viruses, whose high mutation rates allow rapid adaptation to new hosts.
Table 15.6: Factors in the emergence of viral diseases
Factor Examples
Viral evolution Mutation and reassortment/recombination — new influenza subtypes and
clades (H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b spreading to wild birds, poultry, wild mammals
and, since 2024, dairy cattle in the USA); CPV-2 variants (2a, 2b, 2c); FMD

15.7 Surveillance
Surveillance is the ongoing, systematic collection, analysis and interpretation of health data, with
timely dissemination to those who need to act. It is used to detect incursions early, measure the burden
of disease, monitor control programmes and demonstrate freedom from disease for trade.

15.8 Molecular Epidemiology
• Molecular epidemiology uses genetic information about viruses — genotyping, sequencing of
key genes (e.g. VP1 of FMDV, N or F of PPRV, HA of influenza) and whole-genome
sequencing — to trace the origin and spread of outbreaks.
• Phylogenetic analysis groups viruses into genotypes, lineages, topotypes or clades; closely
related sequences from different places indicate a common source.
• Phylodynamics combines phylogenetics with sampling dates to estimate the time of origin,
rates of spread (molecular clock) and population dynamics of an epidemic.
• Examples: FMD serotype O lineage O/ME-SA/Ind-2001 (and its sub-lineages) has
predominated in India and spread to other countries; PPRV lineage IV is the lineage found in
India; tracking of H5N1 clade 2.3.2.1a and 2.3.4.4b viruses in India; LSDV genomes in India
showed relationships to field strains circulating in South Asia.
• Molecular data also help differentiate vaccine from field strains, detect reassortants and
recombinants, and select vaccine strains (vaccine matching).
Chapter 15 | Page 186



VMC 503: General Virology
15.9 Prevention, Control and Eradication
Table 15.9: Strategies for the control of viral diseases
Strategy Application
Biosecurity
Bio-exclusion (keeping virus out) and bio-containment (keeping it in) at farm,
zone and national level: quarantine of new stock, restricted entry, disinfection,
all-in/all-out
Quarantine and movement
control
Restrictions on movement of animals, products, vehicles and people from
infected areas; border and import controls
Stamping out Culling of infected and in-contact animals with compensation, disposal and
disinfection (HPAI, ASF, exotic FMD)
Vaccination Mass, ring (around outbreaks), targeted or suppressive vaccination; marker
vaccines with DIVA tests
Test and removal Identification and removal of carriers (EIA, BLV, BoHV-1, BVDV PI)
Vector control Insecticides, habitat management, housing animals at dusk, insect-proof
housing (BTV, JE, LSD)
Zoning and
compartmentalisation
Recognition of disease-free zones or compartments within a country to allow
trade (WOAH)
Education and One Health Farmer awareness, reporting, coordination of veterinary, medical and wildlife
sectors
• Eradication — elimination of a virus from a region or the world — is feasible when there is no
animal reservoir besides the target species, no carrier state, a highly effective vaccine giving
lifelong immunity, good diagnostic tests and strong political support. Rinderpest (declared
eradicated in 2011) is the model; PPR is targeted for global eradication by 2030 under the
WOAH–FAO Global Strategy.
Chapter 15 | Page 187



VMC 503: General Virology
• WOAH official status recognition is available for FMD, PPR, CSF, AHS, BSE and CBPP;
countries can be recognised as free (with or without vaccination), or have officially endorsed
control programmes.
INDIA FOCUS: VETERINARY EPIDEMIOLOGY AND VIRAL DISEASE CONTROL IN
INDIA
▪ ICAR–National Institute of Veterinary Epidemiology and Disease Informatics (NIVEDI),
Bengaluru runs the National Animal Disease Referral Expert System (NADRES v2), which issues
forewarnings of livestock disease risk about two months in advance for districts, using past outbreak
data together with climatic and other risk factors; a newer version (NADRES 3.0) has been introduced.
▪ National Animal Disease Control Programme (NADCP), launched in September 2019, aims to
control FMD (and brucellosis) through mass vaccination of cattle and buffaloes, with serosurveillance
by ICAR–NIFMD, Bhubaneswar, including NSP-based DIVA testing. PPR, CSF and other diseases
are addressed under the Livestock Health and Disease Control (LH&DC) scheme, and India has a
national programme to eradicate PPR by 2030.
▪ Animal identification and traceability — ear-tagging and the Bharat Pashudhan digital database
under the National Digital Livestock Mission — support vaccination records, movement tracing and
outbreak investigation.
▪ ICAR–National Institute of High Security Animal Diseases (NIHSAD), Bhopal is the national
referral laboratory for HPAI and other exotic and emerging diseases (e.g. ASF), and supports stamping-
out based control of avian influenza.
▪ The 2022 lumpy skin disease epidemic — with Rajasthan the worst-affected state — showed how
cattle movement, vector abundance during the monsoon, a fully susceptible population and delayed
vaccination can combine to produce a large propagated epidemic.
▪ One Health: zoonotic viral diseases (rabies, Nipah, KFD, CCHF, JE, avian influenza) require joint
action by the animal husbandry, health and forest departments; the National Institute of One Health,
Nagpur is being developed to support this.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Calculation of prevalence, incidence, attack rate, case fatality rate, R₀ and herd immunity threshold from
outbreak data.
▪ Construction and interpretation of an epidemic curve from a given outbreak line list.
▪ Sample-size calculation for a serosurvey and interpretation of sensitivity, specificity and predictive
values.
▪ Exercise with NADRES forecasts and disease maps for Rajasthan.
▪ Construction of a simple phylogenetic tree from FMDV VP1 or PPRV N gene sequences using MEGA.
▪ Outbreak investigation exercise (e.g. LSD or FMD in a village).
15.10 Summary
• Epidemiology studies the distribution and determinants of viral infections in populations and
applies this knowledge to control.
• Key measures include prevalence, incidence, attack rate, case fatality rate and R₀; the herd
immunity threshold is 1 − 1/R₀.
• Viruses are transmitted by contact, aerosols, fomites, vehicles, mechanical and biological
vectors, vertically and iatrogenically.
• Arboviruses are maintained in vertebrate–arthropod cycles with reservoir, amplifying and dead-
end hosts; their occurrence is seasonal and climate-dependent.
Chapter 15 | Page 188



VMC 503: General Virology
• Epidemic curves (point-source, propagated, seasonal) reveal the mode of spread; epidemics end
when susceptibles fall below a threshold.
• Emergence is driven by viral evolution, ecological change, intensification, trade, climate change
and human behaviour — illustrated in India by LSD, ASF, HPAI and Nipah.
• Surveillance, molecular epidemiology and progressive control strategies (biosecurity,
vaccination, stamping out, zoning) underpin control and eradication, as shown by rinderpest.
15.11 Glossary
Table 15.10: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Endemic Constant presence of an infection at the usual level in a population.
Epidemic Occurrence of cases in excess of normal expectation.
Incidence Rate of new cases in a population over a period.
Prevalence Proportion of a population affected at a point in time.
Case fatality rate Proportion of clinical cases that die.
R₀ Average secondary infections from one case in a fully susceptible population.
Herd immunity threshold Proportion immune needed to stop sustained spread (1 − 1/R₀).
Amplifying host Host producing high viraemia that infects vectors efficiently.
Dead-end host Host that does not transmit the virus further.
Extrinsic incubation period Time from vector feeding on a viraemic host until it can transmit.
Epidemic curve Histogram of cases by date of onset.
Surveillance Continuous collection and analysis of health data for action.
Phylodynamics Study of epidemic dynamics using dated virus phylogenies.
Zoning Defining disease-free sub-populations by geography for trade and control.
One Health Integrated approach to human, animal and environmental health.

UNIT IV — IMMUNITY, VACCINES AND ANTIVIRAL THERAPY
CHAPTER 16
Immune Response to Viruses
Lectures 27–28
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Describe the innate defences against viruses, including pattern recognition receptors and the interferon
system.
▪ Explain the roles of NK cells, complement and macrophages in early antiviral defence.
▪ Describe the adaptive humoral and cell-mediated immune responses to viruses and the mechanisms of
virus neutralisation.
▪ Explain mucosal and maternal immunity and their practical importance in vaccination of young animals.
▪ Describe how viruses evade innate and adaptive immunity.
LECTURE 27: INNATE IMMUNITY AND THE INTERFERON SYSTEM
16.1 Introduction
The outcome of a virus infection depends on a race between virus replication and spread and the host
immune response. Immunity to viruses operates in two phases: innate immunity, which acts within
minutes to hours, is not antigen-specific and limits virus spread in the first days; and adaptive
immunity (antibodies and T cells), which develops over 5–14 days, is highly specific, clears infection
and leaves immunological memory. The two are closely linked — innate responses shape and direct
adaptive responses. Understanding these mechanisms is the basis for vaccine design (Chapter 17), for
interpreting serology (Chapter 6) and for understanding immunopathology (Chapter 12).
16.2 Barriers and Non-Specific Defences
• Physical barriers: keratinised skin, mucus, the mucociliary escalator of the respiratory tract,
gastric acid, bile and proteolytic enzymes of the gut (which inactivate enveloped viruses).
• Antimicrobial molecules: defensins, lactoferrin, collectins (surfactant proteins, mannose-
binding lectin) that bind viral glycoproteins.
• Fever: elevated body temperature reduces replication of some viruses and enhances immune
function.
• Intrinsic (cell-autonomous) restriction factors present before infection — e.g. APOBEC3,
TRIM5α, tetherin and SAMHD1 acting against retroviruses; many are further increased by
interferon.
16.3 Recognition of Viruses: Pattern Recognition Receptors
Cells detect viruses through pattern recognition receptors (PRRs) that recognise pathogen-
associated molecular patterns (PAMPs) — mainly viral nucleic acids in locations or forms not
found in uninfected cells (dsRNA, 5′-triphosphate RNA, cytoplasmic DNA, unmethylated CpG DNA).

Species differences are important in veterinary virology: for example, chickens lack RIG-I (while
ducks possess it), which is one explanation proposed for the much greater susceptibility of chickens
than ducks to HPAI viruses; chickens rely on MDA5 for sensing influenza RNA.
16.4 The Interferon System
Interferons (IFNs), discovered by Isaacs and Lindenmann in 1957 as a factor that "interfered" with
influenza virus replication, are cytokines that induce an antiviral state in cells. They are the most
important innate antiviral defence.
Table 16.2: Types of interferon

16.4.1 Induction and Signalling
1. Induction: PRR signalling activates transcription factors IRF3, IRF7 and NF-κB, which switch
on the IFN-β gene (and IFN-α genes via IRF7). IFN is secreted within hours of infection.
Chapter 16 | Page 195



VMC 503: General Virology
2. Signalling: IFN binds its receptor on the same (autocrine) and neighbouring (paracrine) cells →
receptor-associated kinases JAK1 and TYK2 phosphorylate STAT1 and STAT2, which with
IRF9 form ISGF3 → ISGF3 enters the nucleus and binds interferon-stimulated response
elements (ISREs) → transcription of hundreds of interferon-stimulated genes (ISGs).
3. Antiviral state: ISG products block virus replication at many steps. Positive feedback (IRF7 is
itself an ISG) amplifies the response.
Table 16.3: Important interferon-stimulated antiviral proteins
ISG product Mechanism Viruses affected
PKR (protein kinase R) Activated by dsRNA; phosphorylates eIF2α →
shuts down protein synthesis Broad
2′,5′-oligoadenylate
synthetase (OAS) / RNase L
dsRNA activates OAS → 2′-5′ oligo(A)
activates RNase L → degrades viral and
cellular RNA
Broad; RNA viruses
Mx proteins (MxA, Mx1) Dynamin-like GTPases that trap viral
nucleocapsids and block transcription
Influenza, other negative-
strand RNA viruses
IFITM proteins Block fusion of viral and endosomal membranes Influenza, flaviviruses,
others
Tetherin (BST-2) Tethers budding enveloped virions to the cell

ISG15 Ubiquitin-like modifier conjugated to viral and
host proteins (ISGylation) Influenza, others
Viperin Produces a nucleotide analogue that terminates
viral RNA synthesis; alters lipid rafts Flaviviruses, influenza
ADAR1, APOBEC3 Editing/mutation of viral nucleic acids Various, retroviruses
16.4.2 Practical Aspects of Interferon
• Interferon is host-species specific but not virus specific: IFN from one species acts poorly in
other species, but protects against many viruses.
• IFN production explains viral interference (Chapter 10) and why a live vaccine can briefly
protect against unrelated viruses (e.g. early protection after intranasal vaccines).
• Recombinant feline interferon-ω is licensed in some countries for canine parvovirus enteritis
and feline retroviral infections; recombinant IFN-α is used in human hepatitis B and C (Chapter
18).
• Excessive or prolonged IFN and cytokine responses contribute to fever, anorexia and
immunopathology (e.g. cytokine storm).
Chapter 16 | Page 196



VMC 503: General Virology
16.5 Other Innate Defences
Table 16.4: Cellular and humoral innate defences against viruses
Component Role
Natural killer (NK) cells
Kill virus-infected cells that have down-regulated MHC class I ("missing
self") or express stress ligands; activated by IFN-α/β and IL-12; produce IFN-
γ; mediate ADCC via Fc receptors. Peak activity at 2–4 days after infection
Macrophages
Phagocytose virions and infected cells; produce cytokines (TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6,
IL-12) and IFN; may also be infected and carry virus (PRRSV, ASFV,
lentiviruses)
Dendritic cells Conventional DCs capture antigen and migrate to lymph nodes to prime T
cells (bridge to adaptive immunity); plasmacytoid DCs produce IFN-α
Neutrophils Recruited to sites of infection; mainly contribute to inflammation and

Complement
Can lyse enveloped virions and infected cells, opsonise virions and enhance
antibody responses (lectin pathway via MBL; classical pathway with
antibody)
