LECTURE 28: ADAPTIVE IMMUNITY, MATERNAL IMMUNITY AND IMMUNE
EVASION
16.6 Adaptive Immune Response
16.6.1 Antigen Presentation
• MHC class I pathway: viral proteins synthesised within the infected cell are degraded by the
proteasome, peptides are transported into the endoplasmic reticulum by TAP, loaded onto MHC
class I and displayed on the cell surface to CD8 cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs)	⁺ . Because all
nucleated cells express MHC I, any infected cell can be recognised.
• MHC class II pathway: virions and viral proteins taken up by antigen-presenting cells (DCs,
macrophages, B cells) are degraded in endosomes and presented on MHC class II to CD4⁺
helper T cells.
• Cross-presentation: DCs can present exogenous antigen (e.g. from dying infected cells) on
MHC I to prime CD8 T cells — important when the virus does not infect DCs, and for	⁺
inactivated and subunit vaccines.
16.6.2 Cell-Mediated Immunity
Table 16.5: T-cell subsets in antiviral immunity
Cell Function
CD8 cytotoxic T	⁺
lymphocytes
Kill infected cells by perforin–granzyme and Fas–FasL pathways, ideally
before progeny virus is released; secrete IFN-γ and TNF-α (non-cytolytic
clearance, e.g. in neurons and hepatocytes). Critical for recovery from
infections with cell-associated and cytopathic viruses
CD4 Th1 cells	⁺ Secrete IFN-γ and IL-2; activate macrophages and support CTL development
CD4 T follicular helper (Tfh)	⁺
cells
Help B cells in germinal centres — class switching, affinity maturation,
memory B cells and long-lived plasma cells
CD4 Th2 and Th17 cells	⁺ Th2 support antibody (and allergic) responses; Th17 recruit neutrophils at
mucosae
Regulatory T cells (Treg) Limit immunopathology; may also favour viral persistence
Memory T cells Central and effector memory; tissue-resident memory T cells at mucosal
surfaces give rapid local protection
Delayed-type hypersensitivity and lymphoproliferation assays, and IFN-γ ELISpot or
intracellular cytokine staining, are used to measure cell-mediated immunity to viruses and vaccines.
16.6.3 Humoral Immunity
B cells recognise native viral antigens (mainly surface glycoproteins and capsid proteins) and, with T-
cell help, differentiate into plasma cells secreting antibodies. The first antibodies are IgM (detectable
5–10 days after infection, useful for diagnosis of recent infection), followed by IgG (higher affinity,
long-lasting) and, at mucosal surfaces, secretory IgA. Birds produce IgY (functional equivalent of
IgG) and IgA.
Table 16.6: Mechanisms of antibody-mediated antiviral activity
Mechanism Description
Neutralisation — blocking
attachment
Antibody covers the receptor-binding site (e.g. influenza HA, FMDV
VP1 G–H loop)
Neutralisation — blocking
entry/fusion or uncoating
Antibody prevents conformational changes needed for fusion (e.g. stem
antibodies to HA) or stabilises the capsid
Chapter 16 | Page 198



VMC 503: General Virology
Mechanism Description
Aggregation Cross-linking of virions reduces the number of infectious units
Complement-mediated lysis Antibody + complement lyse enveloped virions and infected cells
Opsonisation Fc and complement receptors promote uptake of virions by phagocytes
Antibody-dependent cellular
cytotoxicity (ADCC) NK cells and macrophages kill antibody-coated infected cells
Inhibition of release Antibodies to neuraminidase (influenza) block release of progeny virions
Intracellular neutralisation TRIM21 binds antibody-coated virions in the cytoplasm and targets them
for degradation
• Relative importance: antibody is most important in preventing infection and reinfection and
in controlling viraemic spread (e.g. FMD, CPV-2, PPR, rabies post-exposure); cell-mediated
immunity is essential for clearing established infections, especially of viruses spread cell-to-cell
or remaining cell-associated (herpesviruses, poxviruses, MDV, ASFV).
• Correlates of protection: measurable immune responses that predict protection — e.g. VNT
titres for FMD and rabies (≥ 0.5 IU/ml for rabies), HI titres for NDV and influenza. Many
diseases (ASF, herpesviruses) lack simple antibody correlates.
• Harmful antibody effects: antibody-dependent enhancement (FIP, dengue), immune complexes
(EIA, Aleutian disease) — Chapter 12.
16.7 Mucosal Immunity
• Most viruses enter at mucosal surfaces (respiratory, intestinal, genital), where protection is
mainly by secretory IgA (transported across epithelium by the polymeric Ig receptor), local
tissue-resident T cells and type III IFN.
• Mucosal immunity is induced most effectively by mucosal (oral, intranasal, ocular)
vaccination with live vaccines — e.g. NDV and IBV vaccines by eye drop or spray, intranasal
BoHV-1 vaccines, oral rotavirus vaccines; parenteral inactivated vaccines generally induce less
mucosal IgA.
• Serum antibodies may prevent disease (by blocking viraemia) but not local infection and
shedding — relevant to FMD carriers and to vaccinated birds shedding NDV.
Chapter 16 | Page 199



VMC 503: General Virology
16.8 Maternal Immunity
Young animals are protected in early life by maternal antibodies. The route of transfer depends on the
structure of the placenta.
Table 16.7: Transfer of maternal immunity in domestic animals
Species Placenta type Route of transfer
Cattle, buffalo, sheep,
goats
Syndesmochorial /
epitheliochorial (cotyledonary)
No transplacental transfer; entirely via
colostrum, absorbed by the gut within ~24
hours of birth ("gut closure")
Pigs, horses Epitheliochorial Entirely via colostrum; failure of passive
transfer common in foals and piglets
Dogs, cats Endotheliochorial About 5–10% transplacental; most via
colostrum in the first 1–2 days
Humans, primates,
rodents Haemochorial IgG transferred across the placenta
Birds None (egg) IgY transferred via the yolk; IgA and IgM in
albumen; protects chicks for 2–3 weeks
• Colostrum contains high concentrations of IgG1 (ruminants), IgA and IgM, along with maternal
lymphocytes and cytokines; milk IgA gives continuing local protection in the gut (lactogenic
immunity — important against rotavirus and coronavirus diarrhoea).
• Failure of passive transfer (inadequate or delayed colostrum feeding) predisposes neonates to
viral diarrhoea and respiratory disease. Colostrum should be given within the first 2–6 hours, at
about 10% of body weight for calves.
• Vaccination of dams before parturition (e.g. with rotavirus/coronavirus vaccines in cows)
increases colostral antibody and protects the newborn.
Chapter 16 | Page 200



VMC 503: General Virology
• Maternal antibodies decline with a characteristic half-life (roughly 1–3 weeks depending on
species and virus) and interfere with active immunisation by neutralising live vaccine virus and
masking antigen.
• The period when maternal antibody is too low to protect against field virus but still high enough
to block vaccination is the "window of susceptibility" — the reason why puppies receive a
series of CPV-2 and CDV vaccinations up to 16 weeks of age, and why vaccination of young
calves, lambs and kids against FMD and PPR is delayed to about 3–4 months of age.
• In poultry, the timing of IBD vaccination is calculated from maternal antibody titres (e.g. the
Deventer formula), and vector (HVT-IBD) and immune-complex vaccines are designed to be
given despite maternal antibody.

16.10 Genetic Resistance to Viral Infection
• MHC haplotype: chicken MHC B21 is associated with resistance to Marek’s disease.
• Receptor polymorphisms: absence or variants of receptors confer resistance — e.g. chickens
lacking receptors for particular ALV subgroups; gene-edited pigs lacking the CD163 SRCR5
domain are resistant to PRRSV.
• Antiviral restriction genes: polymorphisms of Mx, OAS and other ISGs have been studied for
disease resistance; PRNP genotypes determine susceptibility to scrapie (Chapter 7).
• Breed differences: indigenous breeds may be more resistant to some endemic infections (e.g.
bluetongue in native sheep of endemic areas compared with imported breeds).
INDIA FOCUS: IMMUNITY TO VIRUSES: APPLICATIONS IN INDIA
▪ Colostrum management: delayed or inadequate colostrum feeding is common in smallholder dairy
systems and contributes to calf mortality from viral and mixed diarrhoea; extension programmes
emphasise feeding colostrum within the first hours of birth.
▪ Maternal antibody and vaccination schedules: under NADCP, calves are generally vaccinated
against FMD from about 4 months of age, and PPR vaccine is given to lambs and kids from about 3
months of age, to avoid maternal antibody interference.
▪ Canine parvovirus vaccine failures in Indian pups are frequently attributed to maternal antibody
interference, early single-dose vaccination and cold-chain failure; a multi-dose schedule finishing at or
after 16 weeks is recommended.
▪ IBD and ND vaccination in poultry: maternal antibody levels in commercial chicks vary widely, and
serological monitoring (ELISA, HI) is used by Indian integrators to time IBD and ND vaccinations.
▪ Indigenous livestock: Indian researchers have studied immune-response and antiviral genes (e.g. Mx,
TLRs, MHC) in indigenous cattle, buffalo, goats and chicken breeds such as Kadaknath and Aseel for
associations with disease resistance; the results are promising but still largely at research stage.
▪ Rabies: post-exposure prophylaxis relies on vaccine-induced neutralising antibody together with rabies
immunoglobulin or monoclonal antibodies for severe exposures; pre-exposure vaccination with titre
checks is advised for veterinarians and students.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Virus neutralisation test and haemagglutination-inhibition test to measure antibody titres.
▪ Detection of IgM versus IgG antibodies by ELISA and interpretation of paired sera.
▪ Demonstration of interferon bioassay (inhibition of cytopathic effect of a sensitive virus) —
video/demonstration.
Chapter 16 | Page 202



VMC 503: General Virology
▪ Estimation of maternal antibody titres in chicks (IBD ELISA) and calculation of optimal vaccination
age.
▪ Assessment of passive transfer in calves (serum total protein by refractometer, zinc sulphate turbidity
test).
16.11 Summary
• Innate immunity acts within hours: viral nucleic acids are sensed by TLRs, RIG-I/MDA5 and
cGAS–STING, triggering type I and III interferons.
• Interferons act through JAK–STAT signalling to induce ISGs (PKR, OAS/RNase L, Mx, IFITM,
tetherin) that establish an antiviral state.
• NK cells, macrophages, dendritic cells and complement contribute to early defence and link to
adaptive immunity.
• CD8 CTLs clear infected cells; CD4 T cells help; antibodies neutralise virions, prevent	⁺ ⁺
reinfection and control viraemia.
• Secretory IgA protects mucosal surfaces; mucosal immunity is best induced by live mucosal
vaccines.
• Maternal immunity is transferred via colostrum (ruminants, pigs, horses) or yolk (birds) and
interferes with vaccination, creating a window of susceptibility.
• Viruses evade immunity by blocking IFN, down-regulating MHC I, antigenic variation,
virokines, latency and immunosuppression.
16.12 Glossary
Table 16.9: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
PAMP Pathogen-associated molecular pattern recognised by innate receptors.
PRR Pattern recognition receptor (e.g. TLR, RIG-I).
Interferon Cytokine inducing an antiviral state in cells.
ISG Interferon-stimulated gene.
Antiviral state Resistance of cells to virus replication induced by interferon.
pDC Plasmacytoid dendritic cell; major source of IFN-α.
NK cell Innate lymphocyte killing cells lacking MHC I.
CTL CD8 cytotoxic T lymphocyte.	⁺
Cross-presentation Presentation of exogenous antigen on MHC class I.
Neutralisation Loss of virus infectivity due to antibody binding.
ADCC Antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity.
Secretory IgA Dimeric IgA transported onto mucosal surfaces.
Passive transfer Acquisition of maternal antibodies by the newborn.
Window of susceptibility Period when maternal antibody neither protects nor allows vaccination.

Virokine Virus-encoded cytokine or cytokine-receptor homologue.

UNIT IV — IMMUNITY, VACCINES AND ANTIVIRAL THERAPY
CHAPTER 17
Viral Vaccines
Lectures 29–30
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Trace the history of viral vaccines and state the properties of an ideal vaccine.
▪ Compare live attenuated and inactivated vaccines, including methods of attenuation and inactivation.
▪ Describe modern vaccine technologies — subunit, VLP, vectored, nucleic acid, gene-deleted and
marker (DIVA) vaccines.
▪ Explain the role of adjuvants, routes of administration, vaccination schedules and reasons for vaccine
failure.
▪ Describe the production, quality control and regulation of veterinary viral vaccines, with reference to
India.
LECTURE 29: PRINCIPLES AND CONVENTIONAL VIRAL VACCINES
17.1 Introduction
Vaccination is the most effective and economical means of preventing viral diseases, because very few
specific antiviral drugs are available for animals (Chapter 18). Vaccines have achieved the global
eradication of smallpox (1980) and rinderpest (2011), the control of FMD, PPR, rabies, Newcastle
disease, Marek’s disease and canine distemper, and they underpin intensive poultry and livestock
production. A vaccine is a preparation of antigen that induces protective, specific immunity (Chapter
16) without causing the disease.
17.2 Milestones in Viral Vaccinology
Table 17.1: Landmarks in the development of viral vaccines
Year Milestone
1796 Edward Jenner used cowpox to protect against smallpox — the origin of the word "vaccine"
(Latin vacca, cow)
1885 Louis Pasteur used dried spinal cord of rabbits infected with rabies virus to treat Joseph
Meister — first attenuated viral vaccine
1930s Egg-adapted yellow fever 17D vaccine (Theiler; Nobel Prize 1951); fowlpox and early ND
vaccines
1950s Cell culture (Enders, Weller and Robbins, 1949) enabled inactivated (Salk, 1955) and live
(Sabin) polio vaccines and many veterinary vaccines
1960s Plowright’s tissue culture rinderpest vaccine (TCRV) — the vaccine that eradicated
rinderpest
~1970 Marek’s disease HVT vaccine — first vaccine against a cancer
1980s
First recombinant (subunit) vaccine — hepatitis B surface antigen from yeast (1986); gene-
deleted pseudorabies marker vaccines; vaccinia–rabies glycoprotein (V-RG) oral bait
vaccine for wildlife
1990s–2000s Canarypox- and fowlpox-vectored vaccines; HVT-vectored poultry vaccines; PCV2 subunit
vaccines (2006)
2005–2010 First licensed DNA vaccines — West Nile virus in horses (2005) and a canine melanoma
immunotherapy (2010)
Chapter 17 | Page 207



VMC 503: General Virology
Year Milestone
2020 mRNA vaccines (COVID-19) authorised within a year of the virus being identified
2022–2023 First licensed gene-deleted live ASF vaccines (Vietnam); homologous Lumpi-ProVacInd
LSD vaccine in India (2022)
17.3 Properties of an Ideal Vaccine
• Safe: no disease, reversion to virulence, contamination, spread to non-target species or adverse
reactions; safe in pregnant animals.
• Effective: induces protective and long-lasting immunity, preferably after a single dose, against
all relevant strains/serotypes; prevents both disease and infection and shedding (sterilising
immunity) where possible.
• Appropriate immunity: the right type — neutralising antibody, mucosal IgA and/or CMI — as
dictated by the pathogenesis.
• Practical: stable (ideally thermostable, not needing a cold chain), cheap, easy to administer (e.g.
mass administration in water, spray, feed or in ovo), compatible with other vaccines.
• Compatible with surveillance: allows differentiation of infected from vaccinated animals
(DIVA).
17.4 Live Attenuated Vaccines
Live vaccines contain virus that replicates in the host but has lost virulence. Replication provides a
large and prolonged antigen dose presented through both MHC I and II pathways, so they induce
strong, long-lasting humoral, cell-mediated and mucosal immunity, often after one dose and
without adjuvant.

Cautionary examples: vaccine-derived polioviruses in humans; reversion and recombination of live

pregnant animals; contamination of vaccines with extraneous agents such as REV, ALV, BVDV
(hence the use of SPF eggs and screened cells and serum).
17.5 Inactivated (Killed) Vaccines
Inactivated vaccines contain whole virions rendered non-infectious while preserving antigenicity.
Because they do not replicate, larger amounts of antigen and an adjuvant are needed.

acids

"tailing" can leave residual live virus
(Cutter incident, 1955); used in many

(BEI)

• Examples: FMD (BEI-inactivated, oil or aluminium hydroxide–saponin adjuvanted), rabies (cell
culture), avian influenza H5/H9 (oil emulsion), ND and EDS (oil emulsion in layers and
breeders), IBD killed vaccines for breeders, BTV (pentavalent inactivated, e.g. in India), equine
influenza, FeLV and canine coronavirus.
• Antigen payload and purity are crucial: FMD vaccines are standardised by 146S antigen
content and purified to remove non-structural proteins to permit NSP-based DIVA serology.
Chapter 17 | Page 209



VMC 503: General Virology
• Autogenous vaccines — inactivated vaccines prepared from the virus isolated on a particular
farm — are used where commercial vaccines are unavailable or do not match (e.g.
papillomatosis, some swine and poultry viruses).
LECTURE 30: NEW-GENERATION VACCINES, ADJUVANTS AND VACCINATION
PRACTICE
17.6 New-Generation Vaccines
17.6.1 Subunit and Virus-Like Particle Vaccines
• Subunit vaccines contain only the protective antigen(s), produced by recombinant expression
in bacteria, yeast, baculovirus–insect cells, mammalian cells or plants. They are safe (no
infectious virus), allow DIVA, but are usually less immunogenic and need adjuvants and
boosters.
• Virus-like particles (VLPs) are self-assembled viral structural proteins that mimic the virion but
contain no genome. Repetitive, particulate structure makes them highly immunogenic.
• Examples: PCV2 ORF2 capsid (baculovirus-expressed) vaccines for pigs; hepatitis B and HPV
VLP vaccines for humans; experimental FMD, BTV and IBD VLP vaccines; feline leukaemia
recombinant subunit vaccines.
17.6.2 Vectored (Recombinant Live) Vaccines
A safe, replication-competent or replication-defective vector virus carries a gene encoding a
protective antigen of the target virus. The antigen is expressed inside host cells, inducing both antibody
and CMI, with the safety of the vector.
Chapter 17 | Page 210



VMC 503: General Virology
Table 17.5: Viral vectors used in veterinary vaccines
Vector Examples of vaccines Features
Herpesvirus of turkeys
(HVT)

countries
17.6.3 Nucleic Acid Vaccines
• DNA vaccines: a bacterial plasmid carrying the antigen gene under a eukaryotic promoter is
injected (intramuscularly, intradermally or by gene gun/electroporation); host cells produce the
antigen, inducing CMI and antibody. Advantages: stability, rapid design, DIVA; limitation:
poorer immunogenicity in large animals. Licensed examples: West Nile virus DNA vaccine for
horses (2005) and infectious haematopoietic necrosis virus DNA vaccine for salmon (2005).
• mRNA vaccines: synthetic, nucleoside-modified mRNA in lipid nanoparticles; rapid,
adaptable, strong antibody and T-cell responses; require cold storage. Transformed human
vaccinology during COVID-19; self-amplifying RNA and RNA-particle platforms are being
developed for swine and poultry diseases (e.g. HPAI).
17.6.4 Gene-Deleted and Marker (DIVA) Vaccines
• Gene-deleted vaccines are attenuated by deletion of virulence genes such as thymidine kinase
(TK) or glycoprotein E (gE); deletions are stable and cannot revert by point mutation.
• DIVA (Differentiating Infected from Vaccinated Animals) requires a marker vaccine lacking
an antigen that field virus possesses, together with a companion diagnostic test for antibodies to
that antigen.
Table 17.6: DIVA strategies in veterinary virology
Disease Vaccine Companion test
IBR (BoHV-1) gE-deleted live or inactivated vaccine gE-blocking ELISA
Aujeszky’s disease
(PRV) gE (gI)-deleted vaccine gE ELISA — basis of eradication in
Europe and USA
FMD Purified inactivated vaccine free of NSPs NSP (3ABC) ELISA — infected
animals have antibodies to NSPs
Avian influenza Vaccine of different NA subtype (e.g.
H5N2 or H5N3 vaccine against H5N1)
Heterologous NA antibody test;
sentinel birds

Chapter 17 | Page 211



VMC 503: General Virology
Disease Vaccine Companion test
proteins development)
17.6.5 Other Approaches
• Reverse genetics allows rational design of live vaccines (e.g. NDV genotype-matched vaccines,
codon-pair deoptimisation), rapid generation of influenza seed strains (6:2 reassortants on a
safe backbone), and chimeric viruses.
• Plant-made vaccines, edible vaccines and structure-based design (stabilised prefusion
glycoproteins, e.g. RSV F).
• Peptide and multi-epitope vaccines, currently mostly experimental.
17.7 Adjuvants
Adjuvants (Latin adjuvare, to help) enhance the magnitude, type and duration of the immune response
to a co-administered antigen. They act by forming an antigen depot, promoting uptake by APCs, and
stimulating innate immunity (PRR agonists), thereby reducing the antigen dose and number of doses
needed.
Table 17.7: Common adjuvants in veterinary viral vaccines
Adjuvant Mechanism / type of response Examples of use
Aluminium salts (hydroxide,
phosphate)

mainly Th2/antibody
FMD (with saponin), rabies, many
inactivated vaccines
Saponins (Quil A, QS-21,
ISCOMs) Th1 and CTL responses FMD (with alum), equine influenza
(ISCOM)

(W/O), oil-in-water (O/W),

Oil adjuvants may cause injection-site reactions and granulomas; accidental self-injection of oil
vaccines in humans requires prompt surgical attention.

VMC 503: General Virology
17.9 Vaccination Schedules and Programmes
• Schedules are designed around maternal antibody decline (Chapter 16), the age at risk, the
season of disease (e.g. before the monsoon for arboviral and HS vaccination), and the duration
of immunity (boosters).
• Primary course of inactivated vaccines usually requires two doses 2–4 weeks apart, followed
by boosters (e.g. FMD every 6 months under NADCP; rabies annually or as per manufacturer).
• Mass vaccination campaigns aim for coverage above the herd immunity threshold (Chapter 15);
ring vaccination around outbreaks limits spread; targeted vaccination of high-risk groups.
• Core and non-core vaccines in companion animals (WSAVA guidelines): core vaccines for
dogs are CDV, CAV and CPV-2 (and rabies where endemic, as in India); for cats FPV, FHV-1
and FCV.
17.10 Vaccine Failure
Table 17.9: Causes of vaccine failure
Category Causes
Vaccine-related
Inadequate antigen content; antigenic mismatch with field strains (e.g. FMD,
IBV, influenza, NDV genotypes); loss of viability through cold-chain breaks or
exposure to sunlight; expired vaccine; contaminated vaccine
Administration-related
Wrong route, dose or diluent; chlorinated water inactivating live vaccines in
drinking water; disinfectant on syringes; missed animals; incorrect timing relative
to maternal antibody
Host-related
Maternal antibody interference; immunosuppression (IBDV, MDV, CAV,
BVDV, mycotoxins, stress, malnutrition, parasitism); incubating infection at
vaccination; genetic non-responders; very young or old animals
Challenge-related Overwhelming field challenge; very virulent strains (vvIBDV, vv+MDV); new
variants
17.11 Adverse Effects of Vaccines
• Local reactions: swelling, abscess, granuloma; feline injection-site sarcoma associated with
adjuvanted vaccines.
• Systemic: fever, lethargy, drop in milk yield; anaphylaxis (type I hypersensitivity).
• Residual virulence or reversion of live vaccines; abortion and foetal infection after live
vaccines in pregnant animals (BVDV, BTV, IBR).
• Immune-mediated disease and vaccine-enhanced disease in rare cases.
• Contamination with extraneous agents.
• Adverse events should be reported under the pharmacovigilance system of the regulator.
17.12 Production and Quality Control of Viral Vaccines
1. Seed lot system: vaccines are produced from characterised master seed virus (MSV) and
master cell seed (MCS) stocks, limiting passages (e.g. no more than five passages from the
MSV) to avoid genetic drift.
2. Substrates: SPF embryonated eggs, primary cells, or continuous cell lines (Vero, BHK-21
suspension cells for FMD and rabies, MDBK) grown in bioreactors.
Chapter 17 | Page 213



VMC 503: General Virology
3. Harvest, clarification, concentration and purification (ultrafiltration, PEG precipitation,
chromatography); inactivation with validated kinetics (for inactivated vaccines); formulation
with adjuvant or stabiliser; lyophilisation of live vaccines.
4. Quality control tests: sterility (bacteria, fungi), freedom from mycoplasma and extraneous
viruses, identity, safety (in target animals, often at 10× dose for live vaccines), innocuity,
potency (virus titre for live vaccines; antigen content or challenge/serological potency tests —
e.g. FMD PD₅₀ test, rabies NIH test), stability, moisture content and residual inactivant.
5. Good Manufacturing Practice (GMP) and appropriate biosafety containment (e.g. BSL-3 for
FMD vaccine production).
6. 3Rs: replacing animal potency tests by in vitro antigen quantification and serological tests where
validated.
17.13 Regulation of Veterinary Vaccines in India
• Veterinary vaccines are regulated under the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940 and its Rules by the
Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO), headed by the Drugs Controller
General of India (DCGI), with state licensing authorities for manufacturing licences.
• Standards are laid down in the Indian Pharmacopoeia (IP), which contains monographs for
veterinary vaccines (e.g. "PPR vaccine, live, IP").
• Batch testing of veterinary vaccines is carried out by designated government testing laboratories;
ICAR–IVRI, Izatnagar has long contributed to the standardisation and quality testing of
veterinary biologicals in India.
• Genetically modified (recombinant) vaccines additionally require clearance from the Review
Committee on Genetic Manipulation (RCGM) and the Genetic Engineering Appraisal
Committee (GEAC) under the Rules, 1989 for hazardous micro-organisms and GMOs.
• International standards for veterinary vaccines are given in the WOAH Manual of Diagnostic
Tests and Vaccines for Terrestrial Animals.
INDIA FOCUS: VIRAL VACCINES DEVELOPED AND USED IN INDIA
▪ Rinderpest: mass vaccination with tissue culture rinderpest vaccine under the National Project on
Chapter 17 | Page 214



VMC 503: General Virology
Rinderpest Eradication led to India’s freedom from rinderpest (WOAH, 2006).
▪ PPR: the live attenuated Sungri/96 Vero-cell vaccine developed by ICAR–IVRI (with other Indian
strains such as Arasur/87 and Coimbatore/97 used regionally) is produced by several manufacturers and
used in the National PPR Eradication Programme, which aims to eradicate PPR by 2030 through
annual mass vaccination of sheep and goats.
▪ FMD: inactivated, oil-adjuvanted polyvalent vaccines containing serotypes O, A and Asia 1, produced
by Indian manufacturers such as Indian Immunologicals Ltd (NDDB), Hyderabad, are used for six-
monthly mass vaccination of cattle and buffaloes under NADCP; vaccine strains are selected and
matched by ICAR–NIFMD, Bhubaneswar.
▪ Lumpy skin disease: Lumpi-ProVacInd, a homologous live attenuated vaccine developed by ICAR–
NRCE, Hisar with ICAR–IVRI, Izatnagar from an Indian LSDV isolate, was released in 2022; the
heterologous goatpox (Uttarkashi strain) vaccine was widely used during the 2022 epidemic.
▪ Classical swine fever: ICAR–IVRI released a live attenuated CSF cell-culture vaccine based on an
indigenous strain, replacing the older lapinised vaccine that required sacrifice of rabbits; the
technology was transferred to industry together with a sheeppox vaccine.
▪ Poultry: ND vaccines (F, LaSota, R2B Mukteswar strain), IBD, MD (HVT, SB-1, Rispens) and HVT-
vectored vaccines are widely used; the National Action Plan for the Control of Avian Influenza relies
on stamping out rather than vaccination for HPAI.
▪ Rabies: cell-culture inactivated vaccines for dogs and humans; mass dog vaccination is central to the
National Action Plan for Dog-Mediated Rabies Elimination by 2030.
▪ Cold chain: maintaining 2–8 °C in remote and hot areas such as western Rajasthan remains a practical
challenge; thermostable formulations of PPR and other vaccines have been developed by Indian
researchers.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Visit to a vaccine production or testing unit (virtual or physical); study of GMP and cold-chain
requirements.
▪ Titration of a live vaccine (TCID₅₀ or EID₅₀) and calculation of dose.
▪ Sterility and safety testing of a vaccine batch (demonstration).
▪ Evaluation of post-vaccination immune response by ELISA, HI or VNT (e.g. PPR, ND, FMD).
▪ NSP ELISA for DIVA in FMD-vaccinated animals.
▪ Preparing a vaccination schedule for a dairy farm, a goat farm and a poultry farm.
17.14 Summary
• Vaccines are the mainstay of viral disease control and achieved the eradication of smallpox and
rinderpest.
• Live attenuated vaccines induce strong, broad, long-lasting immunity but carry risks of reversion,
contamination and heat-lability.
• Inactivated vaccines (formaldehyde, BEI, BPL) are safe but need adjuvants and boosters and
induce mainly antibody.
• New-generation vaccines include subunit, VLP, vectored (HVT, poxvirus, adenovirus), DNA
and mRNA vaccines.
• Gene-deleted marker vaccines with companion tests permit DIVA (IBR gE, PRV gE, FMD
NSP).
• Adjuvants (alum, saponin, oil emulsions, TLR agonists) enhance and direct immune responses.
• Schedules must account for maternal antibody, season and duration of immunity; failures result
from vaccine, administration, host and challenge factors.
Chapter 17 | Page 215



VMC 503: General Virology
• Vaccines are produced under GMP using seed-lot systems with rigorous quality control and
regulatory release.
17.15 Glossary
Table 17.10: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Attenuation Reduction of virulence while retaining immunogenicity.
Live attenuated vaccine Vaccine containing replicating virus of reduced virulence.
Inactivated vaccine Vaccine containing non-infectious whole virus.
BEI Binary ethyleneimine, an inactivating agent for FMD vaccines.
Subunit vaccine Vaccine containing purified protective antigen(s) only.
VLP Virus-like particle; empty self-assembled capsid.
Vectored vaccine Live vector virus expressing a foreign protective antigen.
DNA vaccine Plasmid encoding an antigen expressed in host cells.
Marker vaccine Vaccine lacking an antigen present in field virus.
DIVA Differentiating infected from vaccinated animals.
Adjuvant Substance enhancing the immune response to an antigen.
Seed lot system Production from characterised master and working seeds.
Potency test Test measuring protective strength of a vaccine batch.
PD₅₀ Dose protecting 50% of vaccinated animals against challenge.
Cold chain Maintenance of vaccines at recommended temperature from manufacture to
use.
Vaccine failure Failure of vaccinated animals to be protected.

UNIT IV — IMMUNITY, VACCINES AND ANTIVIRAL THERAPY
CHAPTER 18
Viral Chemotherapy
Lectures 31–32
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
After studying this chapter, the student will be able to:
▪ Explain why antiviral drugs are more difficult to develop than antibacterial drugs, and state the
properties of an ideal antiviral agent.
▪ Classify antiviral drugs according to the stage of virus replication they inhibit, with mechanisms of
action.
▪ Describe the antiviral drugs and immunotherapeutics used in veterinary practice.
▪ Explain the mechanisms of antiviral drug resistance and methods to test susceptibility.
▪ Discuss the regulatory, ethical and One Health issues in the use of antivirals in animals.
LECTURE 31: PRINCIPLES AND MECHANISMS OF ANTIVIRAL DRUGS
18.1 Introduction
Antiviral chemotherapy is the use of chemical compounds to inhibit virus replication in the host.
Compared with antibacterial therapy, it developed late and remains limited, especially in veterinary
medicine, because:
• Viruses use host-cell machinery, so few targets are unique to the virus; drugs that block virus
replication often harm host cells (low selectivity).
• Narrow spectrum: most antivirals act on a single virus or family, requiring a precise diagnosis.
• Timing: in acute infections, virus replication often peaks before clinical signs appear, so
treatment started after diagnosis may be too late.
• Latent and integrated genomes (herpesviruses, retroviruses) are not affected by drugs that act
only on replicating virus.
• Rapid emergence of resistance, especially in RNA viruses with high mutation rates (Chapter
10).
• Economics: for food animals, treatment is rarely cost-effective and raises residue and resistance
concerns; vaccination and biosecurity remain the mainstay of control.
Nevertheless, antiviral drugs are essential in human medicine (HIV, hepatitis B and C, herpesviruses,
influenza, COVID-19) and increasingly used in companion animals and horses.
18.2 Milestones in Antiviral Chemotherapy
Table 18.1: Landmarks in antiviral drug development
Year Milestone
1962 Idoxuridine (5-iodo-2′-deoxyuridine) used topically for herpetic keratitis — first clinically
used antiviral
1966 Amantadine approved for influenza A prophylaxis (M2 ion-channel blocker)
1977 Acyclovir described (Elion, Schaeffer and colleagues) — first highly selective antiviral;
Gertrude Elion shared the Nobel Prize in 1988

cats with feline infectious peritonitis (FIP) — a landmark in veterinary antiviral therapy
18.3 Properties of an Ideal Antiviral Drug
• High selectivity — inhibits a virus-specific function at concentrations far below those toxic to
host cells (high therapeutic/selectivity index = CC₅₀ ÷ IC₅₀).
• Broad activity against all strains of the target virus, ideally more than one virus.
• Low propensity for resistance (high genetic barrier).
• Good pharmacokinetics — oral bioavailability, penetration into target tissues (including CNS
and eye), suitable half-life.
• Safety — no mutagenicity, teratogenicity or immunosuppression; no residues in food.
• Affordable and stable.
18.4 Targets for Antiviral Drugs
Each step of the virus replication cycle (Chapters 8 and 9) is a potential target. The most successful
drugs inhibit virus-encoded enzymes — polymerases, reverse transcriptase, proteases, integrase and
neuraminidase — which differ from host enzymes.

18.5 Important Groups of Antiviral Drugs
18.5.1 Nucleoside Analogues Against Herpesviruses: Acyclovir as a Model
1. Acyclovir (acycloguanosine) is an analogue of guanosine lacking the 3′-hydroxyl group of the
sugar.
2. In herpesvirus-infected cells it is phosphorylated first by the viral thymidine kinase (TK) —
host kinases do this very inefficiently — so the drug is activated only in infected cells.
3. Host kinases then convert acyclovir monophosphate to acyclovir triphosphate.
4. Acyclovir triphosphate competitively inhibits the viral DNA polymerase (which has much
higher affinity for it than host polymerases) and, when incorporated, acts as an obligate chain
terminator because it lacks a 3′-OH.
5. This double selectivity (activation by viral TK and preferential inhibition of viral polymerase)
gives acyclovir a very high selectivity index.
6. Prodrugs improve oral bioavailability: valacyclovir (of acyclovir) and famciclovir (of
penciclovir).
Chapter 18 | Page 221



VMC 503: General Virology
7. Resistance arises mainly through TK-deficient or TK-altered mutants (which are less virulent
and cannot reactivate efficiently from latency) and less often through polymerase mutations; TK-
independent drugs (cidofovir, foscarnet) remain active.
8. Acyclovir does not eliminate latent virus.
18.5.2 Drugs Against RNA Viruses
• Ribavirin — a guanosine analogue with broad-spectrum activity against many RNA and some
DNA viruses; mechanisms include depletion of GTP (inhibition of IMP dehydrogenase),
inhibition of capping, direct polymerase inhibition and lethal mutagenesis. Used for RSV, Lassa
fever and (with interferon) hepatitis C; toxic (haemolytic anaemia) and teratogenic.
• Favipiravir (T-705) — a pyrazinecarboxamide prodrug converted to a purine nucleotide
analogue that inhibits RdRp and induces lethal mutagenesis; approved in Japan for pandemic
influenza; active in vitro and in animal models against many RNA viruses, including some
veterinary viruses such as FMDV.
• Remdesivir and GS-441524 — adenosine analogues that inhibit coronavirus RdRp by delayed
chain termination; remdesivir is a prodrug, and GS-441524 is its parent nucleoside, orally
available and highly effective in cats with FIP.
• Molnupiravir — a cytidine analogue prodrug (β-D-N⁴-hydroxycytidine) causing error
catastrophe in SARS-CoV-2; also reported effective for FIP.
• Sofosbuvir — uridine nucleotide analogue inhibiting HCV NS5B polymerase.

18.5.4 Antiretroviral Drugs
• NRTIs (zidovudine, lamivudine, tenofovir) are chain terminators of reverse transcription;
NNRTIs bind an allosteric pocket of RT; integrase inhibitors and protease inhibitors complete
the arsenal.
• Combination therapy (three drugs from at least two classes) suppresses replication and prevents
resistance; it controls but does not cure infection, because of latent proviral reservoirs.
• In cats, zidovudine (AZT) has been used for FIV (improves stomatitis and neurological signs, but
causes anaemia); plerixafor (a CXCR4 antagonist) has been studied for FIV.
18.5.5 Protease Inhibitors
• Viral proteases cleave polyproteins into functional proteins; inhibitors mimic the substrate.
Examples: HIV protease inhibitors, HCV NS3/4A inhibitors, nirmatrelvir (SARS-CoV-2 main
protease), and GC376, a 3C-like protease inhibitor developed for FIP in cats.
LECTURE 32: ANTIVIRALS IN VETERINARY PRACTICE, IMMUNOTHERAPY AND
RESISTANCE
18.6 Antiviral Drugs in Veterinary Medicine
Few antiviral drugs are licensed specifically for animals; most veterinary use is extra-label use of
human drugs in companion animals and horses, based on published studies. Species differences in
pharmacokinetics and toxicity are important — e.g. acyclovir and valacyclovir are toxic to cats
(bone marrow suppression, renal and hepatic toxicity) and are not recommended, whereas famciclovir
is safe and effective.

18.6.1 Antivirals in Food Animals and Poultry: a One Health Concern
• Use of amantadine in poultry to control H5N1 and H9N2 avian influenza in parts of Asia in the
early 2000s was associated with widespread adamantane resistance in avian viruses.
• The US FDA prohibited extra-label use of adamantanes and neuraminidase inhibitors in
chickens, turkeys and ducks (2006) to preserve these drugs for human pandemic preparedness.
• Antiviral use in food animals leaves residues in meat, eggs and milk, and resistant animal viruses
(especially zoonotic influenza viruses) could compromise human treatment — a One Health
issue similar to antimicrobial resistance.
• Control of viral diseases of livestock and poultry therefore relies on vaccination, biosecurity,
surveillance and stamping out, not chemotherapy.

requires rigorous evaluation

VMC 503: General Virology
18.8 Antiviral Drug Resistance
Resistance arises by selection of pre-existing mutants in the viral quasispecies (Chapter 10). It is
favoured by high replication rates, high mutation rates, prolonged therapy, sub-optimal drug
concentrations and immunosuppression of the host.
Table 18.6: Mechanisms of antiviral resistance
Mechanism Examples
Loss or alteration of the activating
enzyme Herpesvirus TK mutants resistant to acyclovir, penciclovir
Altered target enzyme Herpesvirus DNA polymerase mutations; HIV RT (M184V, K103N) and
protease mutations; influenza NA H275Y; PA I38T
Altered target protein Influenza M2 S31N (adamantanes)
Increased excision of chain
terminators HIV thymidine analogue mutations (excision of AZT)
Compensatory mutations Restore fitness of resistant viruses, allowing their spread (e.g.
oseltamivir-resistant H1N1 in 2007–2009)
• Prevention: use of combinations of drugs with different targets (HIV, HCV), adequate doses
and duration, drugs with a high genetic barrier, restriction of use in animals, and surveillance of
resistance (e.g. influenza surveillance by WHO collaborating centres and national influenza
centres).
• Susceptibility testing: phenotypic assays — plaque reduction, yield reduction or CPE-
inhibition assays to determine the IC₅₀/EC₅₀; neuraminidase inhibition assays for influenza;
genotypic assays — sequencing to detect known resistance mutations (faster, widely used).
INDIA FOCUS: ANTIVIRAL THERAPY AND RESEARCH IN INDIA
▪ There are no antiviral drugs licensed for food animals or poultry in India; control of livestock viral
diseases relies on vaccination under NADCP and other programmes, biosecurity and, for HPAI,
stamping out.
▪ Companion animals: famciclovir and topical antivirals are used for feline herpesvirus disease, and
feline interferon and supportive therapy for canine parvovirus enteritis, largely as extra-label treatments;
demand for FIP treatment with GS-441524 is growing among Indian cat owners, but products are
mostly obtained from unregulated sources, raising concerns over quality and dose.
▪ Rabies: India was among the first countries to approve a rabies monoclonal antibody (Serum Institute
of India, 2016) for post-exposure prophylaxis, followed by a monoclonal antibody cocktail; these are
alternatives to equine and human rabies immunoglobulin.
▪ Human antivirals of veterinary–public health relevance: oseltamivir is used for H1N1 and other
influenza cases; ICMR–NIV, Pune monitors influenza viruses, including markers of antiviral
resistance; favipiravir received restricted emergency approval for COVID-19 in 2020.
▪ Research: Indian veterinary institutes such as ICAR–NRCE, Hisar have screened repurposed drugs
and small molecules for activity against animal viruses (e.g. PPRV, NDV, BoHV-1, LSDV) in cell
culture, and many studies have examined plant extracts with antiviral activity; most remain at the in
vitro or experimental stage.
▪ One Health: the concerns that led to India’s ban on colistin in food animals (2019) apply equally to
antivirals — they should not be used in food animals, to protect drugs needed for human health.
LINK WITH PRACTICAL CLASSES
▪ Plaque reduction or CPE-inhibition assay to determine the IC₅₀ of an antiviral compound (e.g. acyclovir
against BoHV-1 or FHV-1).
▪ Cytotoxicity assay (MTT) to determine CC₅₀ and calculation of the selectivity index.
Chapter 18 | Page 225



VMC 503: General Virology
▪ Demonstration of neuraminidase inhibition assay.
▪ Sequencing-based detection of resistance mutations (e.g. influenza M2 S31N, NA H275Y) — in silico
exercise.
▪ Case discussions: treatment of feline herpesvirus keratitis, FIP and canine parvovirus enteritis.
18.9 Summary
• Antiviral drugs are limited by the dependence of viruses on host cells, narrow spectrum, late
treatment, latency and resistance.
• Most successful drugs target virus-encoded enzymes: polymerases, reverse transcriptase,
integrase, protease and neuraminidase.
• Acyclovir illustrates selective toxicity — activation by viral TK and chain termination by viral
DNA polymerase.
• Drugs against RNA viruses include ribavirin, favipiravir, remdesivir/GS-441524, molnupiravir
and sofosbuvir; anti-influenza drugs include adamantanes, neuraminidase inhibitors and
baloxavir.
• In veterinary practice, famciclovir (FHV-1), GS-441524 and related drugs (FIP), feline IFN-ω
(CPV, retroviruses) and valacyclovir (EHV-1) are the main agents; antivirals should not be used
in food animals.
• Passive antibodies, monoclonal antibodies, interferons and nucleic acid-based approaches
complement small-molecule drugs.
• Resistance arises by selection of mutants; it is prevented by combination therapy, correct dosing
and restricted use, and detected by phenotypic and genotypic assays.
18.10 Glossary
Table 18.7: Glossary of important terms
Term Meaning
Antiviral chemotherapy Use of chemicals to inhibit virus replication in the host.
Selectivity index CC₅₀ ÷ IC₅₀; measure of the safety margin of an antiviral.
IC₅₀ / EC₅₀ Concentration inhibiting virus replication by 50%.
CC₅₀ Concentration toxic to 50% of cells.
Nucleoside analogue Modified nucleoside that inhibits viral nucleic acid synthesis.
Chain terminator Analogue lacking 3′-OH that stops elongation of nucleic acid.
Prodrug Inactive compound converted to the active drug in the body.
Lethal mutagenesis Increase of mutation rate beyond viability (error catastrophe).
Neuraminidase inhibitor Drug blocking release of influenza virions.
NRTI / NNRTI Nucleoside / non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor.
Protease inhibitor Drug blocking cleavage of viral polyproteins.
Host-directed antiviral Drug targeting a host factor needed by the virus.
Genetic barrier Number of mutations needed for resistance.
Extra-label use Use of a drug in a manner or species not on its label.
Monoclonal antibody Antibody from a single B-cell clone, used for passive protection.

VMC 503: General Virology
About the Author
Dr. Ashok Baindha
Assistant Professor
Post Graduate Institute of Veterinary Education and Research (PGIVER), Jaipur, Rajasthan University of Veterinary and
Animal Sciences (RUVAS), Jaipur
Dr. Ashok Baindha obtained his B.V.Sc. & A.H. from the College of Veterinary and Animal Sciences
(CVAS), Bikaner, in 2009, his M.V.Sc. from the ICAR–National Dairy Research Institute (NDRI),
Karnal, in 2011, and his Ph.D. from RAJUVAS (PGIVER, Jaipur) in 2022. He qualified the UGC-NET
in 2011 and the ICAR-NET in 2012.
He served as a Veterinary Officer in the Animal Husbandry Department, Government of Rajasthan
(2013–2014), and has been serving as an Assistant Professor (2014 onwards) at different units of the
Rajasthan University of Veterinary and Animal Sciences. He is presently posted at the Post Graduate
Institute of Veterinary Education and Research (PGIVER), Jaipur, under the Rajasthan University of
Veterinary and Animal Sciences (RUVAS), Jaipur.
He is engaged in teaching Diploma (A.H.D.P.), undergraduate (B.V.Sc. & A.H.) and postgraduate
(M.V.Sc. and Ph.D.) students, in guiding research of M.V.Sc. and Ph.D. scholars, and in extension
activities for the livestock farmers of Rajasthan through demonstrations and on-campus and off-
campus training programmes.
He has published numerous research papers in national and international journals of repute. He has
received the Best Thesis Award (Ph.D. research) and the Young Scientist Award from different
professional societies of India.

For suggestions and feedback, please write to the author at PGIVER, Jaipur.
